PREFACE
Does the world need another book on VHDL? That’s the question I asked myself when I began  
embarking on the task of writing this book. The short answer is yes, but the reasons behind this answer  
are more complex. Most books on VHDL fall into one of two broad categories. The first category  
provides excellent coverage on the entirety of the VHDL language and makes excellent reference  
manuals but contains too much material for an introductory class on VHDL. The second category  
covers the features of the language that are most commonly used in digital design but does not explain  
how to properly use these features. In both cases, while all of these books do a good job of covering  
the syntax and features of the language, what they don’t cover is how to effectively use VHDL in an  
efficient manner, that is, one that minimizes design time while maintaining a high probability of first-
time design success. This book attempts to fill that void by presenting a formal digital design process  
and  the  best-case  design  practices  that  have  been  developed  over  25+  years  of  VHDL  design  
experience in military ground and satellite communication systems. This real-world experience has  
been  captured  in  this  book,  where  not  only  is  the  what of  VHDL presented  but  also  the  how. 
Throughout  the  text,  recommended  methods  for  performing  digital  design  are  presented,  as  are  
common pitfalls that are encountered during the design process. In each of these cases, techniques are  
presented in order to successfully avoid the pitfalls.
My first exposure to VHDL was at a VHSIC (Very High Speed Integrated Circuit, the “V” in VHDL)  
conference at Johns Hopkins University in the fall of 1986. The stated objective of creating a hardware  
description language (the “HDL” in VHDL) was to simplify the digital design process in a manner  
similar to the advancements of software design through the use of higher-level languages versus that of 
programming  in  assembly.  In  1989,  I  attended  a  weeklong  VHDL training  course,  learning  the  
language syntax and writing and simulating several simple logic circuits. My first digital design done  
entirely in VHDL was in 1994, and I occasionally look back at that design to humble myself when I  
look at the code and see how much I didn’t understand about VHDL modeling and the design process  
at the time. In the 25 years since that first design, the design process has been refined and has become  
much more formal and robust, as the design team I was a part of learned from our mistakes and found  
what worked and what didn’t.
The seeds for this book were first planted in a series of PowerPoint slides that were used to teach  
recently hired engineering graduates in the spring of 2000 and again in the summer of 2006. This grew  
into a set of over 700 slides that was used to teach an introductory VHDL class at Purdue University,  
Fort Wayne, beginning in the fall of 2018. Those slides became the outline that this book follows.
This book is intended to be used as an introduction to the VHDL language, and although it is primarily  
targeted for second- or third-year electrical engineering or computer engineering students, it can be  
used by anyone in the engineering profession who is exploring the use of VHDL as a digital design  
vehicle. There is one assumption that is made about the reader: that he or she has prior experience,  
xiii

--- Page 15 ---
PREFACE
either academically or professionally, in software programming (using a language such as C) along  
with experience in Boolean logic design. We will not describe how a do loop executes in C, nor will  
we discuss the differences in functionality between a Boolean AND and NOR gate—it is assumed that  
the  reader  already  has  this  knowledge.  Several  brief  references  are  made  to  object-oriented  
programming, but knowledge or experience with an OOP language such as C++ or Java is not a  
requirement.
The book can be viewed as containing five main sections:
• Section one (chapters 1 through 4) explains the reason why a concurrent language such as VHDL  
is necessary to model digital electronic circuits, presents the recommended design flow used to  
create digital circuits, and covers the VHDL design environment, that is, the basic foundational  
features of the language.
• Section two (chapters 5 through 10) introduces the reader to the concept of design libraries and  
design units and covers most of the syntax that comprises the actual language. As each syntax  
element is introduced, examples of typical use and a description of the Boolean logic inferred by  
each statement is presented.
• Section three (chapters 11 through 13) describes the method by which VHDL models are verified  
via simulation and discusses how to properly write a test bench to fully test the model. A method  
of developing a simulation test bench via a test bench template is presented.
• Section four (chapters 14 through 17) introduces the reader to the concept of logic synthesis, with  
a special emphasis placed on writing VHDL models that can be readily and successfully converted 
into the actual logic gates that perform the desired function. Code examples for a standard set of  
common macrolevel digital functions are covered, which can be combined together to create  
almost any digital logic circuit.
• The final section (chapters 18 through 21) covers several advanced design topics such as state  
machine design, functional decomposition, and design reuse and provides a detailed narrative  
using the recommended digital design process for the steps taken to design several real-world  
logic circuit examples.
The reader is encouraged to follow the book from the beginning to the end, as each chapter utilizes and  
builds on concepts from previous chapters. Most chapters have review questions that can be used to  
make sure that the reader understands the concepts that have been presented. The review questions are  
brief and generally do not require long, drawn-out answers. It is assumed that in an academic setting  
there will be corresponding lab assignments that will be used for additional reinforcement of the  
subject matter.

ABOUT THE AUTHOR
Bruce  Reidenbach  has  almost  forty  years  of  professional  experience  
designing  custom  digital  integrated  circuits  and  FPGAs  for  military  
ground and satellite communication systems. His early experience used  
the paper and pencil logic diagram design process. He transitioned to  
designing  exclusively  in  VHDL  in  1994.  He  retired  from  full-time  
engineering in 2018 and currently teaches an introductory VHDL class at  
Purdue University Fort Wayne. He continues to perform contract FPGA  
design services, primarily in the area of underwater sensor systems for  
use by  the US  Navy.  He  received  his  bachelor’s  degree  in  electrical  
engineering from Purdue University in 1982, and a master’s in business  
administration from Indiana University Fort Wayne in 1989. In his spare  
time, the author is a long-time volunteer on-air jazz program host on his  
local public radio station.

CHAPTER 1
INTRODUCTION
Digital electronics have become such a way of life over the past 40 years that more often than not we  
don’t even notice it. We expect our cell phones to connect us with social media, make and receive text  
messages, and maybe even make the occasional phone call . We expect our home Wi-Fi to connect us  
to the internet. We expect our car navigation system to take us to our destination in the fastest and  
safest way possible. It is only when these modern conveniences let us down that we take notice. Such  
is the price of success: only being noticed when something doesn’t work to our satisfaction.
The need for faster, smaller, smarter, more complex digital electronic devices was first recognized in  
1965 by Gordon E. Moore, a cofounder of the Intel Corporation. He stated at the time that the number  
of transistors on a silicon chip would double every two years. This is known as Moore’s Law . He was 
actually  fairly  prophetic,  as  the  actual  data  has  shown  that  the  number  of  transistors  doubles  
approximately every 18 months. To  put this in perspective ,  after a  50-year time span, silicon chips  
were over 10 billion times more complex than they were when Moore first stated his law.
Clearly, to keep up with this increase in complexity, the methods used to design these devices have had 
to evolve over time. This book covers a digital electronic design methodology that uses a high -level 
hardware description language (HDL) to create the digital  logic networks. Specifically, this book  
covers the hardware description language called VHDL.
Target Audience
This book is intended to be used as an introduction to the VHDL language for electrical engineering  
and computer engineering students or anyone in the engineering profession who is exploring the use of  
VHDL as a digital design vehicle. There is one assumption that is made about the reader: that he or she  
has prior experience, either academically or professionally, in software programming (using a language 
such as C) along with experience in Boolean logic design. We will not describe how a do loop executes 
in C, nor will we discuss the differences in functionality between a Boolean AND and NOR gate—it is 
assumed  that  the reader already  has this  knowledge.  Several  brief references  are made to  object  
oriented programming, but knowledge or experience with an OOP language such as C++ or Java is not  
a requirement.
1

--- Page 21 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
A Brief History of Digital Design
In order to get a sense for why using an HDL to design digital logic is desirable, it is good to see how  
the methods used for digital design have evolved over time.
Rubylith. In the early days, digital silicon chips were designed by hand-crafting the topology of each  
transistor and creating a set of fabrication masks using a material called rubylith, a red translucent film. 
Each fabrication feature (diffusion, metal interconnect, etc.) was hand -cut on the rubylith sheet to  
expose windows in the film, which was then used to create the photolithography masks that were in  
turn used to fabricate the device. Obviously, this was a very time -consuming task, one that could take  
months if not years to complete and was fraught with error. Each rubylith sheet had to align with each  
of the other rubylith sheets in the set or else the device could fail after fabrication because of errors in  
what is known as mask registration. Failures could also occur due to incorrect transistor sizing, design  
rule errors (fabrication features that are too close to each other or are too narrow), or even logic design  
errors.
The logic devices created using this method were known as full custom devices in that all of the mask  
layers needed to fabricate the device were custom mask layers.
Standard cells  and gate  arrays.  The  first  major  innovation  in  digital  logic  design  was  the  
invention of the standard cell and the gate array silicon wafer. A standard cell library consisted of all  
the masks necessary to fabricate each individual logic gate (AND, OR, DFF, etc.), which could then be  
joined together to make a custom digital logic device. No longer did the designer need to spend time  
sizing transistors and cutting rubylith, as these predefined “standard cells” could be used for each logic  
gate in the design and the only additional work was connecting the inputs and outputs of the cells  
2
A technician makes small-detail corrections of a circuit image on a large rubylith.
Source: U.S. National Archives.


--- Page 22 ---
CHAPTER 1 INTRODUCTION
together using metal traces to form the logic network. The common characteristic of standard cell  
devices is that they contain multiple rows of logic gates separated by areas of metal traces that are used  
for interconnection of the logic cells. Custom design features such as memory arrays and simple analog 
circuitry could also be included in a standard cell device.
These devices are also known as  full custom  devices—even though the logic cells are predefined  
standard library components—because all of the mask layers needed to fabricate the device are still  
custom mask layers for the device.
A gate array, on the other hand, is a silicon die that uses a common base layer of prefabricated  
transistors laid out in a large array. These devices come in different sizes, and wafers can be fabricated  
and  stockpiled  awaiting  final  personalization  via  a  set  of  custom  metal  interconnect  masks.  The 
advantage of gate arrays is that the turnaround time is fast; only the metal mask needs to be created,  
and the only processing step is metalization (all the other mask steps had been previously completed  
when the wafer was made). The disadvantage is that any transistor on the device that is not used is  
wasted. The transistor still exists and consumes area on the die; it is just not connected. Therefore, the  
unit cost (price per device) of gate arrays is more than that of standard cells because the size of the die  
is larger. However, the development cost is much lower mainly because only a few custom masks need  
to be made in order to produce the part.
3
A Standard Cell Digital Logic Chip
Note the horizontal rows of logic gates on the die and the memory array in the upper right corner.


--- Page 23 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
A gate array is referred to as a semicustom design. The base layers of the device are common across all  
devices; the customization only comes from the custom metal interconnection masks used to connect  
the transistors.
Both standard cell and gate array devices are called Application Specific Integrated Circuits  (ASICs). 
Simply put, these devices perform a function that is specific to the application and cannot be used for  
any other design application.
Computer-aided engineering. It is worth noting that all the design techniques discussed so far  
were performed using paper and pencil to draw a logic diagram schematic. The functionality of the  
design was confirmed by simulating a hand-edited netlist, which was a text file that defined the logic  
functions  and  the  interconnections  between  those  functions  (readers  who  have  used  Spice  will  
recognize what is meant by the term netlist).
Several problems existed with this method of design. First, changes to the design, either by errors  
uncovered during simulation and board-level testing or by changes to the functional requirements,  
resulted in erasing a portion of the existing drawing and then redrawing the updated logic, which was a  
tedious and time-consuming task. In addition, errors uncovered during logic simulation could be from  
one of two sources: an error in the actual logic design or an error in translating the design from the  
paper schematic into the logic netlist. A third problem, not mentioned thus far, is that the layout of the  
device was also done by hand, which could easily result in errors that would not be caught until the  
4
A Gate Array Digital Logic Chip
Note the areas without metal traces represent wasted die area.


--- Page 24 ---
CHAPTER 1 INTRODUCTION
devices were fabricated and tested. Thus, it was always imperative that the designer check and recheck  
both the logic netlist and the device layout prior to committing the design to silicon.
In  the  mid-1980s,  a  new  type  of  powerful  graphic-intensive  computer  called  a  workstation  was  
developed. When used for digital logic design, these workstations were loaded with software that  
would help alleviate the problems associated with hand-edited netlists and hand layouts. On the front  
end, a designer would use a schematic capture tool that was used to instantiate logic gates on a virtual  
schematic and draw the interconnections via lines, all performed on the computer screen. Obviously,  
this was much faster than using the traditional paper and pencil approach and had the advantage that  
groups of gates  could be moved,  copied, deleted,  etc.  with a few keystrokes. Integrated  into the  
software was a simulation tool that would automatically generate the netlist from the schematics,  
therefore removing the issues associated with an incorrectly translated logic netlist (now errors in the  
simulation were because of bad logic design, not bad netlists). Finally, the chip vendors had integrated  
place-and-route tools that would create the layouts automatically from the schematics, again removing  
the prospect of a bad design due to an incorrect hand layout.
Although  these  workstations  were  considered  state  of  the  art  and  the  most  powerful  computers  
available outside of mainframe computers and cost nearly $50,000 in 1980 dollars, their performance  
pales in comparison to a midlevel desktop computer that one can buy today for well under $1,000. This 
is yet again evidence of Moore’s Law at work.
The VHSIC program. In the early 1980s, the US Department of Defense observed the explosion of  
the personal  computer  market  and  reasonably  concluded  that  this  was  due  to  the  availability  of  
predesigned chips such as microprocessors, RAMs and ROMs, and peripheral interface adapters. The  
personal computer manufacturers did not need to design and fabricate the digital components; they  
5
A (much younger) version of the author at a Mentor Graphics workstation (ca. 1986).
Note the cardboard light hood.


--- Page 25 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
needed only to design boards that incorporated those components. The benefits were twofold: first, the  
amount of time needed to design a computer was greatly decreased since the chips were already  
designed and tested, and second, the unit price for these components were reduced because of the  
economies of scale that the chip manufacturers enjoyed due to the increased number of chips that were  
sold.
The Department of Defense launched a program to see whether a set of predesigned chips could be  
produced to help in the development of equipment that met the needs of the military, such as secure  
communication,  radar  detection,  and  satellite  applications.  This  program  was  called  VHSIC  
(pronounced  VIH-zic),  which  stands  for  Very  High  Speed  Integrated  Circuits .  Several  defense  
contractors were tasked with creating a set of chips that could be used  by most if not all other  
contractors for their programs. The end result, they hoped, was to replicate the advantages enjoyed by  
the personal computer manufacturers.
Unfortunately, these premade chips suffered from the syndrome of one size fits all in that the chips  
needed to be overly complicated in order to have the best chance at meeting the needs and being  
incorporated into the various defense program products. The resultant integrated circuits were big,  
power hungry, and expensive. From a pure production standpoint the program was not successful, and  
the chips never found the level of inclusion into the contractor’s products that had been hoped for.  
However, during the execution of the program, the realization occurred that the traditional way of  
performing logic design on these complex chips, by connecting logic gates together, was not very  
efficient, and a new method needed to be developed.
The advent of the Hardware Description Language.  One of the tasks of the VHSIC program  
was to raise the level of digital  design abstraction, much like that enjoyed by software developers.  
Instead of writing programs using assembly language, programmers would develop programs using a  
higher-level language such as (at the time) C or Fortran. This is analogous to digital designers using a  
higher-level language to design with instead of using gates and wires.
The  language  that  came  from  this  task  was  called  VHDL,  which  stands  for  VHSIC  Hardware 
Description Language  (this may be the first abbreviation in history to have an embedded acronym  
within its name). Designers could write logic equations and algorithms using this high-level language,  
simulate the behavior of the code, and synthesize it into logic gates using a software tool called logic  
synthesis. The language was purposely made very flexible, covering three major styles of coding:  
behavioral, a style that could be used to quickly develop algorithms and most approximated software  
development techniques; register transfer level  (RTL), a unique subset of the language that could be  
readily synthesized into logic gates; and structural, a form that could be used for netlists.
6

--- Page 26 ---
CHAPTER 1 INTRODUCTION
FPGAs. The  final  innovation  of  digital  design  we  will  cover  is  the  development  of  the  Field 
Programmable Gate Array , commonly referred to as an  FPGA. Instead of creating a custom digital  
chip by fabricating the custom metalization masks used to connect transistors and logic gates together,  
an FPGA is a predefined array  of logic blocks implemented  as lookup tables (LUTs). LUTs  can  
implement any arbitrary n-input Boolean logic function, where n represents the number of inputs of  
the LUT. These logic elements are connected together via programmable switches and wires, thereby  
creating the desired logic network. The real innovation of FPGAs was that they were reprogrammable;  
the digital designer could create a logic design, download it onto the FPGA, and test it. Any changes to  
the logic as a result of testing could be made and a new design could be downloaded to the same chip,  
thereby reprogramming it. The designer did not need to resubmit a design to an ASIC house and wait  
eight to ten weeks for the first chips to be fabricated, packaged, and tested and then remove the old  
chip from the board and solder the new one onto it prior to testing. A new FPGA layout could be  
created in a couple of hours and downloaded to the existing device on the circuit board.
Altera (now owned by Intel) was the first company to create a reprogrammable logic device in 1983  
featuring a quartz window in the package that allowed users to shine an ultraviolet lamp on the die to  
erase  the  device  configuration.  The  first  commercially  viable  fully  reprogrammable  FPGA was  
developed  by  Xilinx  in  1985.  These  devices  were  not  erased;  the  function  of  the  device  was  
programmed via a bitstream that was loaded into it during power-up. Note that there are some FPGAs  
that are onetime programmable or have a limited number of times in which the configuration can be  
changed, but the most popular devices are the ones that use a bitstream on power-up and can be  
reprogrammed virtually an unlimited number of times.
Summary. Each innovation listed above addresses the same problem: time to market. Why? Being the 
first device that solves a particular problem creates an almost monopolistic hold on the market until  
competitors can design competing products. An extreme example of this is the market share that Intel  
has enjoyed with its microprocessor products since the introduction of its first viable microprocessor,  
7
Diagram of a Typical FPGA Layout.
I/O Blocks
Logic Blocks
Interconnect

--- Page 27 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
the 8080. Time to market is driven by the time to create a working design that has been decreased by  
each of these innovations. In addition, these innovations have increased the confidence of first-time  
design success.
From a business standpoint, sales are important because they are equated with profits, and profits  
enable future investments. A healthy company that pursues future investments and products results in  
happy shareholders, and that is the ultimate goal for every company.
The Need for a Hardware Description Language
The first question you may ask yourself is “why do we need yet another design language?” It seems  
that there is another software language introduced almost every month that is touted to be the best and  
final language that meets all current and future needs.
The simple answer to this is that there are major inherent differences between software and hardware  
processing. Traditional software is procedural in nature, which is known as sequential processing. One 
reason for this is how software is executed. There is one element, the central processing unit, that  
performs  all  the fetch/execute  cycles,  and  thus  all  commands  execute  one  instruction  at  a  time.  
Hardware, on the other hand, i s inherently  concurrent. A hardware logic design has many elements  
(i.e., logic gates) that operate independently and concurrently. The function of each logic gate depends 
solely on its inputs; when an input changes, the output potentially changes. In a logic network there  
can be thousands of logic gates that have inputs changing simultaneously, but the output of each gate  
only needs to update based on its own individual inputs. A hardware description language captures the  
concurrent nature of logic, something that a traditional software language does not do.
A second reason for a hardware description language lies in the fact that the function of hardware is  
closely tied to the time delays through the logic gates and interconnection wires and thus requires the  
modeling of these delays in order to be accurate. Software does not have the concept of time built into  
the language; it merely executes each instruction in turn without any knowledge of how long each  
instruction takes to execute. Software tools do exist to compute the amount of time it will take to  
execute a section of code, but this is a postprocessing exercise—the software language itself does not  
have this knowledge.
Based on these two major differences, it should be clear that software languages cannot accurately  
model and describe the concurrency of hardware, thus the need for a hardware description language.
The firewood example.  To illustrate how hardware and software differ in the method in which  
algorithms are implemented, the following example of making firewood is presented.
There are three basic tasks that are used to create firewood:
1. The wood is cut into logs that are approximately 18 ” in length. This is done so that the logs  
will fit in the fireplace, and it also makes them easier to handle.
2. The logs are split. This is done to increase the surface area of the wood and opens the wood  
grain so that it ignites easier.
3. The wood is stacked. This increases the air flow around the split logs so that they dry faster  
and also helps with ignition of the logs when placed in the fireplace.
8

--- Page 28 ---
CHAPTER 1 INTRODUCTION
Let’s look at these operations from the standpoint of how software approaches the solution to the  
problem  at  hand.  Since  all  software  tasks  are  performed  via  the  central  processing  unit,  this  is  
analogous to one person performing all of the tasks needed to create the firewood. The first task that  
our intrepid woodsman needs to perform is to fire up the chainsaw and cut the trunk and branches of  
the trees into logs. Next, when all the logs have been cut they are moved to the splitter, and each log is  
split. Finally, after all the logs have been split they are stacked for drying. Note that each individual  
task is completed before the next task is started: since it is more efficient to cut all the logs as long as  
the chainsaw is running, all of the logs are cut instead of continually shutting down the chainsaw,  
splitting the one log that has just been cut, and then starting the chainsaw and cutting the next log. Just  
as you should have learned when studying proper software design techniques, it is more efficient to  
work on blocks of data (all of the logs) than on individual data samples (a single log).
Next, let’s look at these same operations from the standpoint of how hardware approaches the solution.  
Remember, a logic network is composed of many independent gates operating concurrently, which is  
analogous to having three people working on creating the firewood in our example. The first person  
cuts the wood into logs using a chainsaw. As long as there is more wood to be cut, he continues to cut  
it into logs. The second person, working independently and simultaneously, takes each log as it is cut  
and splits it. Finally, the third person, also working independently and simultaneously, takes each split  
log and stacks it as the split wood appears. Note that this process of having independent processes  
running concurrently is exactly how hardware operates: each task is performed when a new input  
appears (a log is split when the log appears, and the split wood is stacked after each log is split). The  
output is updated each time the input changes.
It should be apparent from this example that the hardware approach is more time-efficient than that of  
the  software  approach:  it  is  more  efficient  to  perform  multiple  tasks  concurrently  rather  than  
performing each of the tasks sequentially. Although the time needed to perform each of the tasks does  
not change,  the overall time needed  to  complete the work  in  its entirety  is reduced  because the  
individual tasks are executed in parallel. Consider what would happen if we added a fourth person to  
our firewood team, one who collects the cut logs and takes them to where the wood splitter is located.  
This additional person could be moving the cut logs while the others are still performing their tasks.  
Hence, the efficiency of the overall job would be increased even further. This concurrent divide-and-
conquer approach to solving a problem is one of the hallmarks of hardware solutions.
A Brief Tour of a VHDL Model
This introductory  chapter concludes with a fully working example of VHDL code that  models a  
standard SN74151 logic device. The SN74151 is a 1-of-8 data selector logic function, where three  
inputs ( A,  B, and  C) select one of eight data inputs and generates the true and complement of the  
selected input. An active low strobe input ( G) disables the logic function and drives both outputs to  
their inactive, or de-asserted, state.
The schematic symbol and truth table for the SN74151 is:

The VHDL model for this logic function is:
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  SN74151 1:8 Data Selector
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  This circuit models an SN74151, a 1 of 8 data selector logic function, where three
--  inputs select one of eight data inputs and generates the true and complement of the
--  selected input. An active low strobe input disables the logic function and drives
--  both outputs to their inactive, or de-asserted, state.
library  IEEE;
use      IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
-----------------
entity SN74151 is
-----------------
  port ( D       : in  std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);  --  Data input
         C, B, A : in  std_logic;                      --  Data selection
         GN      : in  std_logic;                      --  Disable (active low)
         Y, W    : out std_logic );                    --  True/complement selected output
end entity SN74151;
10

G

SN74151

--- Page 30 ---
CHAPTER 1 INTRODUCTION
------------------------------
architecture RTL of SN74151 is
------------------------------
  signal  SEL  : std_logic_vector (2 downto 0);  --  Data selection control
  signal  DATA : std_logic;                      --  Selected input data bit
begin
--  Combine the selection bits into a 3-bit bus which selects the input data bit
  SEL  <= C & B & A;
  DATA <= D (0) when (SEL = "000") else
          D (1) when (SEL = "001") else
          D (2) when (SEL = "010") else
          D (3) when (SEL = "011") else
          D (4) when (SEL = "100") else
          D (5) when (SEL = "101") else
          D (6) when (SEL = "110") else
          D (7);
--  Drive the true/complement outputs based on the data selection and GN
  Y    <=     DATA when (GN = '0') else '0';
  W    <= not DATA when (GN = '0') else '1';
end architecture RTL;
We can break this VHDL model down into several sections, which will be described briefly along with  
cross-references to pages in this book where the functionality for each of the statements in these  
sections is discussed in detail.
The model begins with a comment that describes the intended functionality of the circuit:
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  SN74151 1:8 Data Selector
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  This circuit models an SN74151, a 1 of 8 data selector logic function, where three
--  inputs select one of eight data inputs and generates the true and complement of the
--  selected input. An active low strobe input disables the logic function and drives
--  both outputs to their inactive, or de-asserted, state.
Comments (page 54) begin with a double-dash (“ --”) and extend to the end of the line. It is a good  
idea to create a header comment for each of your models that provides enough detail that someone who 
is  reviewing your code or is  contemplating using the existing  code has  a general  idea what  the  
functionality of the model is.
Next, we declare which external libraries, packages, and elements within each package are used by the  
model:
library  IEEE;
use      IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
This portion of the model is referred to as the  context clause  (page  82), and in general all VHDL  
models require this section. In this case, the library clause makes a library named IEEE visible to the 
model,  and  the  use clause  enables  all of  the  elements  within  the  std_logic_1164 package.  The  
std_logic_1164 package  (page  44)  contains  the  preferred  data  types  that  should  be  used  for  
synthesizable VHDL—VHDL that can be compiled into actual Boolean logic gates. As we will find  
11

--- Page 31 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
out, the standard data types of  boolean (true/false) and  bit ('0'/'1') are insufficient to model  
actual logic gate behavior.
This is followed by the entity declaration (page 69), which describes the external view of the circuit  
that is being modeled:
-----------------
entity SN74151 is
-----------------
  port ( D       : in  std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);  --  Data input
         C, B, A : in  std_logic;                      --  Data selection
         GN      : in  std_logic;                      --  Disable (active low)
         Y, W    : out std_logic );                    --  True/complement selected output
end entity SN74151;
In VHDL, there are two different viewpoints that are required to model a digital circuit. The entity 
declaration is used to describe the external view of a logic function, which in this case consists of the  
pins (called ports in VHDL) for the circuit, their direction, and the data type. For the SN74151, we  
have declared an 8 -bit input port named  D; four single-bit inputs named  A,  B,  C, and  GN; and two  
single bit output ports named Y and W.
The architecture declaration (page 75), which follows the entity statement, is used to describe the  
internal view of a logic function and is the place in the model where the actual functionality of the  
circuit is described. The architecture begins by declaring the internal signals that are used to model the  
circuit:
------------------------------
architecture RTL of SN74151 is
------------------------------
  signal  SEL  : std_logic_vector (2 downto 0);  --  Data selection control
  signal  DATA : std_logic;                      --  Selected input data bit
begin
As is the case in most modern conventional programming languages, the name and data type of all  
objects must be declared before they can be used. In VHDL, all of the internal object declarations must  
be between the  is and  begin keywords in the  architecture statement. We have declared a 3 -bit 
internal bus named SEL that we will use for data selection control, and the actual selected data bit is  
named DATA. As we shall see in chapter 2, signals are used to connect the concurrent statements of  
VHDL together to form a logic network. Simply put, signals can be viewed as the “wires” that connect  
the logic elements together.
After the  begin keyword, the functionality of the circuit is described. First, we combine the three  
selection input bits together to form the 3 -bit SEL bus and then use that bus value to select which data  
input will be used to drive DATA:
12

--- Page 32 ---
CHAPTER 1 INTRODUCTION
--  Combine the selection bits into a 3-bit bus which selects the input data bit
  SEL  <= C & B & A;
  DATA <= D (0) when (SEL = "000") else
          D (1) when (SEL = "001") else
          D (2) when (SEL = "010") else
          D (3) when (SEL = "011") else
          D (4) when (SEL = "100") else
          D (5) when (SEL = "101") else
          D (6) when (SEL = "110") else
          D (7);
SEL is driven by concatenating the inputs together using the & operator (page 38), and DATA is driven 
by a construct known as a conditional signal assignment  statement (page 85). Although the format of  
these statements may be somewhat foreign to the reader at this point, you should be able to see how  
these statements describe the intended input data selection function. Thus, when SEL equals "000", 
bit 0 of the data input bus is used to drive the internal data signal; when it equals "001", bit 1 drives 
the internal data signal, and so forth. Notice that there is a comment prior to this section of code that  
describes the functionality. It is always a good idea to sprinkle your code with comments to inform the  
reader as to the intended functionality of code that follows the comment.
Next, we use the internal selected data signal to drive the two output ports, qualifying the drivers with  
another pair of conditional signal assignment statements that use the GN input:
--  Drive the true/complement outputs based on the data selection and GN
  Y    <=     DATA when (GN = '0') else '0';
  W    <= not DATA when (GN = '0') else '1';
In this case, Y is driven by the internal DATA signal when GN equals '0' and is forced to '0' when 
GN equals '1'. Similarly, W is driven by the Boolean inverse (the not operator) of DATA when GN 
equals  '0' and is forced to  '1' when  GN equals  '1'. Note that these two statements describe the  
equivalent of the following logic diagram:
Finally, we complete the architecture declaration by “closing” the architecture with:
end architecture RTL;
Note in this example that we have adopted the divide-and-conquer philosophy much like that of our  
multimember firewood team. We have created a simple data multiplexing operation using the first two  
statements, the output of which is then fed to the statements that drive the two outputs of our device.  
While it is possible to perform this operation by combining all of the functionality into a single VHDL  
statement for each output, it is always better to write VHDL code using the approach shown above.
This example shows that VHDL is an extremely structured language, one that is also very expressive.  
The statements that provide the definition of the circuit are written in a fashion in which the underlying

logic functions can easily be determined . This is one of the main benefits of VHDL: describing digital  
logic in a way that is both easy to write and read without having to resort to Boolean equation  
reductions and Karnaugh maps to design the logic.

CHAPTER 2
SIGNALS, TIME, AND THE SIMULATION CYCLE
We begin our study of the VHDL language by describing how VHDL models digital logic within the  
confines of concurrency and time. VHDL is a concurrent language because this most closely models  
and describes how digital logic operates in the real world. This chapter describes how VHDL connects  
concurrent statements together via a construct known as a signal, discusses how VHDL describes and  
models time delays in digital logic, and works through an example of how VHDL evaluates a logic  
network via the simulation cycle.  Simulation  is the most important tool used in the development of  
digital logic. Understanding how VHDL models logic networks over time and the relationship between 
simulation timing and the actual hardware is crucial to the successful design of these circuits.
The  author  readily  admits  that  the  subject  matter  of  this  chapter  may  seem  overly  detailed  and 
technical, but it is imperative that the reader understand these foundational concepts for the material to  
make sense in the chapters that follow. Think of it like a construction project: if the foundation of a  
building is incorrect, the building itself will suffer from uneven or nonlevel floors, corners that are not  
true, and settling that causes cracks in the walls. The foundation itself is rather unremarkable—after  
all, it is only a hole in the ground with concrete walls and footers—but a proper foundation is critical to 
the success of a building, and no amount of shims will ever fully compensate for a faulty foundation.  
The same is true in understanding VHDL: all of the features of the language are built upon the  
foundational concepts presented in this chapter. The author’s experience is that the best designers are  
the ones who fully understand these concepts.
Signals
The first VHDL concept we will study is the  signal. Put simply, signals are the construct used to  
connect the concurrent statements of VHDL together to form a logic network. The reader can view  
signals as the wires that connect components together on a schematic as well as the physical traces or  
wires that connect the actual components together on a printed circuit board or breadboard.
One might be tempted to assume that signals are the VHDL equivalent of a variable used in traditional  
software, but because VHDL has an inherent understanding of time, signals contain both a value and  
the history of its value over time. Given that signals contain a value/time relationship, a feature of  
signals in VHDL is that they be accessed in the past (the value history), the present (the current value),  
and the future (value assignments).
Contrast that with variables in traditional software; when a variable is set to a value, the variable is  
updated immediately, and this new value is returned when accessed. Hence, variable assignments are  
15

--- Page 35 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
always updated in the “here and now.” Signal assignments, on the other hand, are always scheduled to  
occur at some point in the future. The reason behind this is twofold. First, from a physical standpoint,  
due to the resistance and capacitance of a wire or board trace, a value change contains some finite  
propagation delay from the source to the destination. In order to accurately model the fact that there is  
this delay, signals, when assigned, always update at some future time. The second reason is one of  
convenience. As we shall see when exploring the simulation cycle, it is much easier to construct a  
simulation algorithm that assumes that all signal values are static at any given time (i.e., the signal  
values do not change instantaneously). The entire logic network can be evaluated using the current  
static signal values, updating any resulting signal value changes at some time in the future. When the  
entire network has been evaluated the simulation time can be advanced, and the process repeats with  
the new static signal values.
Signal assignment examples. To help understand how signals behave in VHDL, we will begin by 
examining the following simple signal assignment statement:
FOO <= '1';
There are several VHDL constructs contained within this statement, which we will first break down  
before we analyze the behavior of the statement. 
• The  symbol  “<=”  is  the  signal  assignment  operator  and  means  “evaluate  the  statement  and 
schedule an update on the target signal.” Note that the symbol purposely looks like a left-facing  
arrow. The author likes to use the shorthand term gets for the signal assignment operator, which  
reinforces the concept that signal assignments are not immediate; rather, the signal “gets” that  
value at some future time.
• Logic values in VHDL use character literals, thus the presence of single quotes around the 1. If the 
assignment had not contained the single quotes, it would have been the integer value 1 instead of a  
logic 1.
• All VHDL statements end with a semicolon (statement terminator).
If we were to simulate this statement, we would find that the signal FOO takes on a logic 1 value at  
time 0 and remains there for the duration of the simulation:
Let’s now turn to a more complex signal assignment statement that includes signal value assignments  
that occur at some future time:
FOO <= '1', '0' after 10 nS, '1' after 20 nS;
The inclusion of the after clause in a signal assignment statement means “schedule a value update  
after a specified time from  now.” In other words, the signal  FOO starts out at a logic 1 at time 0,  
transitions to a logic 0 after 10  nS, and then transitions back to a logic 1 after 20 nS. Note that the  
specified times are absolute and not cumulative; the final transition takes place not at 30 nS but instead 
at exactly 20  nS.
16
0
0 nS 10 nS 20 nS 30 nS
1
FOO

--- Page 36 ---
CHAPTER 2 SIGNALS, TIME, AND THE SIMULATION CYCLE
Simulating this statement confirms this behavior:
So far, we have looked at the behavior of a single signal assignment statement. Because VHDL is  
inherently concurrent, we will normally have many signal assignments that interact with each other via  
the  signal  connections.  Let  us  now  turn  to  an  example  where  we  have  two  signal  assignment  
statements, with the second one dependent upon the state of the first:
FOO <= '1', '0' after 10 nS, '1' after 20 nS;
BAR <= FOO after 5 nS;
The second signal assignment for  BAR has one input,  FOO, and the assignment statement will be  
reevaluated each time a value change is detected on FOO. Literally, the second statement is shorthand  
in VHDL that reads “whenever there is a change in value on FOO, schedule a value update on BAR that 
is 5  nS after the change on  FOO.”  Recall that this is how actual logic works: a logic function will  
update its output if and only if a change occurs on any of its inputs.
Simulating these two statements results in the following behavior:
Note that BAR is always updated to its new value 5  nS after FOO changes state, just as we predicted.  
Thus, at 5  nS,  BAR is assigned a logic 1, then at 15  nS it transitions to a logic 0, and finally it  
transitions back to a logic 1 at 25 nS.
This raises the question of what BAR equals during the first 5  nS of the simulation. The simple answer  
is that we don’t know. BAR takes on an undefined, or unknown, state before the first scheduled update,  
which is indicated on this simulation as a dashed line halfway between the logic 0 and 1 state. In real  
life logic elements always drive either low or high, but as the simulation shows we don’t know which  
state it is in this case, thus the unknown indication.
Given  these  simple  examples,  we  can  already  see  the  power  of  VHDL and  how  it  models  the  
concurrent behavior of logic. The following summarizes the behavior of signal assignment statements:
• Signal assignment statements “ wake up ” whenever any of the inputs to the statement change  
(signal references on the right side of the arrow). In other words, the statements are evaluated each  
and every time an event occurs on any of the statement input signals.
• An update to the signal assignment output (the signal reference on the left side of the arrow) is  
scheduled whenever the statement is evaluated.
17
0
0 nS 10 nS 20 nS 30 nS
1
BAR
0
1
FOO
0
0 nS 10 nS 20 nS 30 nS
1
FOO

--- Page 37 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Because all statements in VHDL are concurrent, a surprising consequence is that the order of the  
statements is unimportant! Consider the following statements:
A <=  B or C;
B <= '0', '1' after 2 uS;
C <= '1', '0' after 1 uS;
versus:
C <= '1', '0' after 1 uS;
B <= '0', '1' after 2 uS;
A <=  B or C;
These three statements will exhibit exactly the same behavior during simulation. The signal assignment 
for  A will be evaluated whenever  B or  C changes state, regardless of where the statement actually  
occurs in the file. Before reading any further, convince yourself that this important aspect of VHDL is  
true.
Events
In the previous discussion, the word event was used to signify that there was a change to a signal value. 
The term event is a formal term in VHDL. Put simply, an event occurs on a signal each and every time  
the value of that signal changes. The event stores two pieces of information: the new value that is being 
assigned to the signal and the time when the assignment is made.
Note that if a signal assignment statement is evaluated and the signal value is not changed, an event  
does not occur. An example of this would be a Boolean AND gate, where both inputs are at a logic 0  
and the output is driving a logic 0. If one of the inputs changes to a logic 1, the  AND function is  
triggered, but the output does not change since the other input is still at a logic 0. In this case, the  
output remains at a logic 0, and there is no event on the output signal.
VHDL simulators perform what is known as event-driven simulation whereby the simulator schedules  
events to occur at a specific time in the future. The simulator repeatedly advances to the next event 
time, scheduling future events based on the signal changes of the current event time, and can skip over  
all of the intermediate times when there are no changes to the logic values. This is the reason why  
events must store both the new value and the time that this value is assigned.
Drivers
Consider a block of logic gates that contain multiple inputs and generates a single output. Viewed from 
an electrical standpoint, the logic continually drives the output of the block to a particular value. In  
VHDL the signal assignment statement continually drives a signal to a particular logic level, just like  
actual hardware does. Conceptually, it is the same as a logic gate driving a wire:
18

--- Page 38 ---
CHAPTER 2 SIGNALS, TIME, AND THE SIMULATION CYCLE
Formally, a signal assignment statement implements a signal driver, which continually forces a signal  
to a particular logic state and is fed these states from the VHDL statements. The “ <=” symbol in the  
signal assignment operator can be viewed as the syntactic element that represents the signal driver. Just 
as wires on a schematic or hardware are used to communicate and connect components together in a  
design, the signals in VHDL are used to communicate and connect statements together.
Every signal has at least one driver, although we will see that it is possible to have multiple drivers on  
a single signal.
Delta Time
We now turn to another example and ask the following question: what would be the expected behavior  
if the after clause was removed from the second statement of our last example?
FOO <= '1', '0' after 10 nS, '1' after 20 nS;
BAR <= FOO;
Understanding how these two logic statements behave requires a deep dive into what is known as the  
simulation cycle and an understanding of the delta cycle and delta time.
Earlier in this chapter, we asserted  that signal assignments in VHDL always occur in the future. In the  
case where a signal assignment does not contain a delay via the after clause, the simulator needs a  
way to  schedule the update after some unspecified finite delay , which  is accomplished through a  
mechanism whereby  the simulator keeps track of two types of time. The first is the physical, or  
temporal, time units that we are familiar with (i.e., nS, uS, mS, etc.). The second is a time unit that is a  
virtual unit called delta time. What is delta time? It is the smallest unit of time that occurs after the  
current time. Delta time is considered to be a virtual time delay that is infinitesimally small but does  
not consume any time in the physical time domain. Don’t confuse delta time with the delays that you  
find on data sheets—delta time is virtual, while delays on data sheets are specified using physical time  
units.
One can view delta time as being similar to the concept of limits in calculus. Recall that the derivative  
of a function is defined as:
limh→0
 f ( x+h)−f ( x)
h
What does  h equal in the limit? Is it 10 -100? 10-1000? We don’t have a definitive answer, although we  
know that it is an infinitesimally small number that is greater than 0. The same is true of delta time: it  
19
B
C
D
Wire
A
VHDL 
Statement
Driver
Signal

--- Page 39 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
is an infinitesimally small time delay that is greater than 0. Because delta time is infinitesimally small, 
there are an infinite number of delta time delays per unit of physical time. This is an important feature  
of delta time, one that is frequently asked during class exams and job interviews.
Why does VHDL need to have the concept of delta time? Recall the discussion of the actual physics of  
the hardware. Signals cannot update immediately due to the resistance and capacitance of the wires and 
transistors that make up the logic gates: there is always some propagation delay on signals. However,  
without knowing the technology of the logic, we cannot pick a reasonable delay using physical time  
units. Using a delta time delay allows the simulator to model the propagation delay inherent in the  
hardware without knowing the actual propagation delay for a specific implementation. The second  
reason is again for convenience. A simulation engine is much easier to write if you adopt a “present  
state/future state” model, which entails the following:
• Evaluate all the statements that have an input value change using the present state signal values.
• Update all the signal values that change by placing the updated values in the appropriate future  
state.
• When all of the relevant statements have been evaluated and the signal updates are done, the  
simulator advances to the next time state, making it the new present state.
• Begin the evaluation of the statements in the first step with the signal values in the new present  
state.
Turning back to our example, the simulation results would look like the following:
It appears from the waveform that BAR updates immediately, contradicting what has been discussed in  
regard to delta delays. However, if we were to zoom in on the first transition at 10  nS, we would see  
the following:
We see that  BAR does indeed update in the future; in this case it updates one  delta cycle  after the  
transition of FOO at 10  nS. This is an important concept that needs to be fully understood: no signal

clause) or one delta time in the future (if no time is specified).
The Simulation Cycle
In order to help understand the interaction between multiple signal assignment statements and the  
concept  of physical  and  delta time  delays,  we  will  perform  the  steps  that  take place during  the  
simulation of a collection of concurrent VHDL statements. The example we will use for this exercise  
consists of the following five statements:
A <= not B after 2 nS;
B <= '0', '1' after 30 nS, '0' after 75 nS;
C <=  A and D;
D <= '0', '1' after 25 nS, '0' after 50 nS;
E <=  B or D;
These concurrent signal assignment statements describe and define the following behavior:
• The statement for signal B contains a waveform that begins with a logic '0', transitions to a logic 
'1' at 30 nS, and transitions back to a logic '0' at 75 nS.
• The statement for signal D contains a waveform that begins with a logic '0', transitions to a logic 
'1' at 25 nS, and transitions back to a logic '0' at 50 nS.
• The statement for signal A is the Boolean inverse of B, with a delay of 2 nS.
• The statement for signal C is the Boolean and of signals A and D.
• The statement for signal E of the Boolean or of signals B and D.
Schematically, these five concurrent statements implement the following logic diagram:
We will now go through each simulation step in detail over the next several pages to show how this  
logic  is  simulated.  At  each  step,  the  signal  assignment  statements  that  are  being  evaluated  are  
highlighted, along with a table that shows the assignment of future signal values as these statements  
are evaluated.

Initially, the simulator starts by placing the signal events 1 specified by the signal definitions for B and

• B is set to a logic '0' at time 0 nS, a logic '1' at time 30 nS, and a logic '0' at time 75 nS.
• D is set to a logic '0' at time 0 nS, a logic '1' at time 25 nS, and a logic '0' at time 50 nS.

The simulator executes a simulation cycle at time 0  nS by noting which signals have an event (B and D 
in this case) and evaluating all of the signal assignments that rely on these two signals. Based on these  
two signal events, the following signal updates are scheduled:
• A is scheduled to change to the inverse of B after a 2 nS delay, resulting in scheduling a logic '1' 
on A after 2  nS. Space for this time in the table is created by moving the future pending signal  
assignments at times greater than 2 nS to the right and inserting an entry at 2 nS.
• C is  assigned  the  logical  and of  A (unknown  at  this  point) and  D (a logic 0),  resulting  in  
scheduling a logic '0' on C after 1 delta cycle. Space for the time entry is created by moving all  
the future pending signal assignments to the right and inserting an entry at time 0 nS + 1 delta.
• E is assigned the logical or of B (a logic '0') and D (a logic '0'), resulting in scheduling a logic 
'0' on  E after 1 delta cycle. The time 0  nS + 1  delta already exists, so no new time entry is  
created.
Now that all the updates to the signal assignments have been made at the current time, the simulation  
time is advanced to the next time in our event table, namely 0  nS + 1 delta. At this time, signals C and 
E each have a new event that must be evaluated; however, because there are no signal assignments that  
have these two signals as inputs, no additional evaluations need to take place, and the simulator can  
advance to the next time in the table.

an important aspect of an event-driven simulation: when there are no more pending signal events to be  
evaluated, the simulation is automatically ended.

that time (recall that delta time does not consume any physical time), and executing the simulation will

The simulation example we worked through is exactly how a logic simulator evaluates a collection of  
signal assignment statements, regardless of the complexity of the design. In our example, the network  
consisted of five logic assignment statements. The sequence we presented on how a logic design is  
evaluated  would  be  the  same  for  a  logic  design  that  contained  hundreds  of  thousands  of  logic  
assignment statements.

CHAPTER 3
THE VHDL DESIGN ENVIRONMENT
We  continue  our  study  of  the  VHDL  language  by  introducing  what  is  known  as  the  design  
environment. The term design environment refers to the basic, foundational features of the language as  
well as the methods used to create the end product (an executable for software or a logic device for  
hardware). For example, in the C language, the design environment includes the base variable types  
(char,  int,  float,  double,  etc.)  along  with  the  functions  that  are  part  of  the  standard  libraries  
(<string.h>, <stdlib.h>, etc.). The C design environment also includes the methods used to construct 
the executable file (i.e., each source file is compiled into an object file), and then all of the object files  
are combined into a single executable by the linker.
As we will see, the design environment for VHDL is  much  more complex than that of C given the  
nature of how concurrent logic is modeled.
Modeling Styles
It was mentioned previously that VHDL was purposely designed to be an  extremely flexible language. 
This was done  in order to  help the logic designer quickly  develop algorithms  through the use of  
different views of how to implement the logic for these algorithms.
There are three broad modeling styles used in VHDL:
• Behavioral. Describing what the function does through the means of a high-level algorithmic  
description of the function. This type of modeling most closely resembles the style of coding used  
by traditional software languages and is the quickest to develop and test. Behavioral code cannot  
be synthesized into logic gates but almost always results in code that simulates faster than the  
other styles.
• Register Transfer Level (RTL).  Describing how to perform the function through the use of a  
synthesizable subset of the language, commonly referred to as RTL. This type of modeling can be  
used to create a logic network of traditional Boolean gates and functions via a software program  
known as logic synthesis. This type of modeling takes more time to complete because the exact  
logic and timing implementation is taken into account via the RTL language subset.
• Structural. Describing the how the function is implemented using traditional Boolean gates and  
the interconnections between these gates. This type of modeling is commonly referred to as a  
netlist. Structural models are almost always constructed by the use of a software program because  
the probability of creating an error-free human-generated netlist is slim.
33

--- Page 53 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
How each of these modeling styles is used in the development of digital logic design is described in the 
following section.
Design Flow
The three modeling styles of VHDL are generally used at different points in the design process. To get  
a better understanding of how these modeling styles are used, let us look at the typical digital design 
flow and which modeling styles are used during each step in this process:
• System requirements. A digital logic design is never created in a vacuum; there is always a set 
of requirements that are created to solve a problem. These requirements usually come from either  
the customer or a marketing group and are refined and enumerated by a systems engineering group 
who are tasked with creating the overall design of the system and breaking down the requirements  
into the processing elements and algorithms used to implement the desired end product (i.e.,  
software and hardware and the interfaces between these elements).
• Functional  decomposition. The  first  task  of  the  digital  designer  is  to  break  down  the  
requirements  of  a  design  into  a  set  of  logic  functions  that  can  be  used  to  implement  the  
requirements. Oftentimes this entails creating functional flow block diagrams and truth tables and  
developing the algorithms in digital logic. Algorithm development is best achieved through the use 
of behavioral VHDL that implements the functional and timing requirements of the algorithm.
• RTL VHDL model. Once the behavioral description of the function has been sufficiently proven,  
the designer then converts this description into an RTL VHDL model that can be synthesized into  
logic gates.
• Test bench. In order to confirm the proper functionality of the RTL model, a simulation of the  
model is developed using a special type of VHDL that can be used to drive the inputs and test the  
output response. This is called a test bench, from the days when a circuit was tested on a lab bench  
using a variety of test equipment such as signal generators, oscilloscopes, and digital multimeters.  
This type of VHDL is often composed using a behavioral model, although certain aspects of RTL  
modeling are oftentimes employed as well.
34
Typical Digital Design Flow.

--- Page 54 ---
CHAPTER 3 THE VHDL DESIGN ENVIRONMENT
• Simulation. The RTL model is simulated using the VHDL test bench to stimulate the inputs and  
verify the outputs. If properly composed, the simulation can also be performed on the algorithm  
behavioral model developed during functional decomposition in order to verify that the algorithm  
meets the requirements set forth by the system requirements.
• Logic synthesis. When the simulation of the RTL model is complete and the circuit is verified  
to be operating correctly, the RTL model is used as the input into a program called logic synthesis,  
which converts the RTL into the actual gate-level implementation of the model. The output of the  
logic synthesis program is oftentimes a structural model describing the logic gates  and  their  
interconnections.
• Timing analysis.  After the logic gates have been synthesized, the actual worst-case delays  
through the logic network can be analyzed to determine the maximum clock rate that the device  
will operate at, along with potential timing violations of the input and output pins on the device.  
Any timing violations require changes to the RTL model and verification via simulation that the  
functionality still meets the system requirements of the device prior to being synthesized again.
• Logic netlist. The logic netlist is the actual gate-level logic network that has been generated by  
the logic synthesis program and represents the final detailed logic design. The netlist is generally  
not meant for human inspection but periodically can be fed back into the simulation to check the  
functionality. Simulating a logic netlist is a rarity, however, and one that takes considerable time to 
complete.
• Lab testing.  The final  step  in the digital design process is programming and  installing the  
completed logic device on the board and testing the functionality in the lab using the target  
hardware.
A consequence of the digital design flow shown in the diagram is that as one moves to the steps that  
are to the right or down from the upper left corner, the time to complete the task increases, and the cost  
to make changes to the design increases. For example, the most costly and time-consuming step is  
during lab testing. Any problems found during this step may involve a large amount of testing to  
capture the error and determine the source. Then, changes will need to be made to the RTL model,  
along with potential changes to the test bench, to make sure the change fixes the problem without  
introducing any unintended regressions, followed by running through the process of synthesizing the  
logic, running timing analysis, and retesting the new device on the board.
Clearly, if problems can be found early in the design process, the changes can be made relatively  
inexpensively without having to step back through the process and restart the steps to get to a working  
device.
Data Types
The term  data type  refers to the classification of an object (a variable or signal)  that defines the  
possible set of values that can be assigned to the object. In the C language,  char,  short,  int, and 
float are all examples of data types.
In working with data types in VHDL, w e start with the rather incongruent statement that VHDL is a  
strongly typed language, yet VHDL does not have any predefined data types. It seems somewhat  
strange for a language to be so strongly typed yet not provide the user any basic types. What VHDL  
35

--- Page 55 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
does provide, however, is a robust set of constructs that can be used to create virtually any data type  
imaginable. There is a reason for this language design decision. By not having any predefined types,  
the language is not stuck with a predefined set of types. For example, if at some future point someone  
comes up with a quaternary (four-value) logic system instead of our familiar binary system, VHDL  
could quickly adapt to that change without having to create a formal revision to the language.
An example of having a predefined set of data types that doesn’t meet today’s needs is that of 24-bit  
audio data used by professional recording studios. In the C language, the built-in data types support  
char (8 bits), short (16 bits), and int (32 bits). There is no native data type that supports 24 bits “out  
of the box.” Because of this, tricks need to be employed in the code to support 24-bit data (e.g., always 
normalizing any data computation to fit into a 24-bit value) . VHDL does not suffer from this problem.  
Without any native data types and a robust method for creating whatever data types are needed, a 24-
bit data type could be created and utilized very quickly.
Type Definition
In VHDL the type keyword is used to define a data type, as the following example demonstrates:
type BYTE is integer range 0 to 255;
This statement creates a data type named  BYTE that can take on any integer value from 0 to 255,  
inclusive. In order for this data type to be useful, any of the operations we wish to perform on this data 
type must be explicitly declared (i.e., addition, subtraction, multiplication, etc.).
While creating a data type and having to define the operators can be tedious, it does vastly increase the  
flexibility of the language. However, there is a shortcut method to declare a data type if it is based on  
another preexisting type. If we use the subtype keyword to define a type, all of the operators of the  
parent type are inherited by this subtype. For example, if we declare BYTE using the subtype command:
subtype BYTE is integer range 0 to 255;
then all of the operators that are defined for the parent type integer are also available for use by the  
subtype BYTE.
The basic types of objects that VHDL supports are:
• Scalar. A scalar type is an arithmetic type that can only take on one value, such as 15, −12, or  
1.235.
• Array. An  array  type contains multiple elements  that can be accessed  via an index into the  
collection of values. Array indices are integer values that are enclosed in parentheses, such as  
ARY (12), which would select the array index number 12 of an array named ARY. Array types can 
be multidimensional, and the index values can take on any integer range, including negative  
indices.
• Bit. A bit type is a logic type that can take on a single value, such as '0', '1', or 'X'. Note that 
bit type values are always enclosed in single quotes.
• Vector. A vector type is a logic type that contains multiple bits, such as "0000" or "01010010". 
Note that vector type values are always enclosed in double quotes.
36

--- Page 56 ---
CHAPTER 3 THE VHDL DESIGN ENVIRONMENT
• Enumerated. An enumerated type can be assigned a value that is defined in a comma-separated  
list of unique identifiers enclosed in parentheses. For example, the following statement defines an  
enumerated type that can be assigned one of four possible values:
type STATES is ( IDLE, START, RUN, DONE );
Vector Data Types
The use of bit and vector data types  in VHDL is quite common, which makes sense considering that  
VHDL is primarily used to describe logic functions. As noted in the previous section, bit values are  
enclosed in single quotes, and a vector data value is enclosed in double quotes. This was done on  
purpose, as it reinforces the idea that a vector is nothing more than a string of bits.
There are several syntax rules that dictate how vectors are created and manipulated. The first rule is  
that vectors can be defined with either ascending or descending indices. The keyword  to is used to  
define a vector that has ascending index values. The keyword downto is used to define a vector that has 
descending  index  values.  The  following  statements  declare  two  vectors  using  both  directions  of  
indices:
signal BYTE  : bit_vector (7 downto 0);
signal SHIFT : bit_vector (1 to 6);
These statements result in declaring two signals that look like the following:
Slices are used to select a contiguous subset of elements from a vector:
MSB  <= BYTE  (7 downto 4);
DATA <= SHIFT (3 to 6);
The first statement drives the 4-bit signal MSB with the upper four bits of BYTE. The second statement 
drives the 4-bit signal DATA with the rightmost four bits of SHIFT. Note that the direction of the slice  
(using either to or downto) must match the direction of the indices in the definition. A frequent mistake 
made by people new to VHDL is attempting to reverse the direction of the bits in a vector by using the  
slice indices in the opposite direction, like:
MSB <= BYTE (4 to 7);
This statement will result in a syntax error because the direction of the slice does not match the  
direction of the signal declaration for BYTE.
Note, however, that the index direction of the assignment target does not have to be the same as the  
slice  direction.  For  example,  even  though  SHIFT has  been  defined  using  ascending  indices,  the  
following statement is perfectly acceptable in VHDL:
SHIFT <= BYTE (5 downto 0);
This statement is the equivalent of the following set of signal assignment statements:
37
BYTE
7 6 5 4 3 2 1 0
SHIFT
1 2 3 4 5 6


--- Page 57 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
SHIFT (1) <= BYTE (5);
SHIFT (2) <= BYTE (4);
      ···
SHIFT (6) <= BYTE (0);
Vector concatenation can be performed by using the & operator. Given the following signal definitions:
signal LSB  : bit_vector (3 downto 0);
signal CHAR : bit_vector (7 downto 0);
signal DATA : bit_vector (1 to 6);
signal DIN  : bit;
the following signal assignments are valid:
BYTE <= "0000" & LSB;
BYTE <= '0' & CHAR (6 downto 0);
DATA <= DIN & SHIFT (1 to 5);
Individual bits (or slices) of a vector can be driven via a signal assignment statement as well. The  
following examples show how this is done:
BYTE (7) <= '0';
BYTE (7 downto 4) <= "0000";
Vectors can also be driven using a form of index selection known as an  aggregate as shown in the  
following statements, which are equivalent:
BYTE <= (7 downto 4 => "0000", 3 downto 0 => LSB);
BYTE <= (3 downto 0 => LSB, others => '0');
These two statements assign the lower 4 bits of BYTE with the value contained in the 4-bit vector LSB 
and set the upper 4 bits of BYTE to a logic '0'. Note the use of the others clause in the aggregate,  
which specifies what to assign any other index value not already mentioned in the aggregate. In the  
second statement indices 3 through 0 are explicitly called out, and the others clause sweeps together  
the remaining indices (7 through 4) and assigns them to '0'. The others clause can also be used to  
refer to all the elements if used by itself. Thus, the following statement will set all the bits in BYTE to 
'0':
BYTE <= (others => '0');
There are several shorthand forms that can be used to create vectors using different number bases.  
Binary is the default case, but octal and hex constants can be created by including a prefix character of  
o or x to the constant (a b prefix can also be used to specify a binary constant, but since the default is a  
binary value, it is never used). The following statement assigns an 8-bit value "10000000" to BYTE:
BYTE <= x"80";
There is a caveat to using a constant that uses an octal or hex prefix. When using an octal constant, the  
returned value is always a constant that is a multiple of three bits. The same is true of a hex constant  
except that it always returns a constant that is a multiple of four bits. Therefore, trying to set a 10-bit  
vector to a hex value using the following statement is illegal and will result in a compilation error:
VEC <= x"080";
because VEC is 10 bits wide, while the specified constant is 12 bits wide.
38

--- Page 58 ---
CHAPTER 3 THE VHDL DESIGN ENVIRONMENT
Boolean operations can be performed on vectors with the provision that the vectors  must be of the  
same size. For example, assuming that signals A, B, C, and D have been defined as vectors with indices  
of (7 downto 0), the following statements are valid:
C <= A and B;
D <= not A;
which is equivalent to the following series of statements:
C (7) <= A (7) and B (7);
C (6) <= A (6) and B (6);
      ···
C (0) <= A (0) and B (0);
D (7) <= not A (7);
D (6) <= not A (6);
      ···
D (0) <= not A (0);
Note that it is not possible to perform Boolean operations on vectors with a single bit. An example of  
this is a bus enable function, which drives a bus with a value when enabled and sets the bus to zeros  
when not enabled, as follows:
B <= A and ENA;
This  will  result  in  a  syntax  error.  However,  as  we  will  see  when  we  discuss  concurrent  signal  
assignment statements on page 86, there is an easy way of performing this action using a conditional  
signal assignment statement.
Operators and Precedence
VHDL contains a set of built-in operators that can be used to perform mathematical and Boolean  
operations on data types. The following tables list the built-in operators in order of precedence from 
the highest order to the lowest order. Within each group of operators with equal precedence, the  
operations are evaluated from left to right. The order of the operations can be modified through the use  
of parentheses.
** exponentiation numeric ** integer, returns numeric
abs absolute value abs numeric, returns numeric
not complement not logical array | boolean, returns same
* multiplication numeric * numeric, returns numeric
/ division numeric / numeric, returns numeric
mod modulo integer mod integer, returns integer
rem remainder integer rem integer, returns integer
+ Unary plus + numeric, returns numeric
– Unary minus – numeric, returns numeric

+ Addition numeric + numeric, returns numeric
– Subtraction numeric – numeric, returns numeric

sll Shift left logical array sll integer, returns array
srl Shift right logical array srl integer, returns array
sla Shift left arithmetic array sla integer, returns array
sra Shift right arithmetic array sra integer, returns array
rol Rotate left array rol integer, returns array
ror Rotate right array ror integer, returns array
= Test for equality expression = expression, returns boolean
/= Test for inequality expression /= expression, returns boolean
< Test for less than expression < expression, returns boolean
