2. What is the main difference between a custom type and a custom subtype?
3. Why should you always use a range constraint for integer types and subtypes?
4. True or false, real data types represent synthesizable floating point logic.
5. True or false, arrays of the predefined scalar types are natively defined in the standard libraries.
6. What does the symbol <> mean in a range definition, and what is it called?
7. Declare a custom real data type named CELSIUS with a range of −100.0 to 200.0. Then, declare a 
signal named WATER that uses this data type and is range constrained to be between 0.0 and 
100.0. What are the allowable operations that can be performed on the signal WATER?
8. Repeat the last question using a subtype definition. What are the allowable operations that can 
now be performed on WATER?
9. Convert the following multidimensional array definition into a set of one dimensional array type 
definitions:
type    POINT3D is array (natural range <>, natural range <>, natural range <>) of real;
signal  POINT : POINT3D (0 to 9, 0 to 19, 0 to 99);
67

--- Page 88 ---
CHAPTER 5
LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
Before we can begin learning the functional syntax of VHDL and how that syntax is used to model  
digital logic systems, there is one more  fundamental item that needs to be addressed. In traditional  
programming languages, most of the executable statements must appear inside functions—they cannot  
exist outside any function declarations. Even more restrictive  are object-oriented languages, where  
different types of statements can only appear inside certain constructs.
The restrictions of statement placement are even stricter with VHDL. This is because VHDL needs to  
describe the logic from several different “viewpoints,” which are known as library units. One can view 
these library units as the  containers that hold  the various syntax elements of the VHDL language. The  
two most common library units are the entity, which describes the external view of a logic function,  
and the architecture, which describes the internal functionality of the logic.
This chapter addresses the use of library units within VHDL and also describes how to enable and use  
the predefined packages from the standard libraries available to the VHDL design environment.
Library Units
In VHDL all of the syntax is contained in library units, which are the top-level building blocks used to  
describe a logic circuit. As we shall see, with the exception of  the  library and  use statements, all  
other VHDL statements can only appear within a library unit.
There are two types of library units in VHDL:  primary and  secondary. The primary unit is used to  
create a unique top-level unit within the library. The secondary unit is used in association with and  
support  of  the primary  unit  and  provides  detailed  information  about  the primary  unit.  Note that  
secondary units cannot exist on their own—they must have a defined primary unit associated with the  
secondary unit.
There are three types of primary units: entities, packages, and configurations. There are two types of  
secondary units: architectures (which support entities) and package bodies (which support packages).  
Each of these units is described in the following sections.
Entity Declaration
In VHDL, different library units are used to describe what the logic function looks like from the  
outside (the  external viewpoint) versus what the function looks like from the inside (the  internal 
69

--- Page 89 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
viewpoint). The entity declaration is a primary library unit that is used to describe the external view  
of a logic function.
The syntax that is used to declare an entity is as follows:
 entity_declaration ::=
entity identifier is
entity_header
entity_declarative_part
[ begin
entity_statement_part ]
end [ entity ] [ identifier ] ;
The entity header consists of two parts: the  ports, which define the I/O for the function, and the  
generics, which are used to pass constants into the function. The entity declarative part contains only  
passive statements, which are statements that do not contain signal assignments. The entity declarative  
part and entity statement parts are rarely used in synthesizable code and, although equally rare, are  
more appropriate for use in behavioral code.
Note how the entity statement is terminated with the syntax:
end [ entity ] [ identifier ] ;
The 1993 revision to the VHDL standard made all of the end statements consistent with one another  
yet backward-compatible with the original 1987 standard. Thus, an entity statement can be terminated  
in any one of the following manners:
end;
end entity;
end identifier;
end entity identifier;
It doesn’t matter which style you choose to use, but make sure that you use that style consistently  
throughout all of your code.
70
The entity describes the external view of a logic function.
ENTITY

--- Page 90 ---
CHAPTER 5 LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
An example of a typical entity declaration is shown below:
---------------
entity ADDER is
---------------
  generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );  --  Number of bits
  port    ( A    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            B    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            CIN  : in  std_logic;    --  Carry in
            SUM  : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            COUT : out std_logic );  --  Carry out
end entity ADDER;
Ports
The port clause defines the input and output signals that are passed into and out of the entity. Inside  
the component each port is treated like a signal, meaning that you do not need to connect the ports to  
internal signals in order to use them.
The syntax for the port clause is:
 port_clause ::=
port ( interface_element { ; interface_element } ) ;
 interface_element ::=
identifier_list : [ mode ] type := static_expression
 mode ::=
( in | out | inout | buffer | linkage ) 
The port clause consists of a semicolon-separated list of interface elements that are commonly referred 
to as the ports on the entity. Each interface element consists of a list of identifiers and the type  
definition for the identifiers. The two features required to describe each port are the port data type and  
the port mode.
The port mode is the direction in which the data information flows through the port with respect to the  
entity. The following list describes the different allowable port modes:
71

--- Page 91 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
• in: Data flows into a port of mode in, and the value can only be read within the component. The  
port name can appear  only on the right side  of a signal assignment. A port of mode  in is the  
default case if no mode is specified, although the common practice is to always specify the port  
mode in, which aids with the readability of the code.
• out:  Data flows  out  of a  port  of mode  out,  and  values  can  only  be  written  to  within  the 
component. It can appear only on the left side of a signal assignment. This means that the value of 
a port of mode out cannot be read within the component.
72
Entity
Z
 
Driver Resides
Inside the Entity
Port Signal Z Cannot Be
Read Within the Entity
Port Signal
Entity
A
Driver Resides
Outside the Entity
Port Signal

--- Page 92 ---
CHAPTER 5 LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
• inout: Data flows both into and out of the port. The value of a bidirectional port can both be read  
and driven within the component and can appear on either side of a signal assignment.
• buffer: Data flows out of the port like a port of mode out, but the value can be both read and  
driven within the entity and can appear on either side of a signal assignment.
• linkage: The data value of a linkage port can be read and updated only by another port of mode  
linkage.
Ports of mode buffer and linkage are almost never used in synthesizable code, so this book will only  
deal with the commonly used ports of mode in, out, and inout.
The usage rules for ports of mode in make sense in that inputs can be read but not driven. The usage 
rules for ports of mode inout make sense as well, since they specify a bidirectional port signal and  
thus can both be read and driven. However, the usage rules for ports of mode out seemingly present a 
challenge. Oftentimes, you will find that a signal that is destined for an output port is also used inside  
the component. The rule for ports of mode out is that they can be driven but not read. Attempting to  
read the value on an output port will result in a compilation error.
How is this overcome? You may be tempted to change the port mode of any output signal that is also  
used internally into a port of mode inout, but this can (and oftentimes will) cause additional problems.  
Most logic synthesis tools treat ports of mode inout as tri-state drivers, a driver that can drive a signal 
low or high or be turned off (the “hi-Z” state).  Most logic famil ies do not support  internal tri-state 
gates; they only support tri-state drivers at the top level of the design hierarchy, in other words, on the  
actual pins of the device. In this case, the synthesis engine will throw an error and halt. Therefore, the  
use of the port mode inout to overcome this problem is discouraged.
The preferred method of dealing with this problem is to create an internal signal that is driven by the  
internal logic of the component that can be read by other statements inside that entity. That internal  
signal is then used to drive the output port using a simple signal assignment statement. Thus, if A and B 
are internal signals and Q is the output port, the following statements will implement this method:
A <= some logic function              --  A is used both internally and externally
B <= some logic function involving A  --  B is used internally and is dependent on A
Q <= A;                               --  Q is the entity output and equals A
73
Entity
 C
Port Signal
Z
Drivers May Reside Both
Inside and Outside the Entity
Port Signal Z Can Be
Read Within the Entity

--- Page 93 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The Q output port will follow the internal signal A, although there will be one delta cycle delay between 
the internal signal and the port. This delta cycle delay is usually not a problem.
Generics
The generic clause allows the user to pass parameters into an entity to modify the resultant behavior  
of the component. A typical use is to write the functional code of a component using a generic bit  
width, which is then set by the generic when the component is used. Generics are treated as constants  
inside components and are always treated as inputs.
The syntax for the generic clause is:
 generic_clause ::=
generic ( interface_element { ; interface_element } ) ;
 interface_element ::=
identifier_list : type := static_expression
Note that the syntax for a generic clause is almost exactly the same as that of a port clause, with two 
exceptions. First, the interface elements do not contain a mode, because generics are always treated as  
inputs. Second, the generic must include a default value assignment (the := clause at the end of the  
element definition).
Why are generics part of the entity declaration? Generics can be used to change the behavior of the  
component and form the basis for design reuse, where a single component can be designed once and  
used in many places. The actual parameters of each component implementation can be changed with a  
simple change to the generic value. Design reuse is an important aspect of VHDL and greatly enhances 
the efficiency in which designs can be created. The philosophy of design reuse is “write once, use  
many.”
At this point, let us go back to the entity example presented earlier and describe what that entity  
definition contains:
---------------
entity ADDER is
---------------
  generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );  --  Number of bits
  port    ( A    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            B    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            CIN  : in  std_logic;    --  Carry in
            SUM  : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            COUT : out std_logic );  --  Carry out
end entity ADDER;
• The entity is named ADDER.
• The entity has a generic named BITS that is used to set the bit width of the component.
• The entity contains three inputs: two bus inputs named A and B (with a width of BITS set by the 
generic) and a single bit-wide input named CIN.
74

--- Page 94 ---
CHAPTER 5 LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
• The entity contains two outputs: an output bus named SUM (with a width of BITS set by the 
generic) and a single bit-wide output named COUT.
• The data type for all of the ports is std_logic or std_logic_vector. In general, it is best to 
have a consistent set of data types that is used for all the entities in your design. This will greatly 
simplify the design process when making connections between these entities. Using the 
std_logic and std_logic_vector types is the preferred data type for ports on an entity.
• Note the use of comments on the BITS, CIN, and COUT elements and the use of “highlight bars” 
around the beginning of the entity statement, which helps locate important sections of the code.
• Note that the entity statement maintains consistent vertical alignment of the elements in the 
statement to aid in readability. This is much easier to read and understand than:
---------------
entity ADDER is
---------------
  generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );  --  Number of bits
  port ( A : in std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
         B : in std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
         CIN : in std_logic;  --  Carry in
         SUM : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
         COUT : out std_logic );  --  Carry out
end entity ADDER;
Architecture Declaration
We have seen how VHDL describes what a logic function looks like from an external viewpoint via the 
entity declaration. We will now look at how VHDL describes a logic function from the internal  
viewpoint  via  the  architecture declaration.  Architectures  always  support  an  entity,  and  the  
entity/architecture pair completely describes the functionality of a logic component from both the  
external and internal viewpoints.
The syntax that is used to declare an architecture is:
 architecture_body ::=
architecture identifier of entity_name is
architecture_declarative_part
begin
architecture_statement_part
end [ architecture ] [ identifier ] ;
75


--- Page 95 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The  architecture body is tied  to the appropriate  entity via the entity_name declaration in the  
architecture statement, and the identifier is used to create a unique name for the architecture being  
defined. Architectures require unique identifier names because more than one architecture can be  
defined for a given entity.
This makes sense, given that VHDL supports multiple styles of modeling. The common identifiers  
used to support the three common modeling styles are:
• BEHAVIOR: A nonsynthesizable behavioral model.
• RTL: A version of the component that can be synthesized into logic gates.
• STRUCTURE: The logic netlist of the component.
The requirement that identifiers must be unique only applies to multiple architectures that support a  
single entity. It is perfectly acceptable to use the same architecture identifier for different entities. For  
example, it is recommended that all of the synthesizable architectures in your design use the “ RTL” 
identifier, which informs the reader that these architectures can be synthesized into logic gates.
76
The architecture describes the internal view of a logic function.
ARCHITECTURE
ENTITY
STRUCTURE
BEHAVIOR

--- Page 96 ---
CHAPTER 5 LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
The architecture declarative part is the place in the architecture where all  of  the declarations are  
located, which are local to the architecture. The architecture declarative part typically contains the  
following declarations:
• type declarations
• subtype declarations
• constant declarations
• signal declarations
• alias declarations
• component declarations
• subprogram declarations
• attribute specifications
• configuration specifications
• use clauses
The architecture statement part contains concurrent statements (and  only concurrent statements). In  
other words, this is where the signal assignment statements are located.
The best way to see how an entity and architecture pair are used to create a logic component is by a 
simple example. The following describes a bus enable function that passes the DIN input to the DOUT 
output when ENA is a logic '1' and forces DOUT to an all '0' condition when ENA is a logic '0':
The VHDL equivalent of this logic circuit is:
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  BUS_ENABLE
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  When ENA = '1', passes DIN through to DOUT
--  When ENA = '0', forces DOUT to 0s
library IEEE;
use     IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
--------------------
entity BUS_ENABLE is
--------------------
  generic ( WIDTH : natural := 8 );
  port    ( ENA   : in  std_logic;
            DIN   : in  std_logic_vector (WIDTH-1 downto 0);
            DOUT  : out std_logic_vector (WIDTH-1 downto 0));
end entity BUS_ENABLE;
---------------------------------
architecture RTL of BUS_ENABLE is
---------------------------------
  constant  ZERO : std_logic_vector (WIDTH-1 downto 0) := (others => '0');
begin
  DOUT <= DIN when (ENA = '1') else ZERO;
end architecture RTL;
The example begins with a file header comment that briefly describes the function being implemented.  
The first two statements, the library and use clauses, make the IEEE library and all the declarations  
77
DOUTENA
DIN
WIDTH
WIDTH

--- Page 97 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
inside the  std_logic_1164 package visible to the library units that follow. These two statements  
form what is known as the context clause and will be covered later in this chapter.
Next the  entity for this logic function is declared, using a  generic to select the bit width of the  
function (WIDTH), and defines the input and output signals for the function via the port clause. There 
are three ports defined for this function: a single-bit input named ENA, a vector input named DIN that is 
WIDTH bits wide, and a vector output named DOUT that is WIDTH bits wide (note that a vector that is  
declared as WIDTH-1 downto 0 is WIDTH bits wide).
Finally,  the  architecture for  this  logic  function  is  declared.  In  the  declarative  portion  of  the  
architecture (between the is and begin keywords), we define a constant named ZERO that is WIDTH 
bits wide and is set so that all the bits of the constant are  '0'. Inside the statement portion of the  
architecture  (between  the  begin and  end architecture keywords)  is  a  single signal  assignment  
statement that encapsulates the functionality of the logic we are declaring, namely that DOUT gets DIN 
when ENA = '1' and is set to an all '0' state when ENA = '0'.
This may seem like a lot of typing to describe a logic function that ends up being nothing more than a  
set of Boolean AND gates, and you would be correct in this assessment. In reality, the signal assignment 
statement for DOUT would only be one statement in a larger logic network that consists of many signal  
assignment statements within the architecture. This example is admittedly much more “fine grained”  
than what you would normally encounter but is provided merely as a simple example of how the  
entity and architecture library units are used in tandem to describe a logic component.
In summary:
• The entity describes the external view of a logic network, that is, the inputs and outputs of the 
logic function.
• The architecture describes the internal view of a logic network, that is, the actual function of the 
logic.
• The entity name in the architecture declaration is used to tie the architecture to a specific 
entity by the same name.
• There can be more than one architecture for any entity, differentiated by a unique identifier 
name for each architecture.
• Each port is treated as a signal inside the architecture(s) associated with the entity.
• Each generic is treated as a constant inside the architecture(s) associated with the entity.
Before reading further, take some time to review these summary items to become familiar with how  
logic functions are created using the entity and architecture declarations.
Package Declaration
We now turn our attention to the second pair of library units that do not actually describe any logic  
functions but instead provide a design environment of data types and subprograms (functions and  
procedures) that can be used in tandem with the entity/architecture pairs to create the logic.
78

--- Page 98 ---
CHAPTER 5 LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
The  package is a  primary library unit in VHDL that is somewhat similar in nature to the  .h files  
(header files) that are used in C. The main difference between a package and a header file is that the  
header  files  are  source  files  that  could  potentially  contain  syntax  errors,  whereas  packages  are  
compiled  library  units  that  do  not  contain  any  syntax  errors  (again,  be  aware  that  successful  
compilation does not imply functional correctness).
A  package contains definitions for data types, constants, and subprogram headers. It is similar in  
nature to the concept of public access in object-oriented programming languages. The syntax used to  
declare a package is:
 package_declaration ::=
package identifier is
package_declarative_part
end [ package ] [ identifier ] ;
The  package cannot exist by itself; it must have a  package body that contains the implementation  
details of the package, which will be presented in the next section. Note that while entities can be  
associated with multiple architectures, only one package body can be declared for a given package.
Package Body Declaration
A package body is a secondary unit that provides the definitions for the constructs that are defined in 
the  package.  This is done to hide the implementation details of the package and is similar to the  
concept of private access in object-orientated programming languages.
The syntax used to declare a package body is:
 package_body ::=
package body package_identifier is
package_body_declarative_part
end [ package body ] [ package_identifier ] ;
The  package body contains the variables and constants that are local to the package and cannot be  
seen or accessed external to the package. It also contains all of the code used to implement the  
subprogram  headers  declared  in  the  package.  Do  not  be  tempted  to  declare  the  code  for  the  
subprograms (the subprogram body) in the package instead of the package body, as this will result in a  
compilation error.
As with the section on entities and architectures, the best way to see how a package and package body  
are used is by a simple example. The following code defines a set of convenience functions that can be  
used for status messages during simulation. The first subprogram is a procedure (more on this later)  
that simply prints a message to the screen. The second subprogram is a function that converts a  
std_logic_vector into a hexadecimal string that can be printed to the screen or to a file.
79

--- Page 99 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
library  IEEE;
use      IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
use      IEEE.numeric_std.all;
use      std.textio.all;
---------------------------
package STRING_UTILITIES is
---------------------------
  procedure PUTS       (MSG : string);
  function  Hex_String (V   : std_logic_vector) return string;
end package STRING_UTILITIES;
--------------------------------
package body STRING_UTILITIES is
--------------------------------
  constant  HEX_CHAR : string (1 to 16) := "0123456789ABCDEF";
  --------------------------------
  procedure PUTS (MSG : string) is
  --------------------------------
    variable  LBFR : line;
  begin
    write     (LBFR,   MSG);
    writeline (OUTPUT, LBFR);
  end PUTS;
  -----------------------------------------------------------
  function Hex_String (V : std_logic_vector) return string is
  -----------------------------------------------------------
    constant  LEN    : natural := (V'length - 1) / 4 + 1;
    alias     SV     : std_logic_vector (V'length downto 1) is V;
    variable  XV     : std_logic_vector (LEN*4  downto 1);
    variable  HEXVEC : std_logic_vector (4      downto 1);
    variable  RESULT : string           (LEN    downto 1);
  begin
--  Copy SV to XV, padded with 0s to make it a multiple of 4 bits
    XV := (others => '0');
    for I in 1 to V'length loop
      XV (I) := SV (I);
    end loop;
--  Convert each 4 bits of XV to a hex character
    for I in 1 to LEN loop
      HEXVEC := XV (4*I downto 4*I-3);
      if (Is_X (HEXVEC))
        then RESULT (I) := 'X';
        else RESULT (I) := HEX_CHAR (To_Integer (unsigned (HEXVEC)) + 1);
      end if;
    end loop;
    return RESULT;
  end Hex_String;
end package body STRING_UTILITIES;
Do not get too concerned over the syntax that was used to create this package at this point; all of the  
programming constructs will be described in the following chapters. The main point to take away is  
that  the  package declares  the  subprogram  headers,  while  the  package  body contains  the  
80

--- Page 100 ---
CHAPTER 5 LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
implementation  of  the  subprograms.  Also  be  aware that  the  HEX_CHAR constant  declared  in  the  
package body is local to the package body and is not visible to the user;  that is , it is  private to the  
package body.
One note on the use of custom packages and package bodies: it is tempting for new VHDL designers to 
create custom packages for many of their entity/architecture pairs as a way of “streamlining” the  
source code. Unfortunately, a consequence of code written in this manner is  that it becomes extremely  
hard to read and understand. Using custom packages in your designs should only be approached with a 
high degree of caution.
Configuration Declaration
The final library unit in VHDL is the configuration, which is a primary library unit that specifies which 
architecture to use for each entity in your design. For example, if you have multiple architectures  
defined for a specific entity, the configuration specifies which architecture is used for that entity. In  
VHDL terminology, this is called binding.
Configuration declarations are a very powerful feature of VHDL; however, configuration declarations  
are rather complicated to write. Therefore, we will not cover how to write configuration declarations  
but will instead cover another way to bind a specific architecture to an entity via a much simpler  
method known as a configuration specification. This will be covered in the section on component  
instantiation in chapter 6.
Design Units
Having now seen how VHDL describes digital logic via the different library units that hold the various 
syntax elements of the language, we now turn out attention to how these units are combined together  
within the set of text files used to describe a logic circuit. At the top of the VHDL syntax rule set is a  
definition for what a VHDL source file contains. This rule states that a design file is made up of one or  
more design units, and each design unit consists of a context clause (which we will cover next) and a  
library unit (an entity, architecture, package, package body, or configuration) that contains the  
actual VHDL source code used to describe the logic network:
 design_file ::=
design_unit { design_unit } 
 design_unit ::=
context_clause library_unit
The examples shown in this chapter (the BUS_ENABLE entity/architecture definition on page 77 and 
the STRING_UTILITIES package on page 80) show how design units are typically written.
While it is permissible to have more than one design unit defined in a given file, the generally accepted  
convention is that each file only contains one design unit, and the name of each file reflects the name  
of unit contained within the file. Thus, the recommended file organization for above examples is:
81

--- Page 101 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
• The BUS_ENABLE entity/architecture pair would be in a file named Bus_Enable.vhd.
• The STRING_UTILITIES package would be in a separate file named String_Utilities.vhd.
A word of caution about creating a file that contains more than one design unit. In most languages,  
including a file or library makes the contents of that file or library visible to the end of the file. Not so  
in VHDL: the context clause only applies to the primary library unit that follows (and any associated  
secondary units), not to the remainder of the file. Therefore, for each primary library unit ( entity, 
package, or configuration) there must be a context clause even if that context clause is identical to  
any previous ones in the file:
Context Clause
The context clause contains references to any external libraries along with a listing of packages and  
elements within each package that are used by each library unit (recall that library is the term used to  
describe where the compiled VHDL is stored and accessed). There are two VHDL statements that  
comprise the context clause: the  library clause and the  use clause. These are the only two VHDL  
statements that can exist outside of a library unit. The context clause is defined below:
 context_clause ::=
{ library_clause | use_clause } 
 library_clause ::=
library logical_name { , logical_name } ;
 use_clause ::=
use selected_name { , selected_name } ;
The library clause makes a library visible to the design unit, and the use clause makes a package  
within the library visible to the design unit. The use clause can specify a specific element within the  
package to make it visible or can use the all keyword to make all of the elements within the package  
visible. The format of the selected name in the use clause is:
library_name . package_name . element_name
where the dot operator is used to separate the parts of the selected name.
The following example shows how context clauses are written:
82
Context Clause
architecture
entity
Context Clause
architecture
entity

--- Page 102 ---
CHAPTER 5 LIBRARIES AND DESIGN UNITS
library IEEE;
use     IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
These two statements are very common in designing logic in VHDL. The first line is used to make the  
IEEE library  visible,  and  the  second  line  makes  all  of  the  elements  defined  within  the  
std_logic_1164 package  visible.  If  you  wish  to  use  the  arithmetic  vector  types  signed and  
unsigned along with the std_logic types from the std_logic_1164 package, the context clause  
would be changed to look like the following:
library IEEE;
use     IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
use     IEEE.numeric_std.all;
Note  that  in  the  standard  distribution  of  the  VHDL  packages,  both  the  std_logic_1164 and  
numeric_std packages are stored in a library named IEEE.
In order to use the input/output capabilities of the textio package, the following context clause needs 
to be added to the statements shown above:
use std.textio.all;
The  std library is one that is always available for use and thus does not need a library clause;  
however, in order to enable the features of the textio package, it needs to be explicitly named in a  
use clause.
While it is possible to select only certain elements within a package, it generally is acceptable to  
include all of the elements using the all keyword. Only those elements that are actually used in the  
library unit that follows will be included in the compiled code stored in the library.
Custom packages can be created and enabled as well. For example, if you have created a package  
named  types that contains a set of useful data types and wish to make the  Integer_Array type 
defined in that package visible, the following context clause item would be added:
use work.types.Integer_Array;
Recall that the work library is the other library that is always available for use and thus does not need a 
library clause as well. However, you still need to use the context clause shown above if you would  
need to include a custom package.
Summary
VHDL files are made of up of  library units that contain the statements that are used to describe a  
digital logic circuit. One way to think of the library units discussed in this chapter is that they are the  
“containers” where the VHDL statements reside: entity/architecture pairs that describe the logic being  
implemented and package/package bodies where the data types and subprograms are defined for the  
designer to use in describing the logic. In order to access existing libraries and packages, each VHDL  
file needs to specify a context clause. These clauses contain references to any external libraries that are 
used by each library unit along with a listing of packages and elements within each package that are  
used by the library unit.
83

--- Page 103 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
