CHAPTER 6
CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
Given the fact that VHDL is used to model digital logic  circuits, which are concurrent in nature , it  
should come as no surprise that VHDL contains a fairly rich set of concurrent statements. These  
statements are used to assign values to signals and can only appear inside the architecture statement  
part  (the  area  between  the  begin and  end keywords)  of  an  architecture  declaration.  In  fact,  all  
statements within the architecture must be concurrent statements.
As was previously described, concurrent statements “ wake up” and are evaluated whenever an event  
occurs on any input signal of the concurrent statement (the input signals are the signal references to the  
right of the <= assignment operator). It was also shown that because the statements are concurrent, the  
actual order that the statements appear in the file is unimportant.
This chapter introduces the syntax used for the concurrent statements in VHDL.
Conditional Signal Assignment Statement
The  first  concurrent  statement  we  will  present is  the  conditional  signal  assignment  statement.  
Conditional  signal  assignments  are  used  to  model  combinatorial  digital  logic  equations,  that  is, 
nonclocked Boolean logic gates. We have already seen this statement in our discussion of signals in  
chapter 2, although it was of a simple form:
 conditional_signal_assignment ::=
target <= [ options ] { waveform when condition else }
waveform ;
 options ::=
( guarded | transport )
 waveform ::=
expression [ after time_expression ]
{ , expression [ after time_expression ] }
The conditional signal assignment statement consists of a possibl y repeated series of waveforms that  
will be assigned based on a set of conditions, followed by a final waveform if none of the conditions  
are met. Note that the final waveform is always required so that a waveform will be assigned if none of 
85

--- Page 105 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
the conditions are met. In our previous discussion on signal assignments, the final waveform was the  
only one specified in the assignment that caused the waveform to be assigned unconditionally.
As a simple example of a conditional signal assignment statement, consider a bus enable function,  
which drives a bus with a value when enabled and sets the bus to zeros when not enabled. Because it is  
not possible to perform Boolean operations on vectors with a single bit, the following is not allowed:
B <= A and ENA;
However, this logic can easily be described using a conditional signal assignment statement:
B <= A when (ENA = '1') else (others => '0');
The underlying logic function can easily be seen by this statement: B is driven by A when ENA = '1'; 
otherwise, it is driven to the all '0' state.
A more complex example of a conditional signal assignment is:
SEL <= '1' when (ADDRL = "00" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "10" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "11" and ENA = '1') else
       '0';
This statement will assign the logic value  '1' to the signal  SEL whenever the  ADDRL vector equals  
"00", "10", or "11" and the ENA signal equals '1'; otherwise, SEL will be set to '0'. The inputs to this 
statement are ADDRL and ENA and this statement will trigger and be reevaluated anytime there is an  
event on either of these signals.
The logic described by this statement should be readily apparent from the syntax of the statement; if  
not, take a moment to reread the description of the function to assure yourself that it matches the logic  
of the statement.
This signal assignment could also be written as follows:
SEL <= ENA and (ADDRL (1) or (not ADDRL (1) and not ADDRL (0)));
However, notice that the functional intent is much easier to understand using  when-else clauses as  
opposed to describing it using only Boolean operators. This is one of the most powerful features of  
VHDL—the ability to describe a logic equation in terms that are easy to digest and understand. In  
addition, writing logic equations using the when-else form will usually result in fewer design errors  
than with Boolean operators since it is easier to understand, and this is another way that designing with  
VHDL increases design efficiency.
The conditional signal assignment statement is the concurrent equivalent of the if-then-else construct 
of traditional programming languages. The statement above is the equivalent of:
if (ADDRL = "00" and ENA = '1') then
  SEL <= '1';
else if (ADDRL = "10" and ENA = '1') then
  SEL <= '1';
else if (ADDRL = "11" and ENA = '1') then
  SEL <= '1';
else
  SEL <= '0';
end if;
86


--- Page 106 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
in a traditional programming language. However, as stated previously, there must always be an else 
clause in a conditional signal assignment statement, whereas in a traditional programming language the 
else clause is optional. The reason behind requiring the  else clause in VHDL is that a sequential  
statement  must  be  memory-less  and  fully  defined  within  the  confines  of  a  single  statement  and 
therefore must always drive a value, regardless of the input conditions.
The selected  signal  assignment  statement  is  extremely  flexible in  its  ability  to  describe  a set  of  
combinatorial digital logic gates. For example, the selected signal assignment statement:
SEL <= '1' when (ADDRL = "00" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "10" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "11" and ENA = '1') else
       '0';
could also be written as:
SEL <= '0' when (ENA   =  '0') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "00") else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "10") else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "11") else
       '0';
or as:
SEL <= ENA when (ADDRL = "00") else
       ENA when (ADDRL = "10") else
       ENA when (ADDRL = "11") else
       '0';
In fact, a close inspection of the behavior of these statements reveals that it could be rewritten as:
SEL <= '0' when (ADDRL = "01" or ENA = '0') else '1';
or even:
SEL <= '0' when (ADDRL = "01") else ENA;
This  demonstrates  the power  of  the selected  signal  assignment  statement:  i n  each  of these code  
examples, the resultant behavior is identical. Ultimately it is up to the designer to choose which form  
to use, based primarily on readability.
Several additional examples of conditional signal assignment statements can be found in the SN74151  
definition on page 10, which are repeated here for convenience:
SEL  <= C & B & A;
DATA <= D (0) when (SEL = "000") else
        D (1) when (SEL = "001") else
        D (2) when (SEL = "010") else
        D (3) when (SEL = "011") else
        D (4) when (SEL = "100") else
        D (5) when (SEL = "101") else
        D (6) when (SEL = "110") else
        D (7);
Y    <=     DATA when (GN = '0') else '0';
W    <= not DATA when (GN = '0') else '1';
87

--- Page 107 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Selected Signal Assignment Statement
The second concurrent statement we will look at is the selected signal assignment statement. Like the  
conditional signal assignment statement, it also is used to model combinatorial digital logic equations.  
However, unlike a conditional signal assignment, which can have conditions based on multiple signal  
values, the selected signal assignment statement uses a single signal value to make the selection:
 selected_signal_assignment ::=
with expression select
target <= [ options ] { waveform when choices , }
waveform when choices ;
 options ::=
( guarded | transport )
An example of a selected signal assignment statement is:
with OPCODE select
  DIN_A <= REG_A when "00",
           REG_B when "01",
           REG_C when "10",
           REG_D when others;
This  statement  will  assign  the  value  contained  in REG_A when  OPCODE equals  "00",  the  value 
contained in  REG_B when  OPCODE equals  "01", the value contained in  REG_C when  OPCODE equals  
"10", and the value contained in REG_D otherwise. The inputs to this statement are OPCODE, REG_A, 
REG_B, REG_C, and REG_D, and this statement will trigger and be reevaluated anytime there is an event  
on any of these signals.
It  should  be evident  that  a  selected  signal  assignment  statement  is  most  often  used  to  model  a  
multiplexer. For example, in chapter 1 we created a model for an SN74151 1 of 8 data selector logic  
function, where a conditional signal assignment statement was used to make the input data selection.  
The code on the left is from that example, while the code on the right describes the same function  
using a selected signal assignment statement:
  DATA <= D (0) when (SEL = "000") else
          D (1) when (SEL = "001") else
          D (2) when (SEL = "010") else
          D (3) when (SEL = "011") else
          D (4) when (SEL = "100") else
          D (5) when (SEL = "101") else
          D (6) when (SEL = "110") else
          D (7);
  with SEL select
    DATA <= D (0) when "000",
            D (1) when "001",
            D (2) when "010",
            D (3) when "011",
            D (4) when "100",
            D (5) when "101",
            D (6) when "110",
            D (7) when others;
If all of the signal references in the conditional clause of a conditional signal assignment statement are  
the same, it is usually a better idea to to use a selected signal assignment statement. In the example  
above, SEL is used for all of the condition tests; therefore, an equivalent conditional signal assignment  
can be used.
88

--- Page 108 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
The choices portion of the selected signal assignment can take on several different forms. The first way 
is by specifying a single constant value like that shown in the example above. Choices can also be  
specified using a list of constants separated by a vertical bar token (“ |”), which will be selected when  
the value of the expression equals any of the specified constants, as follows:
with ADDR select
  DATA <= REG_A when "0000" | "0001" | "0011",
          REG_B when "0100" | "0101",
          REG_C when "1000",
          REG_D when others;
Enumerated types can also be used for the selection criteria. For example, if a signal named SOURCE is 
an enumerated type that can take on one of three enumerator values ( RAM, ROM, USB), the following  
code can be used:
with SOURCE select
  DATA <= RAM_DATA when RAM,
          ROM_DATA when ROM,
          USB_DATA when USB;
Finally, ranges can be used as choices, which are primarily used with integer data types:
with ADDRESS select
  DATA <= REG_A when  0 to  7,
          REG_B when  8 to 11,
          REG_C when 12 to 13,
          REG_D when others;
The selected  signal  assignment  statement  is  the concurrent  equivalent  of the  switch construct  of  
traditional programming languages. The first statement example shown in this section is the equivalent  
of:
switch (OPCODE) {
  case "00":  DIN_A <= REG_A; break;
  case "01":  DIN_A <= REG_B; break;
  case "10":  DIN_A <= REG_C; break;
  default:    DIN_A <= REG_D; break;
}
However, there are some important differences between a selected signal assignment statement and a  
switch statement. The first is that there is no case fall-through like in the C language. This means that  
there is no need for a break statement after each choice, like in C.
The second difference  is that  all of the cases in the selection expression must be covered. This is  
important when dealing with std_logic_1164 data types, which have nine possible states for each  
bit.  A two-bit  std_logic_vector, such as  OPCODE in the example above,  has  81 (9 2) possible  
different values, not 4. Therefore, the when others choice is necessary to cover all of the other values  
not  explicitly  called  out.  This  is  a  common  mistake  that  even  seasoned  VHDL  designers  will  
occasionally make. The when others choice in the first example includes the "11" case plus all of the  
other possible values that  OPCODE could equal. Synthesis engines understand that the intent of the  
designer is to include the "11" state.
Notice in the enumerated type example that all three cases were covered in the selection branches, and  
therefore a when others choice was not needed.
89

--- Page 109 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
We previously mentioned that the typical application for the selected signal assignment statements is  
the data multiplexer, commonly referred to as a mux. While a simple mux (one with a single control  
signal) can be written in VHDL using only a selected signal assignment statement, more  complex 
multiplexers must be written using selected signal assignment statements. For example, the following  
mux selects several different arithmetic or logical functions based on the value of MODE and OPCODE:
DIN <= -A       when (MODE = ARITHMETIC and OPCODE = "01") else
        A + B   when (MODE = ARITHMETIC and OPCODE = "10") else
        A - B   when (MODE = ARITHMETIC and OPCODE = "11") else
        not (A) when (MODE = LOGICAL    and OPCODE = "00") else
        A or  B when (MODE = LOGICAL    and OPCODE = "01") else
        A and B when (MODE = LOGICAL    and OPCODE = "10") else
        A xor B when (MODE = LOGICAL    and OPCODE = "11") else
       (others => '0');
However, this statement could be rewritten using a combination of conditional and selected signal  
assignment statements, like so:
with OPCODE select
  ADATA <= -A              when "01",
            A + B          when "10",
            A - B          when "11",
           (others => '0') when others;
with OPCODE select
  LDATA <=  not (A)        when "00",
            A or  B        when "01",
            A and B        when "10",
            A xor B        when "11",
           (others => '0') when others;
DIN <= ADATA when (MODE = ARITHMETIC) else
       LDATA when (MODE = LOGICAL)    else
       (others => '0');
In either case, the function and the resultant logic will be identical. Ultimately, the choice is up to the  
designer to decide which form seems to more clearly show the intent of the code.
Waveform Specification
In the examples for the conditional and selected signal assignment statements shown above, a single  
constant value was assigned. However, as indicated by the syntax rules, a  waveform specification  
could be used instead. A waveform specification is a compact form that allows multiple events on a  
signal assignment in a single assignment statement:
 waveform ::=
waveform_element { , waveform_element }
 waveform_element ::=
expression [ after time_expression ]
A waveform is a comma separated set of value assignments with optional time delays specified by the  
after clause. We have already seen this construct in the example given in chapter 2:
90

--- Page 110 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
FOO <= '1', '0' after 10 nS, '1' after 20 nS;
For review, t he inclusion of the  after clause in a  waveform specification means “schedule a value  
update after a specified time from now.” In other words, the signal FOO starts out at a logic 1 at time 0, 
transitions to a logic 0 after 10  nS, and then transitions back to a logic 1 after 20 nS. Note that the  
specified times are absolute and not cumulative; the final transition takes place not at 30 nS but rather  
at exactly 20  nS.
The following statement will generate a 10 nS pulse on DETECT every time there is a rising edge on the 
signal TRIGGER:
DETECT <= '1', '0' after 10 nS when (TRIGGER = '1') else '0';
The way this statement works is as follows:  when there is an event on  TRIGGER where the value  
changes to a '1', it is assumed to be a rising edge, and a 10  nS pulse is assigned to DETECT. When 
there is an event on TRIGGER where the value changes to anything but a '1', DETECT is assigned '0' 
after one delta delay. Although this statement will generate a pulse even if TRIGGER transitions from 
an 'X' to a '1', which is technically not a rising edge, unknowns in a logic network are almost always  
a bad thing and are indicative of other problems within the network. We can therefore assume that  
when TRIGGER changes to a '1', it is from the '0' state and is in fact a rising edge.
The  after clause is not synthesizable. This is because you cannot generate an exact delay using  
combinatorial logic. Therefore, the after clause should only be used in behavioral VHDL models.
Delay Models
You will have noticed that there is an  option for signal assignment statements that is specified by the  
transport keyword. To understand how this modifies the behavior of the signal assignment, we need  
to understand how signal assignment delay models are used.
In VHDL, there are two different ways to represent delays for signals in  assignment statements. The  
first type of delay , which is the default, is called inertial delay. In an inertial delay model, any pulse 
that is assigned to a signal that is shorter than the specified delay time will not be assigned. Transport 
delays, on the other hand, will always assign the pulses regardless of pulse width of the input signal.
The best way to understand this is by an example. Given the following signal assignment statements:
A <= '0', '1' after 100 nS,
          '0' after 150 nS,
          '1' after 200 nS,
          '0' after 250 nS,
          '1' after 300 nS,
          '0' after 400 nS,
          '1' after 500 nS,
          '0' after 600 nS;
B <= A after 100 nS;
C <= transport A after 100 nS;
a simulation of these statements would generate the following results:
91

--- Page 111 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Note that while the resultant waveform on C exactly follows the waveform of A, the first two pulses of 
A are not transmitted on B. This is because the pulse width on A (50 nS) in these two cases is smaller  
than the delay on the signal assignment for B (100 nS).
It should be noted that the transport option is not a synthesizable construct and should therefore only  
be used in behavioral VHDL models. Why is transport not synthesizable? The simple answer is that  
the behavior of the transport delay model is based on the time delay specified by an after clause, and 
the after clause is not synthesizable.
Generate Statement
The generate statement is used to create conditional concurrent statements in VHDL. There are two  
forms of the generate statement, one that creates multiple copies of the concurrent statements within  
the generate statement using the  for keyword and a second one that only includes the concurrent  
statements if a condition is met using the if keyword:
 generate_statement ::=
label :
generation_scheme generate
{ concurrent_statement }
end generate [label] ;
 generation_scheme ::=
( for identifier in range | if condition )
generate statements are evaluated and expanded at compile time, which means that the generation  
schemes must be static. The term static means that the condition or range must evaluate to a constant at 
compile time. This makes sense when considering how an actual digital logic circuit is implemented.  
Circuitry cannot be dynamically added or removed; either it is physically implemented in the hardware 
or it isn’t. A consequence of generation schemes having to be static  is that you cannot use signals in a  
generation scheme, only constants and generics. References to signals in the generation scheme will  
result in a compilation error.
Note that the label is not optional; all generate statements must be marked with a label.
When a  for … generate statement is compiled, the contents are  unwrapped.  This means that the  
statements within the generate statement are expanded into individual concurrent statements, with the  
92


--- Page 112 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
index value substituted for each reference of the index in those concurrent statements. Do not make the  
mistake of thinking that the for … generate statement is the same as a loop; the compilation result is  
an expansion into individual concurrent statements. For example, the following generate statement:
G1:  for I in 0 to 3 generate
  DOUT (I) <= DIN (3-I) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (I);
end generate G1;
is expanded and replaced with the four statements shown below:
DOUT (0) <= DIN (3) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (0);
DOUT (1) <= DIN (2) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (1);
DOUT (2) <= DIN (1) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (2);
DOUT (3) <= DIN (0) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (3);
Note that in the case of the for … generate form the iteration variable is automatically created, which  
means that you do not need to explicitly declare the variable, unlike in C. The iteration variable exists  
only within the body of the generate statement and is undefined outside of it.
if … generate statements have their contents conditionally compiled if the condition evaluates to a  
true condition. If the condition evaluates to false, the contents are not compiled.
An example of a generate statement is the following, which implements a full adder circuit using the  
Boolean equations for the sum and carry bits of each column in the adder:
library  IEEE;
use      IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
---------------
entity ADDER is
---------------
  generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );  --  Number of bits
  port    ( A    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            B    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            CIN  : in  std_logic;    --  Carry in
            SUM  : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            COUT : out std_logic );  --  Carry out
end entity ADDER;
--------------------------------
architecture BOOLEAN of ADDER is
--------------------------------
  signal  CARRY : std_logic_vector (BITS downto 0);
begin
  ----------------------------------
  G1:  for I in 0 to BITS-1 generate
  ----------------------------------
         SUM   (I)   <= A (I) xor B (I) xor CARRY (I);
         CARRY (I+1) <= A (I) or  B (I) when (CARRY (I) = '1') else
                        A (I) and B (I);
  end generate G1;
  CARRY (0) <= CIN;           --  Drive the LSB of the carry chain with CIN
  COUT      <= CARRY (BITS);  --  Drive COUT with the carry out of the adder MSB
end architecture BOOLEAN;
93

--- Page 113 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The reader should recognize that the contents of the generate statement create the logic that drives the 
individual bits of the  SUM output  for each column of the adder and  creates the logic that drives the  
CARRY bit for the next column of the adder (hence the I+1 index for CARRY within the contents of the 
generate statement). In order for this to work properly, the internal carry chain signal must be one bit  
wider than the bit width of the adder specified by the BITS generic. The least significant bit of the  
carry chain ( CARRY (0)) is driven with the CIN port, and the most significant bit of the carry chain 
(CARRY (BITS)) is used to drive the COUT port.
The  for … generate statement is much more commonly used in VHDL tha n the  if … generate 
statement. Oftentimes, it is easier to use intermediate signals than to use the if … generate statement. 
For example, instead of the following:
G1:  if COND1 generate
  SIG <= ...
end generate G1;
G2:  if COND2 generate
  SIG <= ...
end generate G2;
G3:  if COND3 generate
  SIG <= ...
end generate G3;
do this instead:
SIG1 <= ...
SIG2 <= ...
SIG3 <= ...
SIG  <= SIG1 when (COND1) else
        SIG2 when (COND2) else
        SIG3;
Concurrent Assertion Statement
Assertions are statements used in logic simulation that check  circuit performance parameters and  
signal values and produce user messages if the condition in the assertion statement is not met. The  
assertion  will  trigger  and  will  generate  a  message  whenever  the  condition  evaluates  to  false.  A  
concurrent assertion statement is a statement that continually performs these checks:
 concurrent_assertion_statement ::=
assert condition
[ report expression ]
[ severity expression ] ;
An example of a concurrent assertion statement is:
assert (CLOCK'last_event >= 50 nS)
  report "Pulse width violation on CLOCK"
  severity error;
94

--- Page 114 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
This statement continually checks that the last event on the CLOCK signal is at least 50  nS prior to the  
current event on CLOCK. Every time there is an event on the CLOCK signal, the last_event attribute 
will automatically update with the amount of time that has elapsed since the previous event. This  
update will trigger the assertion statement, which then checks that at least 50  nS of time has elapsed. If 
this is not the case, then the assertion statement generates the message in the report clause with a  
severity of error. If the elapsed time meets the condition, the assertion statement is silent.
The  severity  level  is  predefined  in  the  standard package  and  can  be  one  of  the  following,  in  
increasing level of severity: note, warning, error, and failure. An assertion that triggers with a  
severity level of  failure will  normally  halt the simulation. All other  assertion  severity levels are  
reported, but the simulation continues.
Assertion statements are behavioral in nature and as such should only appear in behavioral VHDL  
models and should not be used in synthesizable code. Many VHDL tools are smart enough to ignore  
assertion statements when synthesizing the code into a logic network, but they should be avoided in  
synthesizable code for maximum code comparability across tool sets.
Component Instantiation
Most designs are very  complex,  and  trying to create  a design using a “flat” design technique is  
virtually impossible. It is easier to create  a design by  breaking it down into  smaller, more easily  
designed  components  that  perform  specific functions.  These  subcomponents  are  then  connected  
together to create the larger design. This is known as creating a hierarchical design, which conceptually 
is shown below:
The term hierarchy refers to creating a design made of subcircuits that in turn may also be composed  
of other subcircuits, which in turn may be made of further subcircuits, etc. VHDL supports hierarchical 
design through the process of instantiating components inside architectures. Component instantiation 
can be thought of as the VHDL equivalent of soldering parts to a board.
One may be tempted to equate component instantiation with calls to a function or subroutine in a  
traditional programming language. Philosophically, this is true  from the standpoint  that  hierarchical 
components define and encapsulate functionality,  which is then included in the  circuitry where the  
component is instantiated. This is similar to calling a subroutine to perform a certain function in  
software, where the actions of the subroutine are included as part of the execution of the code when the 
subroutine is called.
95
E = Entity/Architecture Pair
E
E E E
E
E E

--- Page 115 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
However, there is a fundamental distinction  between component instantiation and a software function  
call. A software function, when compiled, occupies a specific range of addresses in memory, and the  
instructions in that memory range are executed each time the function is called. In other words, there is  
only one copy of the function in the executable image that handles all of the calls to the function. This  
is true even in object-oriented languages, where the methods occupy a single memory range regardless  
of the number of objects created. It is the data storage portion of the object that is distinct for each  
object.
Instantiation, on the other hand, creates a  separate copy of the hardware component each time it is  
instantiated. With component instantiation there is no “sharing” of resources, which again points to the  
differences between sequential software implementations and concurrent hardware implementations. A  
consequence of this distinction is that while software functions can be called recursively, a hardware  
component cannot. A component, if it were to instantiate a copy of itself internally, would create a  
circuit of infinite size.
There are three steps that must be performed  in order to create a hierarchical design in VHDL: defin e 
the component through the use of a component declaration, specify which architecture of a component 
to  use  through  a  configuration  specification,  and  connect  the  component  to  the  signals  in  the  
architecture through a component instantiation statement.
Component Declaration
The  component  declaration provides  a definition  of what  the interface  of the component  is. The  
component  declaration  is  made within  the  declarative  part  of  the  architecture  (before  the  begin 
keyword):
 component_declaration ::=
component identifier [ is ]
[ generic ( interface_list ) ; ]
[ port ( interface_list ) ; ]
end component [ identifier ] ;
Notice that the component declaration looks almost identical to the entity statement with the exception  
that instead of an entity keyword, the keyword component is substituted. In fact, the easiest way to  
compose a component declaration is to copy and paste the entity definition and make that change. For 
example, if an entity is defined as:
---------------
entity ADDER is
---------------
  generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );  --  Number of bits
  port    ( A, B : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            CIN  : in  std_logic;    --  Carry in
            SUM  : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            COUT : out std_logic );  --  Carry out
end entity ADDER;
the component declaration for this entity is:
96

--- Page 116 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
component ADDER is
  generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );  --  Number of bits
  port    ( A, B : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            CIN  : in  std_logic;    --  Carry in
            SUM  : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
            COUT : out std_logic );  --  Carry out
end component ADDER;
Each component that is instantiated in an architecture requires a component declaration, so oftentimes  
there will be several component declarations in the architecture declarative part, one for each unique  
component being instantiated.
Why do we need to create a component declaration? As previously mentioned, component instantiation 
is in some ways the VHDL equivalent of calling a subroutine in a traditional programming language.  
In order to call a subroutine properly, the compiler needs to have the interface information for that  
subroutine (the number of parameters, their types, and the return type). The same is true of VHDL: in  
order for the compiler to instantiate a component, it also needs to know the interface definition for that  
component. In this case, the interface elements are the generics (the number and their types) and the  
ports (the number, direction, and types). Therefore, one can view a component declaration as being the  
equivalent of a function prototype in software.
Configuration Specification
Once a component is declared, a method is needed to select which architecture will be used by each  
component instantiation. Configuration specifications are single line specifications that are included in  
the declaration part of an architecture to select the specific architecture for each component:
 configuration_specification ::=
for instantiation_list : component_name
use entity library . entity_name ( architecture_identifier ) ;
 instantiation_list ::=
( instantiation_label { , instantiation_label } | others | all )
For example, if you have declared an entity named  ADDER with two architectures named  RTL and  
BEHAVIOR, the following configuration specification would select the RTL architecture for all of the  
component instantiations in the architecture:
for all : ADDER use entity work.ADDER (RTL);
Furthermore, you could select the  BEHAVIOR architecture for one of the adder components named  
ADD1 and the RTL architecture for the remaining ones using the others keyword, as follows:
for ADD1   : ADDER use entity work.ADDER (BEHAVIOR);
for others : ADDER use entity work.ADDER (RTL);
Configuration specifications are not needed if there is only one architecture defined for a given entity. 
Furthermore, configuration specifications for entities that do have more than one architecture will use  
the default configuration if a configuration specification is not made. The rule in the VHDL standard  
for this case states that the last architecture compiled will be the one used; however, it is entirely  
possible that the last architecture compiled is not the intended one, which can lead to problems where  
97

--- Page 117 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
the circuit doesn’t function as intended. Bugs due to the use of default configurations are very difficult  
to track down, so to be safe always use a configuration specification for entities with more than one  
architecture definition.
Component Instantiation Statement
The final step that is performed in creating a hierarchical design is instantiating the components and  
connecting the signals in the architecture to the ports of the component via the component instantiation
statement,  a  process  known  as  mapping.  Component  instantiation  statements  are  included  in  the 
statement part of an architecture, where all of the other concurrent statements are located:
 component_instantiation_statement ::=
label :
[ component ] name
[ generic map ( association_list ) ]
[ port map ( association_list ) ] ;
 association_list ::=
[ formal_part => ] actual_part
{ , [ formal_part => ] actual_part }
We made the statement that the component instantiation process  can be thought of as  the VHDL  
equivalent of soldering parts to a board. The component instantiation statement is where this virtual  
soldering takes place. When a hierarchical circuit is compiled, each instantiated component is replaced  
with the statements contained within the architecture for that component. The port references in the  
architecture are replaced with the signals that are mapped to  each of the  ports. This is an important  
feature: if the internal ports were driven with signal assignments to the mapped signals, there would be  
a 1 delta cycle delay for that assignment. By replacing the ports with the signals, the delta cycle delay 
is avoided.
Note that just as was the case with the generate statement, the label is not optional. All component 
instantiation statements must be marked with a label.
Ports and generics are mapped using an association list. The list is a comma -separated set of values  
that are connected  to  each  port  and  generic.  There are two forms  that can  be used.  The first  is  
positional association, where each signal is connected to the ports and generics in the order they were  
declared.  The  second  is  formal  named association,  where  the  ports  and  generics  are  explicitly  
referenced and the order in which they appear is not important.
An  example  where  a  16-bit  adder  is  instantiated  using  our  ADDER component  using  positional  
association is:
------------
UADD:  ADDER
------------
  generic map ( 16 )
  port map    ( DA, DB, CIN, SUM, COUT );
98

--- Page 118 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
The same instantiation using formal named association is:
------------
UADD:  ADDER
------------
  generic map ( BITS => 16 ) 
  port map    ( A    => DA,     -- in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                B    => DB,     -- in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                CIN  => CIN,    -- in  std_logic;
                SUM  => SUM,    -- out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                COUT => COUT ); -- out std_logic;
Clearly  the  formal  named  association  form  provides much  more  information  tha n the  positional  
association one. The formal named association form is the preferred method, even though it requires  
more  typing,  because  it  greatly  aids  in  the  understanding  of  how  the  instantiated  component  is  
connected and is much easier to debug when problems occur (and they will).
There are several things to note about the component instantiation example:
• The “name” of the component is the instantiation label (UADD). A short but meaningful label is 
preferred over a long one because the label is used like a directory name in a file system to locate 
each signal in the design; that is, if this instantiation is 4 levels deep in a design hierarchy, the SUM 
port would be referenced as /TOP/UDP/URX/UFIR/UADD/SUM.
• “Highlight bars” are used around the label and component name, which helps locate where the 
instantiations are in the code.
• The format of both the generic and port map clauses are generic => value and 
port => signal. A right arrow is used to reinforce that no delta delays are consumed in 
associating the signals with the ports.
• There is no semicolon after the generic map clause (even though there is a semicolon after the 
generic clause of an entity declaration). This is another error that even seasoned VHDL designers 
occasionally make.
• Only one generic or port mapping is made per line. This helps in two ways. First, it is easier to see 
each of the mappings, and second, it allows for a comment with each mapping that lists the 
direction and data type of the port. This helps immensely when debugging or reviewing the code.
• Maintain vertical alignment!
An issue that comes up occasionally is how to handle ports that are unused in an instantiation. If the  
port is of mode in, the port must be driven, usually to its disabled value. If the port is of mode out or 
inout, the port association can be excluded from the mapping or be designated as an open circuit by  
using the keyword open. The preferred way to designate an open circuit output is by using the open 
keyword; it indicates that the designer knows that the output exists on the component and that the  
output was intentionally left open. For example, if we did not need to use the carry input and output of  
our adder, the following component instantiation would accomplish this:
99

--- Page 119 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
------------
UADD:  ADDER
------------
  generic map ( BITS => 16 ) 
  port map    ( A    => DA,     -- in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                B    => DB,     -- in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                CIN  => '0',    -- in  std_logic;
                SUM  => SUM,    -- out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                COUT => open ); -- out std_logic;
Generics, on the other hand, do not need to be mapped  to a value in a component instantiation  
statement. In cases where a generic is not mapped, the default value, specified in the generic clause of 
the entity declaration statement, will be used. It’s a good idea to always map values to all generics in  
an instantiation statement, however, for two reasons. First, it’s easier for a reviewer to see what the  
value is, and second, it ensures that the correct value is used if the default were to be changed in the  
original entity declaration.
Direct Entity Instantiation
At this point, you may be thinking to yourself that this is an awful lot of typing in order to create a  
hierarchical design. When the author first complained about this, the answer was, in essence, “Tough,  
get used to it.” Actually, with modern text editors such as notepad++ (a powerful and free text editor),  
the process of instantiating a component is much easier than it appears.
There is,  however,  another way  of instantiating  a component  that  does  not require a component  
declaration or a configuration specification; instead it uses a feature called direct entity instantiation 
which was made available in the 1993 revision of the VHDL standard:
 direct_entity_instantiation_statement ::=
label :
entity name [ ( architecture_identifier ) ]
[ generic map ( association_list ) ]
[ port map ( association_list ) ] ;
The difference between  the standard component  instantiation and direct entity instantiation is the  
inclusion of the  entity keyword before the entity name and (optionally) the architecture identifier  
inside parentheses after the entity name.
Thus,  to  instantiate  our  adder  (with  the  architecture  identifier  RTL)  using  direct  component  
instantiation, the following statement would be used:
------------------------
UADD:  entity ADDER(RTL)
------------------------
  generic map ( BITS => 16 )
  port map    ( A    => DA,     -- in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                B    => DB,     -- in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                CIN  => CIN,    -- in  std_logic;
                SUM  => SUM,    -- out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                COUT => COUT ); -- out std_logic;
100

--- Page 120 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
The manner in which component instantiation is accomplished is up to the designer unless there is a  
coding standard that dictates which method is to be used. In either case, consistency is key. Use only  
one method for all of the component instantiations in your design.
A complete example of all three steps used to perform a component instantiation is shown in the  
following code:
------------------------------
architecture RTL of CIRCUIT is
------------------------------
  component ADDER is
    generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );  --  Number of bits
    port    ( A, B : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
              CIN  : in  std_logic;    --  Carry in
              SUM  : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
              COUT : out std_logic );  --  Carry out
  end component ADDER;
  for all : ADDER use entity work.ADDER (RTL);
  constant  BITS       : natural := 16;
  signal    A, B, QOUT : std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
  signal    COUT       : std_logic;
begin
  ...
  ------------
  UADD:  ADDER
  ------------
    generic map ( BITS => BITS ) 
    port map    ( A    => A,       --  in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                  B    => B,       --  in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                  CIN  => '0',     --  in  std_logic;
                  SUM  => QOUT,    --  out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
                  COUT => COUT );  --  out std_logic;
  ...
end architecture RTL;
Block Statement
Blocks primarily provide a method to partition and organize statements within an architecture. Blocks  
also provide a method to create guarded expressions and signal assignments, but this is an advanced  
topic—one rarely seen in actual code—that will not be covered in this book:
 block_statement ::=
label :
block [ ( guard_expression ) ] [ is ]
block_header
block_declarative_part
begin
block_statement_part
end block [ label ] ;
101

--- Page 121 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The  block statement consists of a declarative part that defines signals and constants that are only  
visible within the block. The block statement part is composed of a set of concurrent statements that  
has access to any signals or constants declared both inside and outside the block.
An example of an architecture that contains block statements is shown below:
-------------------------------
architecture RTL of COM_PORT is
-------------------------------
  --  Signal and constant declarations visible to the architecture and both blocks
begin
  --  Signal assignments common to both blocks
  ----------
  RX:  block
  ----------
    --  Signal and constant declarations visible only to block RX
  begin
    --  Signal assignments for the RX function
  end block RX;
  ----------
  TX:  block
  ----------
    --  Signal and constant declarations visible only to block TX
  begin
    --  Signal assignments for the TX function
  end block TX;
end architecture RTL;
The example shows an outline of a full-duplex serial communication interface (such as a serial RS-232  
comm  port)  that  is  broken  into  three  sections:  common  functions,  a  receiver  (block  RX),  and  a 
transmitter  (block  TX).  The  first  section,  inside  the  architecture  but  outside  the  blocks,  contains 
declarations and signal assignments that can be considered “global” to this architecture and will be  
visible both within and outside the blocks. The second section, contained within a block named  RX, 
specifies declarations that can be considered “local” to the RX block and are only visible within the  
block. The signal assignments can be made to both the local and global signals from inside this block.  
The third section is contained within a block named TX. As with the RX block, the TX block specifies 
declarations  that  can  be  considered  “local”  and  are  only  visible  within  the  block.  The  signal  
assignments can be made to both the local and global signals from inside this block.
Blocks are best used in organizing the concurrent statements of an architecture into sections for better  
readability. It should be noted that any code organization that can done via blocks can be done via  
component instantiation as well.
Process Statement
The last concurrent statement we will cover is the process statement. The process statement provides a 
link between the concurrent nature of VHDL and the sequential world of traditional software. Process 
statements contain sequential statements and variables, which cannot  be otherwise used inside an  
architecture. Processes can be written in two different ways: either with or without a sensitivity list, 
102

--- Page 122 ---
CHAPTER 6 CONCURRENT STATEMENTS
which is a list of signals that will trigger the process to be evaluated when an event occurs on any of  
those signals:
 process_statement ::=
[ label : ]
process [ ( sensitivity_list ) ] [ is ]
process_declarative_part
begin
process_statement_part
end process [ label ] ;
Processes with sensitivity lists are triggered by an event on any signal listed in the sensitivity list. The  
statements in the process are evaluated until the end of the process is reached, at which point the  
process  evaluation  is  suspended  until  another  event  on  any  of  the  signals  in  the sensitivity  list.  
Processes without sensitivity lists are triggered whenever a  wait statement is satisfied and will be  
evaluated until another  wait statement is encountered. When the end of the process is reached, it  
automatically restarts evaluation at the beginning without suspension. Processes cannot contain both  
sensitivity lists and wait statements—it must be one or the other. Attempting to compile a process with 
both will result in a compilation error.
Processes  with  wait statements  are  typically  used  in  behavioral  modeling,  while  processes  with  
sensitivity lists are typically used in both behavioral and RTL models. As we shall see in chapter 8, 
processes with sensitivity lists are the preferred method to create flip-flops in VHDL.
Processes without either a sensitivity list or any wait statements are infinite loops. An infinite loop will 
cause a simulation to “hang” because the process never suspends. While this is undesirable, it is not  
considered to be a syntax error and will not cause an error when the process is compiled. If you run a  
simulation and the time never advances beyond 0, it is because there is a process in the design without  
either a sensitivity list or a wait statement that is continually running without suspension.
Examples of process statements are shown below: 
process (SIG_A) begin
  ...
  --  sequential statements
  ...
end process;
process begin
  ...
  --  sequential statements
  ...
  wait on SIG_A;
end process;
process begin
 ...
 --  sequential statements
 ...
end process;
Infinite Loop!
process begin
  ...
  --  sequential statements
  ...
  wait on SIG_A;
  ...
  --  sequential statements
  ...
  wait on SIG_B;
end process;
103
Equivalent


--- Page 123 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The reason why the top two process statements are equivalent has to do with the fact that during  
initialization, all processes are evaluated one time. Thus, the process on the right will run once (during  
initialization), then wait on  SIG_A. This is the exact same behavior  that will be exhibited by  the  
process with SIG_A in the sensitivity list. Although a process with a sensitivity list may look like it  
repeatedly performs a  wait-then-execute cycle,  in reality it performs  a repeated  execute-then-wait 
cycle. This will be discussed further when we present the concepts of simulation phases in chapter 11.
Note that there is an equivalence between concurrent signal assignment statements and the process  
statement. Given a concurrent signal assignment of:
SEL <= '1' when (ADDRL = "00" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "10" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "11" and ENA = '1') else
       '0';
an equivalent statement could be written using a process statement:
process (ADDRL, ENA) begin
  if (ADDRL = "00" and ENA = '1') then
    SEL <= '1';
  elsif (ADDRL = "10" and ENA = '1') then
    SEL <= '1';
  elsif (ADDRL = "11" and ENA = '1') then
    SEL <= '1';
  else
    SEL <= '0';
  end if;
end process;
As already discussed, any event on a signal reference on the right side of the assignment statement will  
cause the statement to “ wake up ” and be evaluated. By placing references to the same signals in a  
sensitivity list,  this instructs the process to wake up on events on those signals and  evaluate the  
statements in the process. Although these two statements are equivalent, the use of a process statement 
to describe combinatorial logic is discouraged. It is easy to inadvertently miss a signal in a sensitivity  
list  that  is required  to  make  the  process  function  correctly.  The  conditional  signal  assignment  
automatically creates the list of signals that are used to wake up the statement and is therefore the  
preferred method.
Process statements are so important in VHDL that we will devote an entire chapter on how to use them  
in chapter 8 of this book.
Summary
VHDL is a concurrent language, so it might be surprising to learn that there are only seven concurrent  
statements  that can be used to describe a digital logic network. In fact, only three statements (the  
conditional signal assignment, the selected signal assignment, and the process statement) can be used  
to describe a digital logic function and drive signals. The remaining statements are used for either file  
and design organization (the generate statement, component instantiation, and the block statement) or  
for verifying performance under simulation (the concurrent assertion statement).
This points to the amount of thought that was put into designing the language. Only a few fairly simple  
constructs are necessary to describe digital logic networks ranging from the trivial to the complex.

--- Page 124 ---

CHAPTER 7
SEQUENTIAL STATEMENTS
Thus far, we have made a strong case that the behavior of concurrent logic networks is best described 
using concurrent statements in order for the behavior to be  modeled efficiently. It therefore may come  
as a surprise that VHDL also contains sequential statements, such as those of traditional programming  
languages. The reason for this is that it is simpler to describe the behavior of sequential logic circuits  
(those that contain flip-flops) using sequential language constructs. Oftentimes, sequential circuits  
contain logic for control of the flip-flops as well as logic to generate the data values being stored in the  
flip-flops, and combining these two paths together into a series of sequential statements is much easier  
to read and more efficient than trying to “force” the functionality into concurrent statements. The fact  
that sequential statements are best used to describe sequential logic is not coincidental.
As mentioned previously, concurrent statements “ wake up” when an event occurs on any input signal,  
the statement is reevaluated, and a possible update to the output signal is scheduled as a result of the  
reevaluation. Furthermore, all statements within an architecture must be concurrent, and as mentioned  
earlier the consequence of concurrency is that the actual ordering of the statements is unimportant. The 
ordering of s equential statements, on the other hand, is important, and they are executed as they are  
encountered  during execution. Sequential statements can only appear inside process statements and  
subprograms (which we will deal with in chapter 10). While concurrent statements stand on their own  
and cannot be combined, sequential statements can be nested.
Equally surprising is that VHDL also supports the use of variables. While signals are used to connect  
concurrent  statements  together much  like wires and  always  update at some future point  in  time,  
variables  update immediately  and  can  only  be used  for local value storage within processes  and  
subprograms.  Because  they  are  used  for  local  storage,  variables  can  only  appear  within  process 
statements and subprograms.
Null Statement
The simplest sequential statement in VHDL is the null statement:
 null_statement ::=
null ;
The null statement means “do nothing” in VHDL. The reason for its inclusion is that the semicolon in  
VHDL is a statement terminator, as opposed to its use as a statement separator in C. While in C it is  
107

--- Page 127 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
perfectly acceptable to have a semicolon in the source code without any actual source code before it  
(which often occurs during expansion of preprocessor directives), a semicolon in VHDL without a  
statement is considered a syntax error. Placing a null statement before a semicolon honors the rules of  
VHDL syntax with regard to how semicolons are used.
The most common use of the null statement is with case statements, as we shall see in a subsequent  
section in this chapter.
Wait Statement
The wait statement halts execution of a process until one or more of the following specified conditions  
are met:
• An event occurs on a signal using the on clause.
• An expression becomes true using the until clause.
• A specified amount of time has elapsed using the for clause.
 wait_statement ::=
wait [ on sensitivity_list ] [ until condition ] [ for time_expression ] ;
Any combination of the three clauses can be used in a wait statement, which will terminate if any of  
the conditions are satisfied.  wait statements can only appear inside  process statements that do not  
contain  a  sensitivity  list  or  inside  procedures,  one  of  two  types  of  VHDL subprograms.  wait 
statements are behavioral constructs that should not be used in synthesizable code.
Examples of wait statements are:
wait for 5 uS;                    --  Waits for a specified time
wait on CLOCK;                    --  Waits for an event on a signal
wait until QOUT = "00000000";     --  Waits for a specified value on a signal
wait until INTR = '1' for 15 uS;  --  Compound wait (value OR time)
wait;                             --  Wait forever (stops the process)
The final form of the  wait statement shown above is a special case that halts the process for the  
remainder of a simulation. It is generally used as the last statement in a process used in a test bench  
(the code that is used to drive a simulation).
The user may be curious as to what the behavior would be  for the following (perfectly legal)  wait 
statement:
wait for 0 nS;
What  this statement does  is wait  for one  delta cycle  before continuing. This allows prior signal  
assignments to update, and subsequent references to those signals will use the newly assigned values.  
The effect of this statement is shown in the following:
108

--- Page 128 ---
CHAPTER 7 SEQUENTIAL STATEMENTS
A <= 1;         --  A gets 1 after one delta cycle
wait for 0 nS;  --  Wait for 1 delta cycle (signal A updates)
B <= A;         --  B gets 1 after one delta cycle
Of course, this statement can only be used in behavioral VHDL modeling, since the wait statement is 
not supported in synthesizable code.
If Statement
The if statement is identical to the typical if-then-else statement that you should be familiar with in  
traditional programming languages. It provides conditional execution of sequential statements based  
upon a series of test conditions:
 if_statement ::=
if condition then
sequence_of_statements
{ elsif condition then
sequence_of_statements }
[ else
sequence_of_statements ]
end if ;
Note that the keyword elsif is not a typo; this is the keyword in VHDL that defines the beginning of  
an else-if branch in an if statement.
The following are examples of how the if statement is used:
if (COUNTER = 15) then
  COUNTER <= 0;
end if;
if (RESET = '1') then
  Q <= '0';
else
  Q <= DIN;
end if;
if (ENA = '1') then
  if (LOAD = '1') then
    COUNTER <= INPUT;
  else
    if (MODE = UP) then
      COUNTER <= COUNTER + 1;
    elsif (MODE = DOWN) then
      COUNTER <= COUNTER – 1;
    end if;
  end if;
end if;
The if statement is the sequential equivalent of the conditional signal assignment, with the exception  
that the  else clause is optional in an  if statement, whereas a final  else clause is required in a  
conditional signal assignment.
109

--- Page 129 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Case Statement
The case statement is similar to the switch statement in C. It provides for conditional execution of  
sequential statements based on the value of a single expression:
 case_statement ::=
case expression is
when choices =>
sequence_of_statements
{ when choices =>
sequence_of_statements }
end case ;
There are two differences between the case statement in VHDL and the switch statement in C. The  
first is that the choices must cover all the possible values that the expression can equal. The second is  
that execution does not fall through the branches like in C. These rules are identical to those of the  
selected  signal  assignment  statement.  A  typical  mistake  that  even  seasoned  VHDL  designers  
occasionally make is:
case OPCODE is
  when "00"   =>  DATA <= A and B;
  when "01"   =>  DATA <= A or  B;
  when "10"   =>  DATA <= not (A and B);
  when "11"   =>  DATA <= not (A or  B);
end case;
Even though it appears that all four cases of  OPCODE have been covered by the case statement, if  
OPCODE is a s td_logic_1164 vector, there are nine possible values for each bit in  OPCODE.  This 
means that there are 81 possible cases (9 2) that have to be covered, not 4. Therefore, the others case 
must be included as the final choice  of the case statement in order to cover all of the other possible  
values that OPCODE could equal, as follows:
case OPCODE is 
  when "00"   =>  DATA <= A and B;
  when "01"   =>  DATA <= A or  B;
  when "10"   =>  DATA <= not (A and B);
  when "11"   =>  DATA <= not (A or  B);
  when others =>  null; 
end case;
Note as mentioned before that the  null statement is typical ly used in this manner within a  case 
statement.
The case statement is the sequential equivalent of the selected signal assignment statement. Just as in  
selected signal assignments, the choices specified in a case statement can take on several different  
forms. The first way is by specifying a single constant value such as that shown in the example above.  
Choices can also be specified using a list of constants separated by a vertical bar token (“ |”) or can be 
ranges, as follows:
110


--- Page 130 ---
CHAPTER 7 SEQUENTIAL STATEMENTS
case ADDRESS is
  when 0 | 7  =>  DATA <= REG_A;
  when 1 to 5 =>  DATA <= REG_B;
  when 6      =>  DATA <= REG_C;
  when others =>  null; 
end case;
Just like with the selected signal assignment statement, the choices must be static, meaning that the  
choice values must be known at compile time. Thus, only constant values can be used when specifying  
the choices used in a case statement.
Loop Statement
The loop statement is used to repeatedly execute a set of sequential statements. In VHDL, t here are 
three forms of the loop statement:
• for … loop: uses an automatically created iteration counter variable to execute a specified number 
of passes through the loop.
• while … loop: continually executes passes through the loop as long as a specified condition is 
true.
• loop: loops that do not contain either of the other two forms are infinite for loops.
 loop_statement ::=
[ label : ]
[ iteration_scheme ] loop
sequence_of_statements
end loop [ label ] ;
 iteration_scheme ::=
( while condition | for identifier in range )
Examples of these three forms of loop statements are:
for I in 1 to 10 loop     --  Loop 10 times
  sequential statements
end loop;
while (STATE = RUN) loop  --  Loop as long as STATE equals RUN
  sequential statements
end loop;
loop                      --  Infinite loop
  sequential statements
end loop;
Note that in the case of the  for … loop form, the iteration variable is automatically created. This  
means that you do not need to explicitly declare the variable like you would in C. The iteration variable 
exists only within the body of the loop statement and is undefined outside of the loop.
When used in synthesizable code, the loop iteration scheme must be static (the condition or range must 
evaluate to a constant at compile time). Hence, the only form of the loop statement that can generally  
111

--- Page 131 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
be used in synthesizable code is for … loop; the while … loop and infinite loop forms are typically  
not static and therefore should not be used. As was the case with generate statements, a consequence  
of this static requirement for synthesizable code is that you cannot use signals in the  for … loop 
iteration range specification, only constants and generics.
Loop Control Statements
During the execution of loops, there are times where they need to be terminated or restarted based on a  
set of conditions. This is especially true for infinite loops, where there must be a way of controlling  
when the loop terminates; otherwise the loop would execute forever, and the simulation would get  
“stuck.” There are two loop control statements, next and exit:
 next_statement ::=
next [ label ] [ when condition ] ;
 exit_statement ::=
exit [ label ] [ when condition ] ;
next is the equivalent of the  continue statement in C, which terminates the current iteration of the  
loop and restarts the loop at the next iteration.  exit is the equivalent of the  break statement in C,  
which terminates the current iteration and exits the loop. Both statements can specify a loop label,  
which  is  mostly  used  for  nested  loops;  loop  labels  are  rarely  used  in  cases  where  loop  control  
statements do not need to select the target of a nested set of loops or where only a single nonnested  
loop is used.
It should be noted that the next and exit statements should only be used in behavioral models. Since  
loop iteration schemes must be static in synthesizable code, the condition that would trigger a next or 
exit statement must also be static. There are cases  where the designer could possibly use these  
statements in synthesizable code,  but the chances  of confusing  the logic synthesis  tool are high;  
therefore, it is best to avoid these statements if at all possible.
The most common use of the next and exit statements are actions to be taken as the result of a test for  
a particular condition.  The  when clause provides  a shorthand  way  of creating  this  common  code  
construct:
if (condition) then
  exit;
end if;
exit when condition;
The following behavioral example shows how the loop control statements are used:
L1:  for I in VEC'reverse_range loop
       next when VEC (I) = '0';       --  Continue with the next iteration
L2:    for J in VEC'range loop
         exit L1 when I = J;          --  Exit the outer loop
         RESULT (J) <= not VEC (J);
       end loop L2;
     end loop L1;
112
Equivalent

--- Page 132 ---
CHAPTER 7 SEQUENTIAL STATEMENTS
Readers may have noticed that there is no one-trip loop construct in VHDL, where the loop will be  
executed at least one time, such as the do … while statement in C. There is only the zero-trip behavior 
of the  while … loop statement. In order to implement the equivalent of the do loop in C, the loop  
statement must be constructed in VHDL using an exit statement as follows:
do {
  sequential statements
} while CONDITON = true;
loop
  sequential statements
  exit when CONDITON = FALSE;
end loop;
Note that the “sense” of the loop control test must be inverted: in C, a true condition continues the 
loop; in VHDL, a true condition will terminate the loop.
Assertion and Report Statements
The syntax for the sequential assertion statement looks and behaves exactly the same as the concurrent  
assertion statement presented in chapter 6. However, as opposed to the concurrent assertion statement,  
where  the  parameter  is  continually tested  and  produces  messages  when  the  assertion  fails,  the  
sequential assertion statement will test a condition and generate messages  only when it is encountered  
during the execution of a series of sequential statements:
 assertion_statement ::=
assert condition
[ report expression ]
[ severity expression ] ;
The  report statement  is  a  shorthand  form  of  the  assertion  statement  where  the assertion  test  is  
removed and the report message and severity  level are  always generated. This is mainly used to  
generate status messages during testing:
 report_statement ::=
report expression[ severity expression ] ;
The following report statement:
report "Starting receive data testing" severity note;
is equivalent to:
assert FALSE report "Starting receive data testing" severity note;
As with concurrent assertion statements, the sequential a ssertion and report statements are behavioral  
in nature and as such should only appear in behavioral VHDL models. They should not be used in  
synthesizable code.
113

--- Page 133 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Signal Assignment
The sequential signal assignment statement is a simplified version of the concurrent conditional and  
selected signal assignment statements. In this case, a target signal will be unconditionally assigned a 
single waveform when the statement is executed. Since this statement will appear inside a series of  
sequential statements, the statements around the signal assignment will create the conditions in which  
the signal can be assigned a waveform:
 signal_assignment ::=
target <= [ options ] waveform ;
 options ::=
( guarded | transport )
 waveform ::=
expression [ after time_expression ]
{ , expression [ after time_expression ] }
Sequential signal assignments always update the signal at some future time,  exactly like concurrent 
signal assignments, including an update one delta cycle in the future if no after clause is included in  
the waveform. The  delay model rules  (inertial vs. transport) are exactly the same as those used for  
concurrent signal assignments.
One issue that can arise  when using  sequential signal assignments is how signals react to multiple  
assignments within the same set of sequential statements . The problem is that a series of sequential  
statements are executed without consuming any simulation time, meaning they execute in a single  
delta cycle. This behavior is interrupted by a wait statement, and only then will the simulation time be 
advanced and the signal assignments take place.
Consider the following sequential code sample:
SIG <= '1' after 10 nS, '0' after 30 nS, '1' after 50 nS, '0' after 80 nS;
if (CONDITION) then
  SIG <= '1' after 20 nS, '0' after 40 nS;
end if;
wait for 100 nS;
If CONDITION is false, the second signal assignment will be skipped, and the resultant waveform on  
SIG will look like:
However, if  CONDITION is  true, the second signal assignment will be  executed, and the resultant  
waveform on SIG will look like:
114
0
0 nS 50 nS 100 nS
1
SIG

--- Page 134 ---
CHAPTER 7 SEQUENTIAL STATEMENTS
What occurs in this case is that assign ing  SIG to  '1' at 20  nS  cancels all the subsequent signal  
assignments that occur at or after 20  nS. Thus, the first waveform element in the  initial assignment 
(assigning SIG to '1' at 10 nS) will still occur when the wait statement is executed, but the subsequent 
waveform elements of that initial assignment are canceled, and instead SIG is assigned a '1' at 20 nS 
and a '0' at 40 nS. Care must be taken whenever the possibility of multiple signal assignments to the  
same target can occur within a series of sequential statements that do not include a wait statement.
Care must also be taken when assigning and accessing signal values within a process. Since signal  
values always update at a future time, accessing the value of a signal returns the current value, not a 
value that  may have been  assigned within the process. Consider the following code for a processor  
address counter that determines the address increment value based on the value of a MODE signal and  
updates the value of ADDRESS. This process will execute each time there is an event on UPDATE and 
suspend when the process completes. The INCREMENT and ADDRESS signals will be recomputed and  
updated one delta cycle after the process suspends whenever there is a rising edge on UPDATE. Again, 
we assume that UPDATE should only contain values of '0' and '1'; if UPDATE is an unknown state, it 
is indicative of larger problems within the logic network:
ADDRESS_PROCESS:  process (UPDATE) begin
  if (UPDATE = '1') then
    if (MODE = BYTE) then
      INCREMENT <= 1;
    elsif (MODE = WORD) then
      INCREMENT <= 2;
    else
      INCREMENT <= 4;
    end if;
    ADDRESS <= ADDRESS + INCREMENT;
  end if;
end process;
In this case, ADDRESS will always update with the current value of INCREMENT instead of the newly 
assigned value based on  MODE, which only changes after the process suspends. The better way to  
perform this function is to use the following process instead:
ADDRESS_PROCESS:  process (UPDATE) begin
  if (UPDATE = '1') then
    if (MODE = BYTE) then
      ADDRESS <= ADDRESS + 1;
    elsif (MODE = WORD) then
      ADDRESS <= ADDRESS + 2;
    else
      ADDRESS <= ADDRESS + 4;
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
Using this process will always correctly update the address counter based solely on the value of MODE.
115
SIG
0
0 nS 50 nS 100 nS
1


--- Page 135 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Variable Assignment
Variables are used to store data values that are local to either a process or a subprogram. They update  
immediately and cannot be used to connect concurrent statements together within an architecture:
 variable_assignment ::=
target := expression ;
The assignment operator for variable assignment is the  := token (colon-equals) instead of the  <= 
operator used for signal assignment. This is done to reinforce the concept that signals update in the  
future, while variables update immediately. Because variables update immediately, there cannot be any  
time expressions in the assignment like that of the waveform specifications that are used in signal  
assignments.
Consider the address counter example from the previous section on sequential signal assignment. The  
process could be written using variables as follows:
ADDRESS_PROCESS:  process (UPDATE) is
  variable  INCREMENT : natural range 0 to 4;
begin
  if (UPDATE = '1') then
    if (MODE = BYTE) then
      INCREMENT := 1;
    elsif (MODE = WORD) then
      INCREMENT := 2;
    else
      INCREMENT := 4;
    end if;
    ADDRESS <= ADDRESS + INCREMENT;
  end if;
end process;
Using a variable for INCREMENT instead of a signal will cause the desired increment value to update  
immediately, and the value used for the final address assignment will be correct.
Variables should be used sparingly in synthesizable code because they are a behavioral construct and  
should typically only be used in behavioral models. Often, the same behavior can be achieved solely  
through the use of signals, which generally results in a better chance that the synthesized hardware will  
match the results of simulation. The address counter could be written as follows:
INCREMENT <= 1 when (MODE = BYTE) else
             2 when (MODE = WORD) else
             4;
ADDRESS_PROCESS:  process (UPDATE) begin
  if (UPDATE = '1') then
    ADDRESS <= ADDRESS + INCREMENT;
  end if;
end process;
INCREMENT is now assigned via a concurrent selected signal assignment and will update every time the 
MODE input changes.  The value of  INCREMENT will then  be correct  when  used  to increment  the  
ADDRESS signal. This last example shows how logic functions are composed using VHDL and the  
116

--- Page 136 ---
CHAPTER 7 SEQUENTIAL STATEMENTS
“division of labor” between multiple concurrent statements to describe the functionality of digital logic  
networks.
Summary
Although VHDL is a concurrent language, it also contains sequential statements that can be used to  
efficiently model the behavior of sequential circuits (circuits that contain flip-flops). Seven of the eight 
sequential VHDL statements do not consume any simulation time and execute during a single delta  
cycle  time.  The  exception  is  the  wait statement,  which  suspends  the  processing  of  sequential  
statements until the specified condition is met, at which point the execution begins again.
All of the sequential statements must be wrapped inside either a process statement or subprogram.
117

--- Page 137 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL

CHAPTER 8
THE PROCESS STATEMENT
The  process statement is the most  commonly used statement to describe a digital logic network in  
VHDL. Processes can be used to describe the behavior of any type of digital logic, which can be  
separated into the following three broad categories:
• Combinatorial logic. Digital logic that contains Boolean logic gates (AND/NAND, OR/NOR, 
exclusive OR/NOR, INVERTERS, etc.).
• Sequential logic. Digital logic that contains storage elements such as flip-flops and latches.
• Memory. Digital logic that contains arrays of data that can be read and (optionally) written such 
as RAMs and ROMs.
The process statement is so important in modeling digital logic that we will spend an entire chapter  
discussing it. Specifically, we will describe how processes operate and how they are used to model and  
create logic functions. Processes are concurrent statements that contain  sequential  statements and  
variable  assignments  and  can  be thought  of as  the  link  that  brings  sequential  functions  into  the  
concurrent environment of VHDL, which could not be otherwise used inside an architecture. The  
diagram on the next page shows how the process statement fits within the hierarchy of the VHDL  
library units.
For review, the syntax definition of the process statement is:
 process_statement ::=
[ label : ]
process [ ( sensitivity_list ) ] [ is ]
process_declarative_part
begin
process_statement_part
end process [ label ] ;

--- Page 139 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
120
This diagram  shows how all of the concurrent and sequential statements fit within the VHDL  
framework  of  the  library  unit.  Entities are  containers  that  hold  generic  and  port  clauses.  
Architectures are containers that hold concurrent statements. Processes are containers that hold  
sequential statements within the architecture. The entity/architecture pair is the container that  
holds the complete description of the digital logic.
