--- Page 164 ---
CHAPTER 9
MODELING CASE STUDIES
At this point, we will take a break from learning the syntax of VHDL and present several examples of  
how VHDL is used to create digital logic networks. We will examine the three general modeling styles  
(behavioral, RTL, and structural) as well as work through a complete design example starting with a  
set of system requirements, creating a functional block diagram, and then writing the VHDL that  
satisfies those requirements.
Before we study the cases presented in this chapter, we first  need to discuss  how the VHDL code  
should be constructed and the overall modeling style that should be used.
Modeling Style
Modeling style differs from coding style in that coding style concerns how the statements in the code  
are to be formatted, such as the number of spaces used for indenting nested code and the use of  
uppercase versus lowercase letters in identifiers and keywords. Modeling style, on the other hand, has  
to do with how the code is written so that it is readable and understandable and allows the code to be 
easily modified at a later date if necessary. Modeling style can have a huge impact on the  results of  
logic synthesis in terms of both the size of the circuit and the resulting functionality. The ultimate goal  
of designing in VHDL is creating a synthesized logic network, one that matches the intended function 
described by the code. Writing code that is logical and well structured is paramount in meeting that  
goal.
As mentioned previously, the entity/architecture pair are the library units that describe a logic network  
in VHDL. The entity provides the interface of the network to the outside world, and the architecture  
contains the statements that describe the functionality and create the actual logic gates. Architectures 
are  typically  made  up  of  a  combination  of  concurrent  signal  assignments  and  clocked  process  
statements. The recommended structure of the statements in the architecture is that the overall signal  
flow within the architecture should follow the functional flow of the circuit. Just as there is a preferred  
signal flow in schematics, block diagrams, and flowcharts (left to right, top to bottom), the functional  
flow within an architecture should be from the top to the bottom of the file, and “closely related” code  
should be grouped together. The processing of entity input signals should generally occur near the top  
of the architecture, and the signal drivers for the entity outputs should generally be near the bottom of  
the file. This allows the reader to scroll through the code and follow the overall flow of the circuit  
being described, which greatly enhances readability both for code reviews and for debugging.
145

--- Page 165 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
An example of how the function should flow through the architecture is shown in the following  
notional code:
architecture RTL of EXAMPLE is
  --  Signals and constant declarations
begin
  --  Concurrent signal assignments
  --  Clocked processes
  --  Concurrent signal assignments
  --  Clocked processes
  --  Concurrent signal assignments
  --  Clocked processes
end architecture EXAMPLE;
The first consideration in describing the logic with  VHDL is to recognize that combinatorial Boolean  
logic gates  are best  described using conditional and  selected  signal  assignment  statements,  while  
circuits  containing  flip-flops  are  best  described using  process  statements  that  contain sequential  
statements. That is not to say that you should place all of the combinatorial logic outside the process  
and clock the result inside the process, like:
D <= A and not B when (ENA = '1') else Q;
---------------------------
QFF:  process (CLOCK) begin
---------------------------
  if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    Q <= D;
  end if;
end process;
If the intermediate combinatorial signal is only used in one place (or perhaps only in a small number of  
places), it is better to write the code like:
---------------------------
QFF:  process (CLOCK) begin
---------------------------
  if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (ENA = '1') then
      Q <= A and not B;
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
However, if the intermediate combinatorial signal is used in many places, it is better to define it as an  
internal signal and drive it using a concurrent statement. This internal signal is then used instead:
146
Related code
Related code
Related code
Functional flow

--- Page 166 ---
CHAPTER 9 MODELING CASE STUDIES
CLKENA <= REQUEST and READY;
---------------------------
FF1:  process (CLOCK) begin
---------------------------
  if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (CLKENA = '1') then
      Q <= A and not B;
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
----------------------------------
FF2:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
----------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    T <= '0';
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (CLKENA = '1') then
      T <= C or D;
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
The second consideration in writing the VHDL model is the number of processes that should be used  
to describe the functionality of the circuit. Too many (or too few) processes leads to code that is hard to 
read and understand and can lead to unintended consequences when the code is synthesized, especially  
when it comes to trying to pack too much functionality into a single process.
As described in chapter  8, functional elements with differing direct-action inputs (clock, set, reset)  
should be in separate processes, which provides some guidance in determining the number of required  
processes. From there, the general rule is that functionality that naturally “goes together” should be  
placed in a common process, while functionality that does not “go together” should be placed in  
separate processes. The definition of what naturally goes together and what doesn’t can be somewhat  
nebulous and can be the subject of a great amount of debate and interpretation. What is helpful when  
trying to determine how many processes should be contained in an architecture is to think in terms of  
functions, not gates.
Binary Adder
The first circuit we will look at is a clocked unsigned binary adder using the three major forms of  
modeling in VHDL, namely behavioral, RTL (synthesizable), and structural. All three forms will use  
the same entity declaration, which must be declared in such a way that it is common to all three  
modeling forms:
147

--- Page 167 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The common entity declaration is shown below:
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  ADDER
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  This entity implements an N-bit adder where the width is specified by BITS.
--  A and B are the two input vectors and CIN is the carry into the adder. SUM
--  is the output vector and COUT is the carry out.
library IEEE;
use     IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
use     IEEE.numeric_std.all;
library Vendor;
use     Vendor.vcomponents.all;
---------------
entity ADDER is
--------------- 
  generic ( BITS : natural :=  8 );                         --  Number of bits
  port    ( RST  : in  std_logic;                           --  Active high master reset
            CLK  : in  std_logic;                           --  Master clock
            A    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);  --  Input data value
            B    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);  --  Input data value
            CIN  : in  std_logic;                           --  Carry in
            SUM  : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);  --  Output data value
            COUT : out std_logic );                         --  Carry out
end entity ADDER;
Note that along with the normal library and package use statements to make the  IEEE data types  
visible, we have also included a package of vendor components named  vcomponents that will be  
needed for the structural model. We also have declared all of our port signals to be of the std_logic 
or std_logic_vector type. Adopting a standard interface type that is used by all the entities greatly  
simplifies the task of connecting the entities together and leads to much less debugging. It is highly  
recommended that the designer adopt a standard entity interface type and follow it religiously.
Behavioral Model
We  now  turn  to  the  behavioral  model  for  our  clocked  unsigned  adder.  Behavioral  modeling  is  
algorithmic in nature and focuses on the  what, not the  how. In other words, a behavioral model is  
concerned with what the algorithm does, not how to implement it in actual logic gates. Thus, at this  
level of modeling, the designer does not need to worry about writing code that can be synthesized into  
gates.
Our behavioral model looks like:
148
ENTITY
BEHAVIORAL
SYNTHESIZABLE
STRUCTURAL
Architectures

--- Page 168 ---
CHAPTER 9 MODELING CASE STUDIES
---------------------------------
architecture BEHAVIOR of ADDER is
---------------------------------
begin
  -----------------------------------
  ADD_PROCESS:  process (RST, CLK) is
  -----------------------------------
    constant  OVERFLOW : natural := 2 ** BITS;
    variable  ISUM     : natural;
  begin
    if (RST = '1') then
      SUM  <= (others => '0');
      COUT <= '0';
    elsif (CLK'event and CLK = '1') then
      ISUM := To_Integer (unsigned (A)) + To_Integer (unsigned (B));
      if (CIN = '1') then
        ISUM := ISUM + 1;
      end if;
      if (ISUM >= OVERFLOW) then
        ISUM := ISUM – OVERFLOW;
        COUT <= '1';
      else
        COUT <= '0';
      end if;
      SUM <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ISUM, BITS));
    end if;
  end process;
end architecture BEHAVIOR;
Our architecture consists of a single process, which uses the direct action signals RST and CLK in the 
sensitivity list to trigger the processing of the sequential statements inside the process. The process  
itself declares a local constant  OVERFLOW, which will detect when the sum overflows and drive the  
carry out bit, and ISUM, an internal variable that is used to compute the sum of the two input vectors.
When RST is asserted, we clear the SUM and COUT outputs; otherwise, we perform our addition on a  
rising  edge  of  CLK.  When  a  rising  edge  is  detected,  the  A and  B input  vectors  are  recast  from  
std_logic_vector to  unsigned and  then  converted to  integer via the  To_Integer built-in  
function, and the two values are added together and stored in ISUM. Next, if the carry in is asserted, the 
internal ISUM variable is incremented by 1. At this point, we have the sum of the two input vectors plus 
the carry in bit and can determine whether there has been an overflow, in which case ISUM is adjusted 
to remove the overflow, and the COUT output is asserted. If there has not been an overflow, the COUT 
output is cleared. Finally, the internal sum is converted to an  unsigned vector and then recast as a  
std_logic_vector, and that value is used to drive SUM.
Synthesizable Model
Synthesizable (RTL) modeling is primarily written in terms of specifying storage elements and the  
logic assignments between them. RTL modeling is concerned with how to implement the circuit in  
actual logic gates. The general format of an RTL model is that the user specifies where all the storage  
elements (flip-flops, memories, etc.) are in the circuit and provides a description of the logic equations  
that are used to connect all of the storage elements together. Conceptually, an RTL model looks like:
149

--- Page 169 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The RTL model for our clocked unsigned adder can be written as:
----------------------------
architecture RTL of ADDER is
----------------------------
  signal  ISUM : unsigned (BITS downto 0);  --  ISUM is one bit wider that BITS
begin
--  Compute the sum on each rising edge of the clock
  --------------------------------------
  ADD_PROCESS:  process (RST, CLK) begin
  --------------------------------------
    if (RST = '1') then
      ISUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLK'event and CLK = '1') then
      if (CIN = '0') then
        ISUM <= resize (unsigned (A), BITS+1) + resize (unsigned (B), BITS+1);
      else
        ISUM <= resize (unsigned (A), BITS+1) + resize (unsigned (B), BITS+1) + 1;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Drive the adder outputs:
--    SUM  is the lower BITS of ISUM
--    COUT is the MSB of ISUM
  SUM  <= std_logic_vector (ISUM (BITS-1 downto 0));
  COUT <= ISUM (BITS);
end architecture RTL;
The most notable change between the behavioral and RTL models is the removal of the variables from 
within the process and the addition of the signal ISUM as an unsigned vector that is one bit wider than 
the number of bits in the adder (to account for the carry out of the adder chain). Also note the use of  
the function resize, which is part of the numeric_std package. This function resizes the A and B 
vectors to be BITS + 1 in width, which is necessary to produce a sum that is also BITS + 1 wide.
An alternative approach to the RTL model would be to create an internal carry signal ICARRY, which 
converts  the  CIN input  from  a  std_logic to  a  natural via  a  conditional  signal  assignment  
statement. This value can then be added directly to the SUM:
150
D Q
LOGIC
EQUATIONS D Q
LOGIC
EQUATIONS

--- Page 170 ---
CHAPTER 9 MODELING CASE STUDIES
----------------------------
architecture RTL of ADDER is
----------------------------
  signal  ISUM   : unsigned (BITS downto 0);  --  ISUM is one bit wider that BITS
  signal  ICARRY : natural range 0 to 1;
begin
--  Convert the CIN bit to a natural for addition
  ICARRY <= 1 when (CIN = '1') else 0;
--  Compute the sum on each rising edge of the clock
  --------------------------------------
  ADD_PROCESS:  process (RST, CLK) begin
  --------------------------------------
    if (RST = '1') then
      ISUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLK'event and CLK = '1') then
      ISUM <= resize (unsigned (A), BITS+1) + resize (unsigned (B), BITS+1) + ICARRY;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Drive the adder outputs:
--    SUM  is the lower BITS of ISUM
--    COUT is the MSB of ISUM
  SUM  <= std_logic_vector (ISUM (BITS-1 downto 0));
  COUT <= ISUM (BITS);
end architecture RTL;
In both cases, note how each section of the code includes a comment that briefly describes the intent of  
the code that is to follow. This is a good habit to get into, as it greatly aids in readability.
Structural Model
The third type of model within VHDL is the structural model, which is often referred to as a netlist. 
Netlists are typically generated by a logic synthesis tool from the RTL code:
Netlists are complicated, and mistakes are easy to make if the netlist is generated by hand, and it is  
therefore best to always let the computer create the structural netlists. An example of a structural model 
for our clocked unsigned adder is:
151
RTL NETLIST
Synthesis


--- Page 171 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
----------------------------------
architecture STRUCTURE of ADDER is
----------------------------------
  signal  NET0001 : std_logic;
  signal  NET0002 : std_logic;
        ... etc ...
  signal  NET0039 : std_logic;
  signal  NET0040 : std_logic;
begin
  U0001:  XOR3 (I1 => A (0), I2 => B (0), I3 => CIN, Y => NET0001);
  U0002:  AND2 (I1 => A (0), I2 => B (0), Y => NET0002);
  U0003:  XOR2 (I1 => A (0), I2 => B (0), Y => NET0003);
  U0004:  AND2 (I1 => NET0003, I2 => CIN, Y => NET0004);
  U0005:  OR2 (I1 => NET0002, I2 => NET0004, Y => NET0005);
  U0006:  DFF (R => RST, C => CLK, D => NET0001, Q => SUM (0));
  U0007:  XOR3 (I1 => A (1), I2 => B (1), I3 => NET0005, Y => NET0006);
  U0008:  AND2 (I1 => A (1), I2 => B (1), Y => NET0007);
  U0009:  XOR2 (I1 => A (1), I2 => B (1), Y => NET0008);
  U0010:  AND2 (I1 => NET0008, I2 => NET0005, Y => NET0009);
  U0011:  OR2 (I1 => NET0007, I2 => NET0009, Y => NET0010);
  U0012:  DFF (R => RST, C => CLK, D => NET0006, Q => SUM (1));
            ... etc ...
  U0043:  XOR3 (I1 => A (7), I2 => B (7), I3 => NET0035, Y => NET0036);
  U0044:  AND2 (I1 => A (7), I2 => B (7), Y => NET0037);
  U0045:  XOR2 (I1 => A (7), I2 => B (7), Y => NET0038);
  U0046:  AND2 (I1 => NET0038, I2 => NET0035, Y => NET0039);
  U0047:  OR2 (I1 => NET0037, I2 => NET0039, Y => NET0040);
  U0048:  DFF (R => RST, C => CLK, D => NET0036, Q => SUM (7));
  U0049:  DFF (R => RST, C => CLK, D => NET0040, Q => COUT);
end architecture STRUCTURE;
Structural models use logic gate components ( XOR3, AND2, etc.), which are connected together via a  
series of signals. Clearly this form of modeling is difficult to read and is quite complicated to write,  
and errors can easily be created if done by hand. This is why structural models are almost always  
generated by a computer program.
Summary
Behavioral modeling in VHDL is the easiest to write, and solutions can be quickly written and tested.  
However,  behavioral  models  do  not  readily  map  to  digital  logic,  which  is  the  ultimate  goal  of  
performing digital design in VHDL. In order to create a digital logic network, you must code in a style  
known  as  RTL,  which  concentrates  on  the  logic  implementation  and  takes  more  time  to  write.  
Structural modeling in VHDL is very difficult to write and is very prone to errors; therefore, it is  
usually generated only by computer programs.
Engine Management System
Our second example will present a more complicated circuit that is used as part of an automobile  
engine management system. We will only look at writing the RTL model for this design.
A digital logic design is never created in a vacuum; there is always a set of formal requirements the  
designer is given to solve a problem. These requirements usually come from either the customer or a  
marketing group and are refined and enumerated by a systems engineering group, who is tasked with  
creating the overall design of the system and breaking down the requirements into the processing  
152

--- Page 172 ---
CHAPTER 9 MODELING CASE STUDIES
elements and algorithms used to implement the desired end product, that is, software and hardware and  
the interfaces between these elements.
In this example, the problem that the circuit will addresses is this:
An engine management system requires a periodic reading of a certain engine performance  
parameter in order to allow the system to adjust the fuel and air mixture to the engine and  
maintain high fuel mileage.
The set of requirements for this example are:
• The engine management system will command the circuit to compute an average via a 
REQUEST input signal.
• When enabled, the circuit will take eight consecutive readings and compute the average.
• The engine readings are 8-bit unsigned data.
• The input readings from the engine are valid when the VALID input signal is asserted.
• The circuit will indicate that an average is available via a READY output signal.
If we read through these requirements, we begin to see that the logic required for this circuit can be  
broken down into three distinct operations:
1. Logic that monitors the REQUEST input, determines when the circuit is running, and counts  
valid samples via the VALID input.
2. Logic that accumulates valid samples and computes the average.
3. Logic that drives the READY output at the end of each averaging request.
From the description of these three operations, we can put together a block diagram that will assist us  
in composing our circuit in VHDL:
We begin the process of creating the circuit in VHDL by writing the entity declaration:
153
RUN
CONTROL
SAMPLE
ACCUM
READY
GEN
RUNNING
REQUEST
RUNNING
DATA
READY
AVG
SAMPLE
VALID

--- Page 173 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  AVERAGE
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  This entity will compute the average of 8 input samples and output the result.
--  The circuit will be enabled each time REQUEST is asserted and then add the next
--  8 samples which are valid when VALID is asserted. The result is output on AVG
--  and the READY output is pulsed to indicate that the average value is ready.
library IEEE;
use     IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
use     IEEE.numeric_std.all;
-----------------
entity AVERAGE is
-----------------
  port ( RESET   : in  std_logic;                      --  Active high master reset
         CLOCK   : in  std_logic;                      --  Master clock
         REQUEST : in  std_logic;                      --  Averaging request
         DATA    : in  std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);  --  Engine reading value
         VALID   : in  std_logic;                      --  Engine reading valid
         AVG     : out std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);  --  Average output value
         READY   : out std_logic );                    --  Average ready
end entity AVERAGE;
Just like in our last example, we make the IEEE library and the std_logic_1164 and numeric_std 
packages visible using the  library and  use statements. Then we declare our entity, using the five  
signals shown in the block diagram along with a clock and reset input.
We next turn our attention to the internal signals that will need to be declared inside the architecture.  
From our block diagram, we will need two internal signals, one that indicates when the circuit is  
running and another that counts the number of input samples in order to compute the average across  
eight of those samples. We also need to determine the width of our sample accumulator and declare  
that internal signal as well. If we accumulate eight 8 -bit unsigned samples, the maximum input value  
for each sample is 255, and the maximum value we could accumulate would be 8  × 255 = 2040. This 
means that our accumulator needs to be 11 bits wide (which has a maximum value of 2047). Therefore, 
we will need an 11 -bit internal vector to represent our accumulated value. Because we are performing  
addition on this vector, it is much more convenient to define this as an unsigned vector since we can  
perform math operations on unsigned vectors but not on std_logic_vector. In addition, we will  
declare one last internal signal, which will recast DIN from a std_logic_vector to an unsigned 
vector and resize it to 11  bits to match the width of the accumulator. The declaration portion of our  
architecture looks like:
------------------------------
architecture RTL of AVERAGE is
------------------------------
  signal  SAMPLE  : natural range 0 to 7;    --  Sample counter
  signal  RUNNING : std_logic;               --  Circuit running indicator
  signal  DIN     : unsigned (10 downto 0);  --  Resized unsigned input data
  signal  ACCUM   : unsigned (10 downto 0);  --  Sample accumulator
begin
A good habit to get into is to write a brief comment for each internal signal that is declared inside the  
architecture. This will help during the review process and also helps with debugging should the code  
need to change.
154

--- Page 174 ---
CHAPTER 9 MODELING CASE STUDIES
We can now begin writing the actual VHDL code that will describe the functionality of our circuit.  
From the block diagram, the first thing we see is the need to write the run control circuit, which we  
will describe using a process. Our block diagram shows that this function produces two outputs,  
SAMPLE and RUNNING, and therefore it is certainly appropriate to drive both of these “closely related”  
signals from within a single process:
--  Determine when the interface needs to run and
--    count the input samples for each request
  ----------------------------------------------
  CONTROL_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ----------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SAMPLE  <=  0;
      RUNNING <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (RUNNING = '0') then     --  If we are not running and get a REQUEST,
        if (REQUEST = '1') then   --    set RUNNING
          RUNNING <= '1';
        end if;
      elsif (VALID = '1') then    --  If we are running, accumulate samples
        if (SAMPLE = 7) then      --    when VALID = '1'
          SAMPLE  <=  0;          --  If SAMPLE = 7, we are done, so clear
          RUNNING <= '0';         --    RUNNING and SAMPLE
        else
          SAMPLE  <= SAMPLE + 1;  --  Count the number of samples received
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Next, our block diagram shows a function block that is used to accumulate incoming samples. We note  
that the first sample after the assertion of REQUEST needs to clear the accumulator and add the first  
sample, which is the same as just clocking the first sample into the accumulator without the addition  
operator. Detection of the first sample can be done by testing if SAMPLE = 0 when both VALID and 
RUNNING are asserted. We also include a simple concurrent signal assignment to recast and resize our  
8-bit DATA input as an 11-bit internal unsigned value named DIN:
155

--- Page 175 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
--  Accumulate valid samples while running
--  Resize the data path for the maximum possible value (8 * 255 = 2040)
  DIN <= resize (unsigned (DATA), 11);
  --------------------------------------------------
  ACCUMULATOR_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      ACCUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (RUNNING = '1') then      --  If we are running and get a valid sample,
        if (VALID = '1') then      --    accumulate the sample
          if (SAMPLE = 0) then     --  First sample?
            ACCUM <= DIN;          --    Set ACCUM to DIN (clears accumulator)
          else                     --  Otherwise,
            ACCUM <= ACCUM + DIN;  --    Add DIN to ACCUM for remaining samples
          end if;
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
The final function on the block diagram generates a pulse on the READY output when eight samples  
have been accumulated and the average value over those eight samples on the  AVG output port is  
available. We note that we can pulse READY when the VALID input is asserted and the internal SAMPLE 
equals 7. For all other conditions, the READY output is cleared:
--  Pulse READY on the clock cycle after the last sample is accumulated
  --------------------------------------------
  READY_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      READY <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (SAMPLE = 7) then
        READY <= VALID;     --  Set READY when the last sample is accumulated
      else
        READY <= '0';
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Finally, we need to compute the average over the eight input samples. Fortunately, division by 8 in  
binary is a trivial task. All that is required is to shift the accumulated sum right by three digits, thereby  
dropping the lower three bits and truncating the result:
--  Take the average by dividing the accumulated samples by 8
--    (shifting ACCUM right 3 bits)
  AVG <= std_logic_vector (ACCUM (10 downto 3));
end architecture RTL;
The simulation results for this circuit show that it indeed computes the average over eight samples  
whenever the  REQUEST line is asserted. The following waveform shows the results of one of those  
computations:
156

--- Page 176 ---
CHAPTER 9 MODELING CASE STUDIES
Note that after the REQUEST is made, the eight input samples are 199, 105, 225, 68, 49, 99, 103, and  
20, which are considered to be valid input values when the VALID control line input is asserted. The  
computed output value on AVG is 108 and is indicated by the assertion of the READY output. Note that:
199 + 105 + 225 + 68 + 49 + 99 + 103 + 20 = 868
and that:
868 ÷ 8 = 108.5
Since we are using truncation and not rounding of the result of the division, this matches the output of  
the circuit, which is 108.
157


--- Page 178 ---
CHAPTER 10
SUBPROGRAMS
In VHDL, subprograms are used to define a sequence of instructions that can be invoked many times  
via calls to the subprogram. Subprograms are mainly used to break up large tasks into smaller, more  
manageable ones. This is definitely the case for traditional software languages, but in VHDL design  
hierarchy is the method that is used to break up large tasks (logic networks) into smaller,  more  
manageable ones.  In  VHDL,  subprograms are mainly used  to efficiently  code repetitive tasks  or  
functions that can be written one time and invoked as many times as necessary. This again is part of the 
design efficiency that is contained in VHDL, the philosophy of “write once, use many.”
In VHDL, there are two types of subprograms:
• functions: A generalization of an expression. Functions encapsulate a series of sequential 
statements that can be executed many times and compute and return results.
• procedures: A generalization of a statement. Procedures encapsulate a series of sequential 
statements that can be executed multiple times and provide a certain behavior.
In VHDL, functions are used in both behavioral and synthesizable code; procedures, on the other hand,  
are mainly found in behavioral code (such as test benches) and are rarely used in RTL models.
Functions
Functions should be a familiar concept to anyone who has studied traditional programming languages.  
A function defines a series of sequential statements and returns  the  result of those statements. The  
function  can then be called wherever expressions are used  via the name of the function, and the  
returned value is substituted back into the calling statement. Function calls can be made in concurrent  
signal assignments and in sequential signal and variable assignment statements.
Functions are nonblocking and operate with zero delay, meaning that they consume no delta delay time 
during execution and always immediately return a value. There are two consequences that arise given  
that functions consume zero time. First, wait statements are not permitted inside a function. Placing a  
wait statement inside a function declaration will result in a compilation error. Second, sequential  
signal assignments are not permitted inside a function. Attempting to drive a signal from within a  
function will also result in a compilation error.
In VHDL, a function is declared using the following syntax:
159

--- Page 179 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
 function_declaration ::=
[ pure | impure ]
function identifier [ ( interface_list ) ] return type is
{ subprogram_declarative_part }
begin
{ sequential_statement }
end [ function ] [ identifier ] ;
 interface_list ::=
interface_element { ; interface_element }
 interface_element ::=
[ constant | signal ]
identifier { , identifier } : [ in ] type [ := expression ]
The interface list is a semicolon-separated list of elements that in turn are comma-separated lists of  
parameter identifiers that declare the type of the identifier and, optionally, a default value for the  
identifier if it is not specified when the function is called. Note that the only mode that is allowed in a  
function declaration is the mode in, which does not need to be explicitly declared. This is different  
from function calls in C, where a pointer could be passed into a function to provide both an initial  
value for the parameter upon calling the function and a final value that is passed back out when the  
function completes.
There are two  object classes that can be specified for each interface element (the term  object class 
refers to the three different objects that can be declared: constants, variable, and signals). The object  
class constant, which is the default, means that the parameter will be treated as if it were a constant  
inside the function. Do not assume that this means that only named or literal constant values can be  
connected  to  a  parameter  designated  as  a  constant.  Any  of  the  three  object  classes  (constants, 
variables, or signals) can be used in the function call.
The second object class that can be specified is  signal. Using the  signal designation forces the  
compiler to only accept references to signals for that parameter in the function call. Attempting to use a 
constant or variable for a parameter designated as a signal will result in a compilation error. The only  
time you would want to use a signal designation is when you are absolutely sure that the only object  
that will ever be connected to the parameter is a signal.
Note that the interface list is optional. It is completely legal to write a function that does not have any  
input parameters when the function is called. The  now function in the standard package, which returns 
the current simulation time, is an example of a function without any input parameters. Calls to a  
function such as now are different than how it is done in C. In C, the call would include an empty set of 
parentheses, whereas in VHDL the parentheses are not included in the function call:
TNOW := now;     --  VHDL call to a function with no parameters
tnow  = now ();  //  C call to a function with no parameters
160

--- Page 180 ---
CHAPTER 10 SUBPROGRAMS
Return Statements
VHDL functions always return a value. There is no type void in VHDL, like there is in C, to signify  
that the function does not return a value. The syntax used to return a value from a function in VHDL is:
 return_statement ::=
return expression ;
Since a function in VHDL always returns a result, this means that the last statement in a function must 
be a return statement. Multiple return statements can be used inside a function declaration, and the  
function will return the value specified by the first return statement that is encountered and then exit.  
For example, the following function declaration will constrain an input value ( DATA) to be within a  
specified  range  of  values  ( LO to  HI,  inclusive),  an  operation  called  saturation in  digital  signal  
processing (also known as clipping):
function SATURATE (DATA : integer; LO : integer; HI : integer) return integer is
begin
  if (DATA < LO) then
    return LO;
  elsif (DATA > HI) then
    return HI;
  end if;
  return DATA;
end function SATURATE;
Examples
Let us look at how functions are declared and called. The following function will compute the familiar  
linear transformation equation Y = M × X + B. The input parameters to the function are the values for X, 
M, and B, and the function returns the computed value for Y based on these inputs:
function LXFORM (X : integer; M : integer; B : integer) return integer is
  variable Y : integer;
begin
  Y := M * X + B;
  return Y;
end function LXFORM;
Note that in this example since the return statement can contain an expression, the function could be  
rewritten as:
function LXFORM (X : integer; M : integer; B : integer) return integer is
begin
  return M * X + B;
end function LXFORM;
Given our declaration for LXFORM, we can call it using the following statement:
A := LXFORM (5, 2, 6);          --  Computes 5 * 2 + 6
Besides being able to pass constants into our function, we can also specify variables, which will pass in 
the value of the variable at the time of the function invocation:
161

--- Page 181 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
A := LXFORM (5, 2, 6);          --  Computes 5 * 2 + 6
A := LXFORM (A, 2, 6);          --  Computes A * 2 + 6
We can also nest the function call to accomplish the same thing as the two lines above:
A := LXFORM (LXFORM (5, 2, 6), 2, 6);
If we wanted to create a more agile version of this function, one in which the M and B parameters take 
on default values  that do not affect the outcome of the computation if they are not specified, the  
function could be modified as follows:
function LXFORM (X : integer; M : integer := 1; B : integer := 0) return integer is
begin
  return M * X + B;
end function LXFORM;
With this declaration, M will be assigned the value of 1 and B will be assigned the value of 0 if they are  
not otherwise specified in the function call. This allows us to perform the following actions:
A := LXFORM (X => 5, B => 7);          --  Computes 5 * 1 + 7 (M defaults to 1)
B := LXFORM (X => 4, M => 3);          --  Computes 4 * 3 + 0 (B defaults to 0)
C := LXFORM (X => 6, M => 2, B => 5);  --  Computes 6 * 2 + 5
D := LXFORM (2);                       --  Computes 2 * 1 + 0
In general, it is always a good idea to use formal parameter mapping when using the default values of  
parameters.  In  fact,  once  a  formal  parameter  mapping  is  used  within  a  function  call,  all  of  the 
remaining parameter mappings must be formal mappings. Thus, the following function call is incorrect 
and will result in a compilation error:
D := LXFORM (X => 2, 3, 7);
As mentioned previously, the interface element can include a designation for whether the parameter is  
treated  as  a  constant (the default)  or  a  signal.  Attempting  to  use a constant  or  variable for  a  
parameter designated as a signal will result in a compilation error:
architecture EXAMPLE of FUNCTIONS is
  function LXFORM (signal X : integer; M : integer; B : integer) return integer is
  begin
    return M * X + B;
  end function LXFORM;
  signal DIN, DOUT : integer;
begin
  ...
  process
    variable X, Y : integer;
  begin
    ...
    DOUT <= LXFORM (DIN, 2, 5);  --  This function call is correct
    Y    := LXFORM (X,   2, 5);  --  SYNTAX ERROR! (X is a variable)
  end process;
  ...
end architecture EXAMPLE;
162


--- Page 182 ---
CHAPTER 10 SUBPROGRAMS
Overloading
VHDL subprograms support the concept of overloading, which is the process of using the same name  
for subprograms with different interface lists. When a subprogram  call is compiled , the types and  
object classes of the interface list used in the function call are matched with the various subprogram  
declarations to select which actual subprogram is called. In VHDL, the types and object classes of the  
interface list are known as the subprogram signature.
For  example,  astute  readers  may  have noticed  that  there  is  no  VHDL equivalent  of the  ternary 
operator (the  ? : operator in C). In order to be able to use a ternary operator, a function must be  
written that emulates the behavior of that operator:
function TERNARY (CONDITON : boolean; IF_TRUE : integer; IF_FALSE : integer)
           return integer is
begin
  if (CONDITON)
    then return IF_TRUE;
    else return IF_FALSE;
  end if;
end function TERNARY;
The user could then use this function to determine the maximum value in an array named VAL:
MAX := VAL (VAL'low);
for I in VAL'low+1 to VAL'high loop
  MAX := TERNARY (VAL (I) > MAX, VAL (I), MAX);
end loop;
The problem  that exists  is that this function is only declared for  integer data types. What if the  
designer wanted to perform the same function on a data type that was  real instead? This is where  
overloading comes in. A second function declaration can be created that perform s the same operation,  
only on real data types, and instead of having to come up with a different name for the function, it too 
can be called TERNARY:
function TERNARY (CONDITON : boolean; IF_TRUE : real; IF_FALSE : real)
           return real is
begin
  if (CONDITON)
    then return IF_TRUE;
    else return IF_FALSE;
  end if;
end function TERNARY;
With these two declarations, the  TERNARY function can be used on both  integer and  real types. 
During compilation, the compiler will look at the data types of the parameters in the function call (the  
signature) to determine which one of the overloaded TERNARY functions is actually called.
Pure versus Impure Functions
If we pass the same input values into a function, we would normally expect that the same result would  
be returned each time that the function is called using those same input values. However, in order for  
this to be true, the function cannot reference any external variables or signals. In VHDL, this is known  
as a pure function. Compilers can take advantage of pure functions by replacing the function call with  
the result of the call if the input parameters are known. This aids in optimization of the code when it is  
163

--- Page 183 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
simulated. Pure functions are the default case in VHDL, and the pure keyword is normally not used in  
the function declaration.
If the function does reference external variables or signals, the return result could potentially change  
with each function call using the same input values. Th e reason for this is that the external variable or 
signal values may change between invocations of the function. This is known as an impure function. In 
VHDL, an impure function requires explicit declaration that it is impure so that the compiler will know 
not to try to optimize the function call.
Our linear transform function,  LXFORM, was written such that the input, slope, and intercept values  
were passed in with each invocation and did not reference any external variables or signals. That  
makes LXFORM a pure function:
function LXFORM (X : integer; M : integer; B : integer) return integer is
begin
  return M * X + B;
end function LXFORM;
If we instead wanted to perform the transform using external signals for the slope and intercept values,  
the function would then be an impure function and would look like:
impure function LXFORM (X : integer) return integer is
begin
  return SLOPE * X + INTERCEPT;
end function LXFORM;
When a function is declared as impure, the compiler relaxes the rules for external access and will allow 
the function to read signals and variables not declared locally within the function. In addition, while a  
pure function does not allow for side effects (the ability to set external signal or variable values), an  
impure function allows it to have the ability to write to any variable that is visible within the scope of  
the calling context. While an  impure function allows the function to read signal values, signals still  
cannot be driven.
For example, if we wanted to track the range of the  X and  Y values that are used during calls to  
LXFORM, we could use a pair of externally declared variables that would store the minimum and  
maximum values that occur for both X and Y:
process
  variable XMIN, YMIN : integer := 0;
  variable XMAX, YMAX : integer := 0;
  impure function LXFORM (X : integer) return integer is
    variable Y : integer := SLOPE * X + INTERCEPT;
  begin
    if (X < XMIN) then XMIN := X; end if;
    if (X > XMAX) then XMAX := X; end if;
    if (Y < YMIN) then YMIN := Y; end if;
    if (Y > YMAX) then YMAX := Y; end if;
    return Y;
  end function LXFORM;
begin
  ...
end process;
164

--- Page 184 ---
CHAPTER 10 SUBPROGRAMS
It is worth noting that the now function in the standard library is an impure function, since it can return 
different values depending on what the simulation time is when the call is made.
Procedures
Whereas functions encapsulate a series of sequential statements that compute and return results, a  
procedure encapsulates a series of sequential statements that provide a certain behavior. There are three 
main differences between procedures and functions:
• Procedures do not return values. Functions return a single value of a stated type.
• Procedure interface lists contain input, output, and inout parameters. Parameters of mode out and 
inout can be used to pass values back to the calling context. Functions can only contain interface 
lists with input parameters.
• Procedures can manipulate time. Procedures have complete read and write access to signals and 
variables declared within the scope of the calling context and can contain wait statements. 
Functions cannot drive signals and cannot contain wait statements.
The purpose of a procedure is to automate a behavioral task that can be invoked multiple times within  
a process or architecture. Procedures are blocking, meaning that the execution of the calling context is  
suspended until the procedure completes. The syntax for the procedure declaration is:
 procedure_declaration ::=
procedure identifier [ ( interface_list ) ] is
{ subprogram_declarative_part }
begin
{ sequential_statement }
end [ procedure ] [ identifier ] ;
 interface_list ::=
interface_element { ; interface_element }
 interface_element ::=
[ constant | variable | signal ]
identifier { , identifier } : [ mode ] type [ := expression ]
 mode ::=
[ in | out | inout ]
The rules governing parameter mode use are exactly the same as those for entity port modes, namely:
• in: Parameter values flow into the procedure, and the value can only be read within the procedure. 
The parameter name can appear only on the right side of a signal or variable assignment.
• out: Parameter values flow out of the procedure, and parameters can only be written to within the 
procedure. The parameter can appear only on the left side of a signal or variable assignment.
165

--- Page 185 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
• inout: Parameter values flow both into and out of the procedure. The value of the parameter can 
both be read and assigned within the procedure and can appear on either side of a signal or 
variable assignment.
The rules describing how procedures are constructed and used can be somewhat complex, so we will  
begin with simple examples and move on toward the more complex aspects of procedures as the  
discussion progresses.
Procedures without parameters: The simplest form of a procedure is one that does not contain an 
interface list. For example, we could compose a procedure that could be used to reset  our logic  
network as follows:
process
  procedure INITIALIZE is begin
    RESET <= '1';
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    RESET <= '0';
  end procedure INITIALIZE;
begin
  INITIALIZE;
  --  Sequential statements
  INITIALIZE;
  --  Sequential statements
end process;
When called, the procedure immediately applies the reset and then waits for the inactive edge (which  
in this case is the falling edge) of the clock to remove it, thereby avoiding races between the clock and 
reset (races in digital logic design occur when there are simultaneous events on two or more flip-flop  
direct action signals—or simultaneous events on any of the indirect action signals and the active clock  
edge—and are almost always bad).
While we could copy and paste the statements contained within the INITIALIZE procedure every time 
we wished to reset the logic, placing the code in a procedure and calling that procedure simplifies the  
task. This is further simplified when we find that we must modify the behavior of our initialization  
procedure. For example, if the INITIALIZE procedure is called just before a falling edge of CLOCK, 
the resultant pulse on RESET could end up being extremely narrow and in fact could end up being only  
one delta cycle wide. If this is not what we intended, the INITIALIZE procedure could be changed to  
apply the reset pulse between two consecutive falling edges of CLOCK, thereby ensuring that the pulse  
is one clock cycle wide:
process
  procedure INITIALIZE is begin
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    RESET <= '1';
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    RESET <= '0';
  end procedure INITIALIZE;
begin
  INITIALIZE;
  --  Sequential statements
  INITIALIZE;
  --  Sequential statements
end process;
166

--- Page 186 ---
CHAPTER 10 SUBPROGRAMS
If we had copied and pasted the initialization procedure throughout the process, we would need to find  
each occurrence of the code and modify each one separately (raising the possibility of missing one or  
more occurrences). By using a procedure we only need to modify the code one time, which guarantees  
that all applications of the initialization procedure will reflect the change.
Procedures  with input parameters :  We next turn our attention to procedures that have input  
parameters. A typical application of a procedure of this type is creating the waveforms necessary to  
write data into a device over a bus interface that contains the address, data, and separate read and write  
enable signals. The following code declares a procedure that writes a specified data value to a specified 
address location over the bus interface:
process
  procedure BUS_WRITE (ADDRESS : natural; DIN : std_logic_vector) is begin
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    ADDR  <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ADDRESS, ADDR'length));
    DATA  <= DIN;
    WRENA <= '1';
    RDENA <= '0';
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    WRENA <= '0';
  end procedure BUS_WRITE;
begin
  --  Sequential statements
  BUS_WRITE (0, x"ABCD");
  BUS_WRITE (1, x"1234");
  --  Sequential statements
end process;
This procedure automates the task of writing data into our device and makes the actual process both  
trivial and easily readable. The waveforms generated by this example are:
Note that the default mode for any interface element in a procedure is in and is usually not specified in 
the procedure declaration if the element is an input.
We  can  extend  our  register  write  procedure  utilizing  overloading  to  create  a  method  of  writing  
natural values into our device when the natural value is more appropriate, as follows:
167


--- Page 187 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
process
  procedure BUS_WRITE (ADDRESS : natural; DIN : std_logic_vector) is begin
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    ADDR  <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ADDRESS, ADDR'length));
    DATA  <= DIN;
    WRENA <= '1';
    RDENA <= '0';
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    WRENA <= '0';
  end procedure BUS_WRITE;
  procedure BUS_WRITE (ADDRESS : natural; DIN : natural) is
    variable VECTOR : std_logic_vector (DATA'range);
  begin
    VECTOR := std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (DIN, DATA'length));
    BUS_WRITE (ADDRESS, VECTOR);
  end procedure BUS_WRITE;
begin
  --  Sequential statements
  BUS_WRITE (0, x"ABCD");
  BUS_WRITE (1,    120 );
  --  Sequential statements
end process;
The first BUS_WRITE procedure shown above is exactly the same as the previous example, but we have 
now added a second overloaded version of the BUS_WRITE procedure that uses a natural data type  
for DIN. Inside this second procedure, the input data value is converted to a std_logic_vector that 
is the same length as DATA, and then the first BUS_WRITE procedure is called. This allows us to write a 
value of 120 instead of having to convert it to the binary equivalent of 120, as shown in the second  
call.
Procedures with output parameters: Since we have a method to write values into our device, it  
follows that we should be able to read values back out of our device and check that the data is correct.  
Procedures cannot return results like functions can, but we can specify a parameter that is instead of  
mode  out that  can be used to read back values. In this case, we must explicitly specify that the  
parameter is of mode out:
process
  variable CHECK : std_logic_vector (DATA'range);
  procedure BUS_READ (ADDRESS : natural; DOUT : out std_logic_vector) is begin
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    ADDR  <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ADDRESS, ADDR'length));
    DATA  <= (others => 'Z');
    WRENA <= '0';
    RDENA <= '1';
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    RDENA <= '0';
    DOUT  := DATA;
  end procedure BUS_READ;
begin
  --  Sequential statements
  BUS_READ (10, CHECK);
  assert (CHECK = x"DEAD") report "Incorrect read value" severity error;
  BUS_READ (11, CHECK);
  assert (CHECK = x"BEEF") report "Incorrect read value" severity error;
  --  Sequential statements
end process;
168

--- Page 188 ---
CHAPTER 10 SUBPROGRAMS
This procedure works by executing a read cycle (driving the address bus to the specified value while  
ensuring that the data bus is driven to the hi-Z state and the read enable input is asserted) and then  
assigning the value of the data bus during the read cycle to the output parameter  DOUT. During  
invocations of the BUS_READ procedure, we assign the variable CHECK to the DOUT interface element  
and can perform a test that the read value is what we expect to see.
The behavior encapsulated by the BUS_READ procedure results in the following waveforms:
Note that in this example we are always checking the result after a read cycle. We can streamline this  
process even further by modifying the procedure to include an input named EXPECT that is the value  
we expect to read back from the device and then perform the assertion test within the procedure:
process
  procedure BUS_READ (ADDRESS : natural; EXPECT : std_logic_vector) is begin
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    ADDR  <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ADDRESS, ADDR'length));
    DATA  <= (others => 'Z');
    WRENA <= '0';
    RDENA <= '1';
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    RDENA <= '0';
    assert (DATA = EXPECT) report "Incorrect read value" severity error;
  end procedure BUS_READ;
begin
  --  Sequential statements
  BUS_READ (10, x"DEAD");  --  Read and compare to x"DEAD"
  BUS_READ (11, x"BEEF");  --  Read but compare to x"BEEF"
  --  Sequential statements
end process;
This simplifies the task of reading a value from the device and comparing the value to an expected  
result.
Return Statements
Procedures  can  be  terminated  early  by  using  the  return statement.  However,  unlike  the  return 
statement in a function declaration, which includes an expression that is to be returned, the return 
statement in a procedure cannot include an expression, since procedures do not return values. We can  
take advantage of the return statement to extend our bus read procedure to make the read comparison  
optional. This is accomplished by assigning a default unknown value to EXPECT and then using that  
default state in a test to see if we want to execute the assert statement:
169


--- Page 189 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
process
  procedure BUS_READ (ADDRESS : natural; EXPECT : std_logic_vector := "X") is begin
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    ADDR  <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ADDRESS, ADDR'length));
    DATA  <= (others => 'Z');
    WRENA <= '0';
    RDENA <= '1';
    wait until CLOCK = '0';
    RDENA <= '0';
    if (Is_X (EXPECT)) then
      return;
    end if;
    assert (DATA = EXPECT) report "Incorrect read value" severity error;
  end procedure BUS_READ;
begin
  --  Sequential statements
  BUS_READ (0);            --  Read but do not compare the read value
  BUS_READ (10, x"DEAD");  --  Read and compare to x"DEAD"
  BUS_READ (11, x"BEEF");  --  Read and compare to x"BEEF"
  --  Sequential statements
end process;
This procedure provides us with a very simple yet powerful way of executing device read cycles and  
optionally checking the results of the read.
Parameter Passing Details
Before we dive further into the more complicated features provided by procedures, we will need to  
describe how the parameters of processes are formally treated. To do this, we first must review how  
parameters are passed into and out of functions in traditional programming languages. As you should  
have been taught when studying a language such as C, there are two methods that are used to pass  
parameter values between the function and the calling context:
• Pass by value: The value of the variable is passed into the function and stored in a temporary 
location. The value of the internal variable can be changed within the function, but it is modifying 
a copy of the variable; the value of the actual variable in the calling context is not changed.
• Pass by reference: The memory address of the variable is passed into the function, which is 
referred to as a pointer to the variable. The value of the internal variable can be changed within the 
function, but it is now modifying the actual variable accessed via the pointer, and the value of the 
actual variable in the calling context will be changed. This is how functions in C pass results back 
to the calling context.
This can best be explained by examining the strncpy library function in C, which copies a specified  
number of characters from one string to another. The function prototype is defined as:
char *strncpy (char *str1, const char *str2, size_t n);
When a call is made to the strncpy library function such as:
strncpy (&dest, &src, len);
the following action takes place:
170

--- Page 190 ---
CHAPTER 10 SUBPROGRAMS
• The memory address of dest is pushed onto the stack (pass by reference).
• The memory address of src is pushed onto the stack (pass by reference).
• The value of len is pushed onto the stack (pass by value).
While the strncpy library function may decrement n as part of the control loop used to copy each  
character from  the source to the destination, the value of  len in the calling context will not be  
modified. However, since pointers have been passed into the function for the source and destination  
strings (the memory addresses where the source and destination strings are located), the actual value of  
the destination string will be modified.
VHDL subprograms work in the same way. Parameters of mode in pass the current value of a signal or 
variable into a local temporary copy of the variable inside the subprograms. The value of the signal or  
variable in the calling context will not be modified.  This is true whether the  object  class of the  
parameter is  constant or  variable. In  fact,  the syntax  rules  of VHDL subprograms dictate that  
parameters of mode  in cannot be used as the target of a signal or variable assignment within the  
subprograms just to reinforce that point. Parameters of mode out and inout, which are only allowed  
with procedures, pass a reference—a pointer if you’d like—of the signal or variable into the procedure. 
Any modifications that are made inside the procedure are made to the signal or variable associated  
with the parameter in the calling context. The user does not need to keep track of pointers for these  
cases; VHDL handles that behind the scenes. It should be noted that the default object class for these 
two modes are variable.
Signal Parameters
Using the  object class  signal in a procedure declaration muddies the waters a bit. Consider what  
would happen if a signal is passed into a procedure, and the procedure executes a wait statement as  
part of its sequence of statements. We would expect that any external changes that occur to the signal  
would be reflected when the referenced signal is read after the wait is satisfied. Using the object class  
signal on a parameter of mode in passes a reference to the signal into the procedure. This eliminates  
the problem of pass by value, where the signal value at the time of procedure invocation  is used, but  
this may not reflect the true value of the signal when it is used at a later time in a signal or variable  
assignment statement.
In the case where an object class of signal uses the parameter mode is out, a reference to the signal  
driver is passed into the procedure. Signal assignments are scheduled on the calling driver of the actual  
signal referenced by the parameter, just as if they were made outside of the procedure. In other words,  
any changes made to the signal within the procedure are reflected on the signal in the calling context.
Finally, in the case where an object class of signal uses the parameter mode inout, a reference to both 
the signal (as with mode in) and the signal driver (as with mode out) are passed into the procedure.  
Reading  the  value  of  the  signal  within  the  procedure  will  return  the  value  on  the actual  signal  
referenced in the procedure call at that point in time, and assignments will be scheduled on the calling  
driver of the actual signal referenced by the parameter, again as if they were made outside of the  
procedure.
171

--- Page 191 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Confused? Procedures were designed to be extremely flexible, but with flexibility comes complexity.  
Fortunately,  the typical  use  cases  for  procedures  rarely  delve into  the nitty-gritty  details  of how  
parameters are passed and how signals modify the behavior of parameter passing. For the most part,  
you will almost always use interface elements of mode in, where a copy of the value at the time of  
invocation is used within the procedure. Occasionally you may find yourself needing to use interface  
elements with a mode of out or inout, which you can treat as modifying the signal or variable that is  
connected to the parameter in the procedure call. Cases where you need to use the object class signal 
are quite rate. It is good to understand how the  signal object class modifies the behavior of the  
procedure call, but for the most part this behavior is only used in special cases.
The table titled “Object Class” on the next page summarizes how procedures treat and process the  
various interface mode and object class parameters that can be passed into and out of the procedure.
Concurrent Procedure Calls
Procedures, like functions, can be called either inside or outside a process but within the concurrent  
statement confines of the architecture. Concurrent procedure calls are made using signals, since signals 
are the only means of connecting concurrent statements together within an architecture. The procedure  
call creates an implicit sensitivity list that contains all of the signals used as input parameters in the  
concurrent procedure call.
For example, we could create a procedure that monitors a referenced signal and reports errors when the 
signal value is an unknown:
architecture EXAMPLE of PROCEDURES is
  procedure MONITOR_X (signal TARGET : std_logic) is begin
    assert (TARGET = '0' or TARGET = '1')
      report "Unexpected unknown on signal" severity error;
  end procedure MONITOR_X;
  ...
begin
  --  Concurrent statements
  MONITOR_X (SIG_A);
  MONITOR_X (SIG_B);
  --  Concurrent statements
end architecture EXAMPLE;
How the two concurrent calls to MONITOR_X are implemented is:
process (SIG_A) begin
  MONITOR_X (SIG_A);
end process;
process (SIG_B) begin
  MONITOR_X (SIG_B);
end process;
Again, the designer could have created a process for each signal that is to be monitored, but placing an  
assertion inside the procedure simplifies  the task and  makes  changes easier as well. Indeed, this  
example will raise an error at time 0  nS for each signal, since the signals are initialized to a value of  
'U' at the start of the simulation. While this error is understandable and can be ignored, it may be  
confusing to the casual  user,  who may not understand  that it can be ignored. To avoid this, the  
MONITOR_X procedure can be changed to:

procedure MONITOR_X (signal TARGET : std_logic) is begin
  if (now /= 0 nS) then
    assert (TARGET = '0' or TARGET = '1')
      report "Unexpected unknown on signal" severity error;
  end if;
end procedure MONITOR_X;
which will only check the signal state after the simulation initialization has completed. Encapsulating  
this in a procedure ensures that each invocation of the concurrent procedure call will also get this  
update, making the task of making changes more robust.
Note that the  signal object class designation is not needed for the  TARGET parameter. Because the  
instantiation of the procedure is a concurrent call, the input must be a signal ( remember, only signals 
are allowed within architectures), and the  object class is automatically promoted to the signal class.  
While the signal designation is not needed, it’s a good idea to include it in procedures that are invoked 
as concurrent procedure calls to make clear what is happening.
Procedures as Functions
Functions must always return a value. In addition, the calling context cannot ignore the return value of  
a function as in C, where the compiler will silently discard a return value of a function if it is not used.  
If the user wishes to create a function without a return value, then a procedure must be used since it  
does  not  return  a  value.  You  may  have  noted  that  if  the  statements  within  a  procedure  never  
manipulates time via a  wait statement, it will return without consuming any time and in essence  
performs the same action as a function. We can take advantage of this behavior to write a procedure as 
a function that doesn’t return a value.
An example of using a procedure as a function is writing a subprogram that takes a string and writes it  
to the screen to provide status messages to the user during a long simulation. Creating this subprogram  
as a function presents some problems. First, since the function needs to return a result, this raises the  
question “what do we return?”  Second, the calling context must do something with this return value,  
such as assigning it to a variable—the syntax rules of VHDL prohibit us from calling a function and  
ignoring the result.
Writing this subprogram as a procedure solves these problems. The procedure does not return a value,  
and the calling context does not need to do anything special to deal with a returned value. The PUTS 
procedure in the following example is basically a function that does not return a value:
--------------------------------
procedure PUTS (MSG : string) is
--------------------------------
  variable  LBFR : line;
begin
  write     (LBFR,   MSG);
  writeline (OUTPUT, LBFR);
end procedure PUTS;
Calls to the PUTS procedure can be simply done as follows:
174

--- Page 194 ---
CHAPTER 10 SUBPROGRAMS
--  Sequential statements
PUTS ("Initializing the internal registers");
--  Sequential statements
Summary
In VHDL, subprograms are used to define a sequence of instructions that can be invoked many times  
via calls to the subprogram. Subprograms are mainly used  to efficiently  code repetitive tasks  or  
computations that can be written one time and invoked as many times as necessary. There are two  
types of subprograms:  functions, which encapsulate a series of sequential statements that compute  
and return a single result, and  procedures, which encapsulate a series of sequential statements that  
provide a particular behavior.
Subprogram references can be used in concurrent and sequential statements. Subprograms can be used  
in both behavioral and synthesizable code; however, while functions do appear in both modeling styles, 
procedures are mainly only found in behavioral code.
This chapter has admittedly been extremely detailed and technical. Subprograms were designed to be  
extremely flexible, but with flexibility comes complexity. If the concepts described in this chapter  
remain a bit fuzzy, it would be a good idea to reread the chapter to make sure you understand all of the  
concepts that have been presented.
175

--- Page 195 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL

CHAPTER 11
SIMULATION AND TEST BENCHES
Simulation has been the most important tool used in the development of digital logic since the very  
early days of digital design. Simulation involves applying i nput stimuli to a logic design, whether it be 
a netlist or a high-level functional model using a hardware description language, and the results of the  
simulation are inspected and verified. If the results are not as expected, errors in the logic are identified 
and corrected, and the simulation is rerun. This test-analyze-correct cycle repeats until the results are  
correct. This chapter covers how models in VHDL are simulated and introduces the concept of the test 
bench, a VHDL behavioral model that is contained within a separate text file that is used to apply input 
signals and verify the results of the simulation.
Simulation
Simulation,  simply put, is the execution of a VHDL model in a software environment. It  does not  
exercise the circuit in actual hardware but instead exercises the model of the hardware on a computer  
using  a  third-party  software  tool.  Examples  of  simulation  software  tools  include ModelSim,  
QuestaSim, XSim, and VCS. Generally VHDL simulator tools are not free, but there are some free  
simulation tools that are available and work well. The digital design flow diagram on page 34 shows 
where simulation fits into the overall design process.
Why would the designer want to simulate? Certainly, simulation is the preferred method used to verify 
the functional  correctness  of  your design.  Errors  can  be  detected  much  more cost-efficiently  via  
simulation than tracking down bugs in the lab using actual hardware. With simulation, you have  
complete control over all of the input signals of your design and can easily construct “what if” and  
“corner cases,” which may be difficult to perform in a lab setting. You also have complete visibility of  
all the signals within the design, whereas in the lab the only signals that are visible are the inputs and  
outputs of the hardware device. Finally, you can perform regression testing where you make sure that  
changes to the VHDL model do not break the existing functionality of the design.
Contrast the hardware simulation process with that of the software design process. Debugging software 
is usually done in the lab using an emulator. The software designer uses break points to halt execution,  
at which point variables and registers can be inspected and stack traces can be used to find how the  
software, through the chain of function calls, got to the instruction where the break point occurred.  
While some processor integrated design environment (IDE) tools have built-in simulation tools, most  
use  proprietary  languages  and  methods  to  stimulate  the  processor  inputs,  which  results  in  the  
simulation not being portable between processors from different manufacturers.
177

--- Page 197 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
VHDL simulators, on the other hand, use a test bench file that is written in VHDL to stimulate and  
verify the functionality of the circuit. Performing a logic simulation is the equivalent of running an  
emulator on your logic. You can run the simulation until a certain event occurs and inspect the internal  
signals of the design. Because the test bench is written in VHDL, it is portable between multiple  
simulation tools and logic device vendors. This is a huge advantage that logic design has over software  
design.
Besides the cost advantage, the other reason why simulating a design is important is that simulation  
equals reality: if the simulation results aren’t correct, the circuit will not function correctly either. This  
is not to say that if the simulation works, the circuit will as well. It is very easy to create a simulation  
that doesn’t test everything in the circuit or does not drive the inputs in the same manner as the circuit  
will see in real life. Having a correct simulation is as important as having a functionally correct circuit.
In general, the characteristics of a “correct” simulation are:
• Standalone. The simulation relies only on the test bench and the circuit to generate the results.
• Comprehensive. The simulation exercises all of the functionality of the circuit.
• Self-checking. The simulation automatically checks the output response of the circuit and 
provides the user with error messages.
Another reason why a circuit may not behave the same as the simulation is that VHDL is a very  
flexible language, and it is quite easy to write code that does not synthesize correctly into logic gates. It 
is imperative that the designer stick with well-documented and -supported RTL-style constructs when  
writing the VHDL component model. These constructs include:
• Using concurrent signal assignments to implement Boolean logic gates,
• Using properly constructed processes to implement flip-flops,
• Avoiding the use of variables within processes unless absolutely necessary, and
• Using either the rising or falling clock edge for all of the flip-flops in the design but not both.
Simulation Phases
The process of performing a logic simulation is formally described in the VHDL standard and consists  
of four distinct phases, which are discussed below:
Analysis. The source code is compiled and stored in a library. Each VHDL source file is examined  
separately for syntax errors, and if there are no errors, an intermediate representation of the code is  
stored in a library. Each design unit is stored separately in the library, and the form of the intermediate  
representation in the library is tool-dependent. Analysis is the software equivalent of compiling source  
code into object code. Designers often refer to this phase as “ compiling the code” instead of the formal 
term analysis.
Elaboration. The simulation model is created by flattening the design into a single unified executable  
model. The simulator flattens the design by replacing each component instantiation with the code in its  
architecture,  along  with  performing  optimizations  to  the  executable  model.  The  final  elaborated  
simulation model is a network of concurrent processes connected via signals. This is the software  
equivalent of linking the object code into an executable image.
178

--- Page 198 ---
CHAPTER 11 SIMULATION AND TEST BENCHES
Initialization. Initialization occurs when the simulation begins and consists of the following steps.  
First, the simulation time is set to 0. Then, all signals are assigned an initial value, which is equal to the  
leftmost  value  for  each  signal  type.  Thus,  for  integer signals,  the  initialization  value  is  
−2147483647. For natural signals, the initialization value is 0. For real signals, the initialization  
value is −1.8×10308. For std_logic signals, the initialization value is 'U', which is how it is used to  
represent a signal that has not been assigned another value in the logic network. Finally, all of the  
processes are executed a single time. Processes will execute until either the last statement in a process  
with  a  sensitivity  list  is  encountered  or  a  wait statement  is  encountered  for  processes  without  
sensitivity lists.
Execution. The simulation model is evaluated using the event-driven simulation cycle discussed in  
chapter 2. The simulation will automatically end when there are no more events to process or can be  
run until a specific time if it is specified by the user via the simulator tool command prompt.
Schematically, the four phases of simulation look like:
Each phase of the simulation process must be completed successfully before the next phase can begin.  
Thus,  the  model  cannot  be  elaborated  until  all  of  the  design  units  are  successfully  analyzed.  
Furthermore, initialization and execution of the model cannot occur until after it has been successfully  
elaborated without error.
Test Benches
Simulation of a VHDL model cannot occur on its own; there needs to be a way to stimulate the inputs  
in order to demonstrate the functionality of the model. A separate text file, called a test bench, is used 
to provide the input stimulus to the logic of the component being tested. The term test bench comes 
from the days when a circuit would be tested in the lab on a bench that contained various pieces of test  
equipment such as meters, oscilloscopes, logic analyzers, and so forth.
The test bench contains code that provides the following:
179
LIBRARY
SIMULATION
MODEL
ANALYSIS ELABORATION
INITIALIZATION EXECUTION
SIMULATION
MODEL
SIMULATION
MODEL

--- Page 199 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
• It instantiates the Unit Under Test (UUT).
• It applies input stimuli to the UUT.
• It computes the expected outputs of the UUT based on the input stimuli.
• It compares the actual results of the UUT to the expected results.
• It prints out error messages when the results to not agree.
A test bench is written using the behavioral style of VHDL, which is usually much easier to compose  
and visualize. The test bench should be used to verify the system requirements along with any other  
tests that the designer deems necessary in order to verify the functional correctness of the UUT.
The basic structure of a test bench is shown in the following block diagram, where each box represents 
a process statement in the test bench:
As the diagram shows, a test bench is made up of multiple processes, each of which performs a simple  
and well-defined function. These individual functions are discussed in the following sections.
Test Bench Control
Ideally, there should only be one process in the test bench that is used as the master control for the rest  
of the processes in the test bench. This is the purpose of the top block in the diagram, named  Test 
Bench Control. Furthermore, simulations can run for a very long time (potentially forever) unless  
there is a provision to terminate the simulation when all of the tests have been completed. The Test 
Bench  Control block  determines  when  the  simulation  is  done  and  provides  a  way  to  stop  the  
simulation.
180
