At this point, we need to determine what test strategy we will use for our test bench. One possible  
method  would  be  to  create  an  exhaustive  directed  test,  one  that  tests  all  of  the  possible  input  
combinations for our UUT:
217

--- Page 237 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process
    constant  MAX : natural := 2 ** BITS – 1;
  begin
    A   <= (others => '0');
    B   <= (others => '0');
    CIN <= '0';
    wait for 2 * TCLK;
    
    wait until CLK = '1';
    for CDATA in 0 to 1 loop
      for ADATA in 0 to MAX loop
        A <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ADATA, BITS));
        for BDATA in 0 to MAX loop
          B <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (BDATA, BITS));
          wait until CLK = '1';
        end loop;
      end loop;
      CIN <= '1';
    end loop;
    
    DONE <= TRUE;
    wait;
  end process;
Although this seems like a reasonable approach to our test bench strategy, there is a problem with this  
method. If we run our simulation using different values for the generic BITS, we will discover that the 
time it takes to complete the simulation becomes unreasonable for larger values of BITS:
BITS Test Time
8 0:00.7
10 0:09.1
12 2:35
16 11:02:17 (est)
While the test times for 8- and 10-bit data path widths are reasonable, when the setting for BITS is 12 
or higher, the test time becomes unrealistic. This is due to the fact that the number of input test vectors  
using an exhaustive directed test is 2 × (2BITS)2. This may be okay, since it is reasonable to assume that  
because the ADDER circuit works with data path widths of 8 and 10, it will work with any setting for  
BITS. This shows one advantage of creating a generic component: you can simulate with a reasonable  
set of parameter settings and, by extension, expect it to work with any set of parameters. If we continue 
to use this test strategy, it would be nice to warn the user that the simulation time will be quite long if  
the BITS selection is large. We can declare the PUTS procedure we developed on page 174 and use it to 
warn the user:
218

--- Page 238 ---
CHAPTER 13 TEST BENCH CASE STUDIES
---------------------------
architecture ADDER of TB is
---------------------------
  ...
  --------------------------------
  procedure PUTS (MSG : string) is
  --------------------------------
    variable  LBFR : line;
  begin
    write     (LBFR,   MSG);
    writeline (OUTPUT, LBFR);
  end procedure PUTS;
begin
  ...
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process
    constant  MAX : natural := 2 ** BITS – 1;
  begin
    A   <= (others => '0');
    B   <= (others => '0');
    CIN <= '0';
    wait for 2 * TCLK;
    if (BITS > 11) then
      PUTS ("Due to the number of BITS, this simulation will take a long time");
    end if;
    ...
  end process;
Although we have every reason to believe that our adder works with any value for  BITS, a better  
method would be to adopt a constrained random test strategy. One aspect of this test strategy that we  
need to be aware of is that as the selection for the number of BITS increases, we should extend the test  
by increasing the number of distinct input vectors we test with. This is accomplished by changing the  
input data driver to the following:
219

--- Page 239 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process
    variable  LFSR : std_logic_vector (1 to 23) := "011" & x"DECAF";
    impure function RANDOM (SIZE : natural) return std_logic_vector is
      variable RTN : std_logic_vector (1 to SIZE);
    begin
      for I in 1 to SIZE loop
        LFSR    := (LFSR (18) xor LFSR (23)) & LFSR (1 to 22);
        RTN (I) :=  LFSR (1);
      end loop;
      return RTN;
    end function RANDOM;
  begin
    A   <= (others => '0');
    B   <= (others => '0');
    CIN <= '0';
    wait for 2 * TCLK;
    wait until CLK = '1';
    for I in 1 to 2 ** BITS loop
      A   <= RANDOM (BITS);
      B   <= RANDOM (BITS);
      CIN <= RANDOM (1)(1);  --  Select bit 1 of the returned vector
      wait until CLK = '1';
    end loop;
    DONE <= TRUE;
    wait;
  end process;
Using this input data driver, the time it takes to complete the simulation becomes:
BITS Test Time
8 0:00.1
10 0:00.11
12 0:00:15
16 0:01.2
and is significantly more reasonable. Our test length is now 2BITS instead of  2 × (2BITS)2.
Next, we need to create our output monitor so that our test bench is self-checking. The following code  
will compute the expected value of our circuit output, wait until the next rising clock edge, and verify  
that the SUM and COUT outputs are correct via assert statements:
220

--- Page 240 ---
CHAPTER 13 TEST BENCH CASE STUDIES
-----------------------
--  Output Data Monitor
-----------------------
  process
    constant MODULO : natural := 2 ** BITS;
    variable EXPECT : natural := 0;
  begin
    wait until CLK = '1';
--  Test with previous input values
    assert (SUM = std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (EXPECT mod MODULO, BITS)))
      report "Incorrect SUM output" severity error;
    if (EXPECT < MODULO)
      then assert (COUT = '0') report "Incorrect COUT value" severity error;
      else assert (COUT = '1') report "Incorrect COUT value" severity error;
    end if;
--  Compute next expected output value
    EXPECT := To_Integer (unsigned (A)) + To_Integer (unsigned (B));
    if (CIN = '1') then
      EXPECT := EXPECT + 1;
    end if;
  end process;
Finally, we need to make sure that our test bench and circuit work properly by verifying that the output  
is correct through inspection of the waveform results. We cannot solely rely on our belief that the test  
bench is correct; this visual inspection is an important step that will help make sure that both are  
correct:
  83 + 192 =   19 (+ COUT)
  80 + 175 = 225
  41 +   21 =   62
108 +   46 + CIN = 155
159 + 167 + CIN =   71 (+ COUT)
This case study shows that although a reasonable test strategy may be created for a circuit, it can  
become in fact unreasonable if the circuit parameters are changed to a set of values that extend the test  
time well beyond what a designer would expect. In general, constrained random testing is superior to  
directed testing, especially when an exhaustive set of input values are used. The constrained random  
testing can be augmented, if desired, with several directed test input values to make sure that the full  
221


--- Page 241 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
range of output values are correct, much like was done in chapter 12 with the test bench for the FIR4  
circuit. In this case with unsigned input data values, we could modify the input data driver to test the  
following conditions prior to performing the constrained random tests:
  procedure TEST (ADATA, BDATA : natural; CDATA : std_logic) is begin
    wait until CLK = '1';
    A   <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (ADATA, BITS));
    B   <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (BDATA, BITS));
    CIN <= CDATA;
  end procedure TEST;
begin
  ...
--  Directed testing
  TEST (  0,   0, '0');  --  Check 0 + 0 without carry in
  TEST (  0,   0, '1');  --  Check 0 + 0 with carry in
  TEST (MAX, MAX, '0');  --  Check MAX + MAX without carry in
  TEST (MAX, MAX, '1');  --  Check MAX + MAX with carry in
--  Constrained random testing
  ...
Adding these four test cases  does not significantly increase our test time but does give us more  
assurance that the circuit is performing properly. Note also that creating a procedure named  TEST 
makes the directed portion of the test bench much easier to read and understand.
Engine Management System
For our second example, we will write a test bench for the engine management system logic that was  
developed starting on page 152. It would be a good idea review the requirements and the development

The entity declaration for this circuit is:

As we did with the previous example, we need to determine what test strategy we will use for our test  
bench. A simple way to perform an initial set of tests on this circuit would be to generate eight identical 
samples of 0, then 1, then 2, etc. and visually check that the output follows the input sample values (the 
average of eight 0 samples is 0, the average of eight 1 samples is 1, and so on). The following is the  
input data driver that would accomplish this:
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process begin
    REQUEST <= '0';
    VALID   <= '0';
    DATA    <= (others => '0');
    wait until RESET = '0';
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
    for I in 0 to 255 loop
      wait until CLOCK = '1';  REQUEST <= '1';
      wait until CLOCK = '1';  REQUEST <= '0';
      DATA <= std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (I, 8));
      for J in 1 to 8 loop
        wait until CLOCK = '1';  VALID <= '1';
        wait until CLOCK = '1';  VALID <= '0';
      end loop;
      
      wait until READY = '1' for 100 * TCLK;
      if (READY = '0') then
        report "READY did not assert" severity error;
        exit;
      end if;        
    end loop;
    DONE <= TRUE;
    wait;
  end process;
224

--- Page 244 ---
CHAPTER 13 TEST BENCH CASE STUDIES
This will produce the following waveforms at the start of the simulation:
Notice that the output value is 0, 1, 2, etc. whenever the READY output is '1', which indicates that our 
circuit appears to be working properly.
At this point, given that we have visual confirmation that the circuit is working to our expectations, our 
next step should be to add an output monitor to make the test bench self-checking:
-----------------------
--  Output Data Monitor
-----------------------
  process
    variable  SUM : natural;
  begin
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
    if (REQUEST = '1') then   --  Initialize SUM upon REQUEST
      SUM := 0;
    elsif (VALID = '1') then  --  Accumulate the DATA samples on VALID
      SUM := SUM + To_Integer (unsigned (DATA));
    elsif (READY = '1') then  --  Check the AVG output against the expected result
      SUM := SUM / 8;
      assert (AVG = std_logic_vector (To_Unsigned (SUM, AVG'length)))
        report "Incorrect AVG value" severity error;
    end if;
  end process;
Note that the output monitor does not depend on any a priori knowledge of what the input driver data  
sequence is, but instead depends solely on the input value each time VALID = '1'. This makes the data 
output monitor process much more versatile. After this process has been written, the simulation can be  
rerun to confirm that the output monitor code is correct and the simulation does not report any errors.
Although our simulation results look good, we are not really fully testing all of the logic within the  
circuit. A better approach, after confirming that the circuit appears in working order, is to modify the  
input driver to a constrained random test, as in the following:
225


--- Page 245 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process
    variable  LFSR : std_logic_vector (1 to 23) := "011" & x"DECAF";
    impure function RANDOM (SIZE : natural) return std_logic_vector is
      variable RTN : std_logic_vector (1 to SIZE);
    begin
      for I in 1 to SIZE loop
        LFSR    := (LFSR (18) xor LFSR (23)) & LFSR (1 to 22);
        RTN (I) :=  LFSR (1);
      end loop;
      return RTN;
    end function RANDOM;
  begin
    REQUEST <= '0';
    VALID   <= '0';
    DATA    <= (others => '0');
    wait until RESET = '0';
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
    for I in 0 to 255 loop
      wait until CLOCK = '1';  REQUEST <= '1';
      wait until CLOCK = '1';  REQUEST <= '0';
      for J in 1 to 8 loop
        wait until CLOCK = '1';  VALID <= '1';  DATA <= RANDOM (DATA'length);
        wait until CLOCK = '1';  VALID <= '0';
      end loop;
      
      wait until READY = '1' for 100 * TCLK;
      if (READY = '0') then 
        report "READY did not assert" severity error;
        exit;
      end if;
    end loop;
  
    DONE <= TRUE;
    wait;
  end process;
After  making  this  change  and  resimulating  the  design,  the  waveforms  at  the  beginning  of  the  
simulation have changed to the following:
Again, we hand-confirm that the simulation and circuit appear to be working by checking the first

(  83 + 192 +   40 +   87 +   81 + 138 +   69 + 133) / 8 = 102.875
(217 + 250 + 125 + 205 + 107 + 135 + 192 +   53) / 8 = 160.625
(Note: our circuit truncates the output, which will generate 102 when the average is 102.875)
At this point, do we declare victory and consider our testing complete? Unfortunately, no. Notice that  
we have created a test bench where the timing of the REQUEST and VALID inputs are very orderly. We 
need to make sure that the circuit works under conditions where the timing of these two signals is  
unexpected, such as when  REQUEST occurs too quickly (i.e., before  READY has asserted), and we  
also  need  to  make  sure  that  the  circuit  produces  the  correct  result  when  VALID is  asserted  on 
consecutive clock cycles.
We will tackle the second problem first. We create a separate process for  VALID using constrained  
random timing, which will generate a randomized series of 1s and 0s, as follows:
----------------
--  VALID Driver
----------------
  process
    variable  LFSR : std_logic_vector (1 to 10) := "1010101010";
  begin
    VALID <=  LFSR (1);
    LFSR  := (LFSR (7) xor LFSR (10)) & LFSR (1 to 9);
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
  end process;
Because VALID is being driven inside its own process, we need to remove VALID from the main input  
driver:
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process
    variable  LFSR : std_logic_vector (1 to 23) := "011" & x"DECAF";
    impure function RANDOM (SIZE : natural) return std_logic_vector is
      variable RTN : std_logic_vector (1 to SIZE);
    begin
      for I in 1 to SIZE loop
        LFSR    := (LFSR (18) xor LFSR (23)) & LFSR (1 to 22);
        RTN (I) :=  LFSR (1);
      end loop;
      return RTN;
    end function RANDOM;
  begin
    REQUEST <= '0';
    DATA    <= (others => '0');
    wait until RESET = '0';
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
227


--- Page 247 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
    for I in 0 to 255 loop
      wait until CLOCK = '1';  REQUEST <= '1';
      wait until CLOCK = '1';  REQUEST <= '0';
      for J in 1 to 8 loop
        loop
          wait until CLOCK = '1';
          exit when  VALID = '1';
        end loop;
        DATA <= RANDOM (DATA'length);
      end loop;
      
      wait until READY = '1' for 100 * TCLK;
      if (READY = '0') then 
        report "READY did not assert" severity error;
        exit;
      end if;
    end loop;
  
    DONE <= TRUE;
    wait;
  end process;
Instead of forcing  VALID, we create a loop that monitors  VALID on each rising clock edge, which  
indicates that the current value on DATA has been clocked into the circuit. We can then generate a new  
random data value and repeat this process eight times, one for each sample in the output computation.  
This construct, namely:
loop    --  Exit on a rising clock edge when VALID = '1'
  wait until CLOCK = '1';
  exit when  VALID = '1';
end loop;
DATA <= RANDOM (DATA'length);
is a common practice in test bench construction and one that should be committed to memory. Recall 
this is the equivalent of:
do {
  wait until CLOCK = '1';
} while (VALID = '0');
in C.
After changing the timing on the VALID signal, the waveforms at the beginning of the simulation have  
changed to the following:
Again, we hand-confirm that the simulation and circuit appear to be working by checking the first  
several input data sequences:
228


--- Page 248 ---
CHAPTER 13 TEST BENCH CASE STUDIES
(    0 +   83 + 192 +   40 +   87 + 138 +   69 +   77) / 8 =   85.75
(133 + 217 + 250 + 125 + 205 + 107 + 136 + 192) / 8 = 170.625
While this  appears  correct,  you may  notice from  the second  set of input  data values  that if the  
REQUEST and  VALID inputs  occur simultaneously,  both  the data  value and  the  VALID input  are  
ignored: that is why in the second set of values, there are actually nine VALID clock cycles before the  
output is generated. This has the effect of computing the average over the next eight valid input values, 
as shown in the following expanded waveform:
Is this okay? In general, it depends on what you and the systems engineer agree to for this case. If it is  
determined to be okay, then the circuit is working according to expectations. If not, then the control  
logic  of  the  circuit  will  need  to  be  modified  such  that  when  REQUEST and  VALID occur  
simultaneously, that initial valid input value is included in the next output average.
Assuming that after discussing this particular corner case with the systems engineer the conclusion is  
that  this  is  not  a problem,  the final  step  in  testing  our circuit  is  randomizing  the timing  of the  
REQUEST input. We begin by noting that we would like our REQUEST input to run at a much slower  
rate than the data VALID input, since the normal operation of t he system is to generate a REQUEST 
followed by eight  VALID samples. Since we are using an LFSR to generate our randomized  VALID 
bitstream,  it  has  a  1:2  probability  of being  a  '1'.  This  means  that  we  would  like  to  have  our 
REQUEST input have a 1:16 probability of being a '1' so the relationship, on average, of eight VALID 
assertions per  REQUEST is maintained. We can create our desired 1:16 probability if we perform a  
Boolean AND on four randomized bits from an LFSR ( REQUEST will be set to a '1' only when the  
four bits equal "1111" and will be set to '0' for the remaining 15 possible bit combinations).
Since we already have a process that we are using to generate the random VALID bitstream, we can  
modify it to add the randomized REQUEST generator:
------------------------
--  REQUEST/VALID Driver
------------------------
  process
    variable  LFSR : std_logic_vector (1 to 10) := "1010101010";
  begin
    REQUEST <= LFSR (1) and LFSR (2) and LFSR (3) and LFSR (4); -- 1:16 probability
    VALID   <= LFSR (5);                                        -- 1:2  probability
    for I in 1 to 5 loop
      LFSR := (LFSR (7) xor LFSR (10)) & LFSR (1 to 9);
    end loop;
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
  end process;
229
1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8

--- Page 249 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Note that the process was modified to “spin” the LFSR five times after each signal assignment in order  
to generate five fresh random bits and thus maintain independence in the two random bitstreams. We  
also do a quick check to determine that 5 is not a factor of the LFSR length 1,023—it’s not—to make  
sure we will create a maximal length sequence when generating five bits at a time.
We next remove REQUEST from the main input data driver process:
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process
    variable  LFSR : std_logic_vector (1 to 23) := "011" & x"DECAF";
    impure function RANDOM (SIZE : natural) return std_logic_vector is
      variable RTN : std_logic_vector (1 to SIZE);
    begin
      for I in 1 to SIZE loop
        LFSR    := (LFSR (18) xor LFSR (23)) & LFSR (1 to 22);
        RTN (I) :=  LFSR (1);
      end loop;
      return RTN;
    end function RANDOM;
  begin
    DATA <= (others => '0');
    wait until RESET = '0';
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
    for I in 0 to 255 loop
      for J in 1 to 8 loop
        loop
          wait until CLOCK = '1';
          exit when  VALID = '1';
        end loop;
        DATA <= RANDOM (DATA'length);
      end loop;
    end loop;
  
    DONE <= TRUE;
    wait;
  end process;
Note how much simpler the form of the input data driver has become after the removal of REQUEST 
and  VALID. This shows how the individual processes within a test bench can become quite easy to  
write if you break up the task into multiple smaller, more manageable processes.
The waveforms at the beginning of the simulation have changed to the following:
230


--- Page 250 ---
CHAPTER 13 TEST BENCH CASE STUDIES
but we now get the following error messages when the simulation is run:
# ** Error: Incorrect AVG value
#    Time: 44 us  Iteration: 1  Instance: /tb
# ** Error: Incorrect AVG value
#    Time: 288 us  Iteration: 1  Instance: /tb
  ... etc ...
What happened? It turns out that the functionality of our circuit does not match the behavioral model  
in the test bench, as the following waveform shows:
The waveform marked ACCUM is the internal accumulator of the circuit, while the waveform marked  
SUM is the summation that occurs in the behavioral model of the test bench. As the waveforms show,  
at time 24  uS a REQUEST occurs that clears SUM to 0, while the accumulator continues to add the  
next  DATA sample to the accumulated result inside the actual circuit. This results in a miscompare  
when READY is asserted at 44 uS and hence the error messages.
So, which is correct, the circuit or the behavioral model? It again depends on what you and the systems  
engineer agree to. If the systems engineer believes that the average always needs to be computed over  
the  next eight  valid  input  samples  (as  previously  determined  when  REQUEST and  VALID occur  
simultaneously), then the control logic of the circuit needs to change to match the behavior of the test  
bench.
If this is indeed the decision that is made, then the following changes need to be made to the circuit.  
First, the control process is modified so that if REQUEST is asserted, the sample counter is reset back  
to 0 instead of being ignored if the circuit is already running due to a previous assertion of REQUEST:
231


--- Page 251 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
----------------------------------------------
CONTROL_PROCESS: process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
----------------------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    SAMPLE  <=  0;
    RUNNING <= '0';
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (RUNNING = '0') then  --  REQUEST was ignored if RUNNING = '1'
      if (REQUEST = '1') then
        RUNNING <= '1';
      end if;
    elsif (VALID = '1') then
      if (SAMPLE = 7) then
        SAMPLE  <=  0;
        RUNNING <= '0';
      else
        SAMPLE <= SAMPLE + 1;
      end if;
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
is changed to:
----------------------------------------------
CONTROL_PROCESS: process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
----------------------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    SAMPLE  <=  0;
    RUNNING <= '0';
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (REQUEST = '1') then  --  REQUEST restarts the counter even if RUNNING = '1'
      SAMPLE  <=  0;
      RUNNING <= '1';
    elsif (RUNNING = '1' and VALID = '1') then
      if (SAMPLE = 7) then
        SAMPLE  <=  0;
        RUNNING <= '0';
      else
        SAMPLE <= SAMPLE + 1;
      end if;
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
Then, the process that generates the READY output is modified so that READY will only be asserted on 
the last sample as long as a new REQUEST is not being made. If REQUEST is asserted at the same  
time that the last input sample and assertion of VALID is occurring, we should ignore VALID and start a 
new eight sample computation. This results in the following modification to the  READY generation 
process:
232

--- Page 252 ---
CHAPTER 13 TEST BENCH CASE STUDIES
-------------------------------------------
READY_PROCESS: process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
-------------------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    READY <= '0';
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (SAMPLE = 7) then
      READY <= VALID;
    else
      READY <= '0';
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
is changed to:
-------------------------------------------
READY_PROCESS: process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
-------------------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    READY <= '0';
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (SAMPLE = 7 and REQUEST = '0') then  --  Ignore VALID if REQUEST = '1'
      READY <= VALID;
    else
      READY <= '0';
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
After these changes have been made to the circuit, the simulation produces the following waveforms  
and the simulation passes:
This example shows how using constrained random testing in terms of both data generation and  
control signal generation can highlight problems that would be hard to conceive of and construct using  
directed tests alone. The circuit now performs in a predictable fashion regardless of how it is driven in  
the system.
Summary
This chapter highlights how directed testing has its place in developing a circuit, but much better and  
robust testing can be done using constrained random testing. While directed testing is a good way to  
verify  that  your circuit  is  working  during  development  and  initial  checkout,  using  a  constrained  
random testing strategy usually results in a shorter and much more comprehensive test of the circuit  
under unusual or unexpected conditions.
233


--- Page 253 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
During simulation, errors can be due to errors in the test bench, where the behavioral model doesn’t  
match the functionality of the unit under test, errors in the unit under test, or errors in both. In order to  
correct these errors, the designer needs to track down the source of the error and correct it, regardless  
of whether it is a real error in the circuit or a false error due to an incorrect test bench.
This chapter has also shown that designs do not exist in a vacuum: agreement must always be made  
between you and the system designer to make sure that your circuit performs up to the expectations of  
the system requirements. A circuit that behaves in a predictable fashion when presented with corner  
conditions (timing control signals that occur at unexpected times, unexpected or illegal input data  
values, etc.) is always a better design as opposed to one that can behave unpredictably or, worse, lock  
up and require a system reset.
234

--- Page 254 ---
CHAPTER 14
LOGIC SYNTHESIS
Thus far in learning how to effectively use VHDL to design digital logic circuits, we have concentrated 
our discussion on the organization of the design through the use of entities and architectures, the use of 
concurrent  and  sequential  syntax  statements  to  describe  the  functionality  of  the  circuit,  and  the  
importance of creating a correct simulation as well as a functionally correct circuit. Although having a 
simulation that meets our criteria of being standalone, comprehensive, and self-checking is of utmost  
importance, it is a means to an end. The point of performing a design in VHDL is to create an actual  
logic device that can be integrated into a product.
This chapter will introduce the reader to the process that must be performed to convert VHDL code  
into the Boolean logic gates that (hopefully) performs the same function. This process is known as  
logic synthesis. As we shall see, the actual logic synthesis process is fairly straightforward; however, in 
order to be successful, there are multiple times in the process where the designer needs to evaluate the  
warning and error messages generated by the synthesis tool to make sure that the resulting logic gate  
design will perform as expected. The digital design flow diagram on page 34 shows where synthesis  
fits into the overall design process.
Logic synthesis uses the Register Transfer Level (RTL) subset of the VHDL language. Conceptually,  
an RTL model can be thought of as a series of storage elements—generally, flip-flops or memory  
arrays—that are connected together via a set of logic functions and equations. This concept is shown in  
the following diagram:
We have mentioned before that it is entirely possible to create a logic device that does not match the  
functionality of the RTL model. It is imperative that the designer stick with well-documented and -
supported  RTL-style  constructs  when  writing  the  VHDL  component  model.  For  review,  these  
constructs include the following:
235
D Q
LOGIC
EQUATIONS D Q
LOGIC
EQUATIONS

--- Page 255 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
• Use signals and ports of type std_logic (which includes std_logic_vector, unsigned, and signed). 
Signals of type integer and natural are also allowed. All other data types should not be used.
• Use concurrent signal assignments to implement Boolean logic gates.
• Use properly constructed processes to implement flip-flops.
• Always use processes with sensitivity lists (do not use wait statements within processes).
• Avoid the use of variables within processes unless absolutely necessary.
• Use either the rising or falling clock edge, but not both, for all of the flip-flops in the design.
Throughout the discussion of synthesis in this chapter, we will be using the Vivado software package  
from Xilinx for our examples. If you are using another FPGA vendor, the process you will follow will  
be similar in nature, but the actual steps you take to create the design will be different. If you are using  
another vendor, you will have to follow the steps that vendor outlines in its application notes.
Synthesis Phases
Just as was the case with simulation, the process of performing logic synthesis follows a prescribed set  
of steps in order to create the logic network. In the case of logic synthesis there are six distinct phases,  
which are discussed below:
Analysis. Just like with simulation, the first step is compiling the code and storing it in a library. Each 
VHDL source file is examined  separately  for syntactic errors, and if there are no errors, a tool-
dependent intermediate representation of the code is stored in a library. With synthesis, however, the  
rules that the compiler follows are more stringent: it checks to make sure that the files adhere to the  
register transfer level (RTL) style of modeling. Note that you do not include the test bench files in the  
file set used for analysis.
Synthesis. Each entity/architecture pair is converted into a set of Boolean logic equations and storage 
elements that are used to implement the design. When all of the entity/architecture pairs have been  
successfully converted, the entire design is elaborated into a single logic network by instantiating and  
connecting each component in the design hierarchy. The designer has the choice of flattening the  
network into a single unified logic network or preserving the design hierarchy. Preserving the hierarchy 
can result in a slightly larger design but is useful in tracking down synthesis issues in a design with a  
large amount of component instantiations.
Optimization. The Boolean logic equations are optimized for speed and area. The synthesis tool will  
perform the logic optimization techniques similar to that of Karnaugh maps and DeMorgan’s Theorem  
to reduce the logic equations to their minimal form. The synthesis tool will analyze the elaborated  
design looking for logic that is unused (logic equation outputs that do not drive any subsequent logic)  
and redundant logic (duplicated logic equations and storage elements). In the case of unused logic the  
equation is removed from the design, which may cause other equations to become unused and removed 
as well. In the case of duplicated logic, the logic network is modified by removing the duplicates so  
that only a single equation or storage element remains in the network.
236

--- Page 256 ---
CHAPTER 14 LOGIC SYNTHESIS
Mapping. The Boolean logic equations are mapped into the target technology logic gates that will  
implement the equations. At this point, the original VHDL RTL model has been converted into a netlist 
of logic gates along with the signals that are used to connect these gates together.
Place and route. The logic gates are placed on the silicon die and are connected together. The place  
and route algorithm will attempt to optimize the timing of the device by placing related logic gates as  
close as possible to each other on the die. After the logic gates are placed, the routing algorithm will  
create the signal routing necessary to connect each of the gates together. Sometimes routing congestion 
will create a network that cannot be routed, in which case the placer will be rerun to alleviate the  
congestion. At this point, there is a device  layout that exactly describes the physical placement and  
routing of all the logic gates and wires needed to implement the design.
Static timing analysis. The estimated best- and worst-case timing of the logic (due to transistor and  
routing delays) is used to calculate and report the maximum clock frequency at which the circuit will  
operate. Estimated setup and hold delays are also computed to make sure that the flip-flop timing  
specifications for the target logic family are met. A timing report is generated that the designer needs to 
review to make sure the device will work at the actual clock frequency being used on the board.
Synthesis Steps
The steps that the designer follows to create a synthesized design are fairly straightforward. The first  
step is to create a project for your logic device and add all of the RTL files needed to implement the  
design. You must make sure that all of the components are included in your project or you will end up  
with empty components that will be treated as black boxes. During the optimization phase these black  
box components will cause the pruning of unused logic, and you could quite possibly end up with a  
design that has no gates in it! A logic design with zero area is almost always the result of not including  
all of the required RTL files in the project. To make sure that all of the required files have been  
included, the designer must inspect the synthesis report and look for black box component warnings  
before moving on.
The second step is creating a constraints file for the design and adding this file to the project. The  
constraints file contains the timing assignments for the design, such as the clock rate that will be used  
on the board and the setup timing of the device inputs relative to the clock. The constraints file also  
contains the pin assignments of the device I/O signals and the electrical I/O standard for each pin.  
Constraint files can be created by hand-editing a text file or, better still, using the constraints wizard  
that is included with the synthesis tool. A portion of a constraint file, which would be used for a Xilinx  
FPGA design on a Nexys 4 demo board, is:
237

--- Page 257 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
################################################################################
# Nexys4 Demo Board Constraint File
################################################################################
# System Clock (100 MHz)
# ----------------------
create_clock -name CLK_I -period 10.000 [get_ports CLK_I]
# Pin Assignments
# ---------------
set_property PACKAGE_PIN E3       [get_ports CLK_I]
set_property PACKAGE_PIN C12      [get_ports RSTN_I]
...
set_property IOSTANDARD LVCMOS33  [get_ports CLK_I]
set_property IOSTANDARD LVCMOS33  [get_ports RSTN_I]
...
# Configuration settings
# ----------------------
set_property CFGBVS VCCO         [current_design]
set_property CONFIG_VOLTAGE 3.3  [current_design]
Synthesis
Once you have all of the files defined and included in your project, the act of performing analysis and  
synthesis is usually a “push-button” operation:
When the synthesis operation is complete, the designer must review the report and message logs that  
are generated by the synthesis tool and fix any errors or critical warnings. Furthermore, the designer  
must look for any components that have been marked as black boxes (indicating a missing file in the  
project), plus look at the warnings that have been generated and modify the RTL to fix those warnings  
if they are not expected or cannot be explained. Note that warnings will almost always be generated  
during the synthesis step, but you will find that most of them can be ignored if they can be explained.  
Messages such as missing signals in sensitivity lists is an example of a warning that must be corrected.  
When the errors and warnings have been fixed, the user can rerun the synthesis step and reinspect the  
report and message logs.
At this point, it needs to be mentioned that any changes in your RTL must be resimulated to make sure  
that the functionality has not been compromised. This is the point of having a test bench that can be  
used  to  perform  regression  testing.  It  is  necessary  that  the  designer  has  the  discipline  to  rerun  
simulations on any RTL files that need to be modified as a result of synthesis. Furthermore, if your test  
strategy uses a test suite, the simulations for the function blocks that contain the modified components  
need to be simulated as well to make sure that the component still operates correctly wherever it has  
been instantiated. Understand that you will be under incredible pressure to “get something in the lab,”  
but you must be resolute in following the best practice design processes that we have been following in 
238


--- Page 258 ---
CHAPTER 14 LOGIC SYNTHESIS
this book. The last place you want to be debugging your circuit is in the lab. Spending 10 or 15  
minutes in simulation to verify that the functionality of your design is correct is better than spending  
hours, if not days, in the lab trying to figure out why the circuit doesn’t work properly.
In Vivado, the synthesis messages can be found by   clicking on the warning/error messages link  
inside the Synthesis section of the Project Summary window,   selecting the Messages tab if not  
already shown in the bottom window, and  going through all of the messages:
Implementation
Once the synthesis phase has been completed successfully, the next step is implementing the design,  
which performs the last four phases of synthesis (optimization, mapping, place and route, and static  
timing analysis). This again is a push-button operation:
Just like with synthesis, when the implementation operation is complete, the designer must inspect the  
report files and message logs and fix any errors or critical warnings. Again, any changes to the RTL  
must be resimulated to make sure that the intended functionality has not been adversely affected. Once  
corrections have been made, the designer will need to rerun both the synthesis and the implementation  
steps. In Vivado, the process used to inspect the implementation messages is similar to inspecting  
synthesis messages by clicking on the warning/error messages link inside the Implementation section  
239
1

3


--- Page 259 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
of the Project  Summary window, selecting the Messages  tab if not already  shown in the bottom  
window, and going through all of the messages.
At this point in the process, you can also inspect the timing analysis results to make sure there aren’t  
any timing violations and that the maximum clock frequency of your design exceeds the actual clock  
rate that will be used on the board. Unfortunately, if the circuit cannot meet the actual intended clock  
rate, this usually means that a major rewrite of the RTL might be necessary, such as adding pipeline  
stages or changing how the logic implements the algorithm. Fortunately, the timing analysis report will  
point you to the specific portion of your logic that does not meet the timing requirements. Running  
regression testing on your RTL after timing violations are fixed is absolutely necessary to make sure  
that the expected functionality is maintained.
Implementation Checks
While your circuit may pass through the steps of synthesis and implementation successfully, it is a  
good idea to make sure that the results make sense. A simple method that can be employed to check the 
results is to perform a mental estimate on the number of flip-flops you anticipate each component  
needing and checking that the flip-flops in the implementation report more or less match your estimate. 
Small discrepancies are okay, but any large discrepancies will need to be investigated. For example,  
given the following code:
type    STATES is ( IDLE, LOAD, RUN );
signal  STATE : STATES;
signal  COUNT : natural range 0 to 8;
···
------------------------------------------
STATE_PROCESS:  process (MRST, MCLK) begin
------------------------------------------
  if (MRST = '1') then
    STATE <= IDLE;
    COUNT <= 0;
  elsif (MCLK'event and MCLK = '1') then
    case STATE is
      when IDLE =>  if (TXREQ = '1') then
                      STATE <= LOAD;
                      COUNT <= 0;
                    end if;
      when LOAD =>  STATE <= RUN;
                    COUNT <= COUNT + 1;
      when RUN  =>  if (TXRDY = '1') then
                      if (COUNT = 8)
                        then STATE <= IDLE;
                        else STATE <= LOAD;
                      end if;
                    end if;
    end case;
  end if;
end process;
we would estimate that the circuit could be implemented using the following flip-flop count:
• STATE. Assuming that STATE is implemented using a one-hot method, where the total number of 
flip-flops equals the number of states in the enumerated type, STATE would require three flip-
flops.
240

--- Page 260 ---
CHAPTER 14 LOGIC SYNTHESIS
• COUNT. The counter will range from 0 to 8, which means that COUNT will require four flip-flops to 
represent the range of possible values.
The estimated total number of flip-flops in this portion of the design is seven. If the total number listed  
in the implementation report is significantly different, you should look into why this is so.
A second simple checking method involves looking at the Boolean gate count and flip-flop count for  
each function block in your design and applying the following rules of thumb:
• Data processing typically uses more flip-flops than logic gates.
• Control circuitry typically uses more logic gates than flip-flops.
• Mixed data processing/control circuitry will use approximately the same amount of logic gates and 
flip-flops.
You can check the implementation report for each component in your design and verify that the results  
generally follow these rules of thumb. Again, any large or unexpected discrepancies will need to be  
investigated.

bitstream and download it to the FPGA device. This again is a push-button operation:

manager. Programming the device is done by right-clicking on the FPGA device in the hardware  
manager and selecting the Program Device… option in the pop-up window:

Alternately, you could program the memory device that is attached to the FPGA so that it automatically

Summary
The synthesis process is in general a very easy process; it mainly involves running each of the steps  
using a push-button operation. There are no Karnaugh maps to contend with and no applications of  
DeMorgan’s Theorem to simplify the logic, and there is no need to draw schematics and connect logic  
gates together with wires. All of this is done behind the scene using a synthesis software tool.
However, to be successful, you must check the reports and message logs that are generated as each step 
of the synthesis process is completed and correct the RTL code associated with each of these messages. 
You should also make sure that the implementation results make sense, using the guidelines outlined in 
this chapter. Whenever the RTL is modified, due to warnings or errors during the logic synthesis  
process, you must go back and perform regression simulations to ensure that the expected functionality  
has not been compromised by your changes. It is imperative that you resist the temptation of getting  
your design  into  the lab  too  soon.  As  previously  stated,  the last  place  you  want  to  debug  your  
nonfunctioning logic is in the lab.

CHAPTER 15
ASIC AND FPGA TECHNOLOGY
Before we can discuss how to compose our VHDL in a manner that results in a logic circuit that can be  
efficiently implemented on silicon, we must take a brief excursion into how the logic gates are actually  
implemented. Why is this important? The author has found that the best digital designers are those who 
can “see” the logic gates that are being described by the RTL. When a designer understands the target  
technology for the target device, the code can be “crafted” to take advantage of the underlying features  
of the technology.  Understanding  how the actual  logic will be implemented  and  what functional  
features can be utilized to create the logic network will almost always result in a more efficient design.  
Design efficiency translates into a smaller design, which results in lower production unit costs and  
lower power requirements for the device.
Digital Logic Technology
There are essentially two types of devices that can be used to implement logic designs: Application  
Specific  Integrated  Circuits  (ASICs,  pronounced  “A-siks”)  and  Field  Programmable  Gate  Arrays 
(FPGAs, pronounced “F-P-G-As”). An ASIC is a device where a custom layout is used to create the  
logic circuit. An ASIC device performs a specific function and in general cannot be used in products  
that need a different logic function. For example, an ASIC used in a cell phone would not be expected  
to be used in a WiFi router because the basic functional requirements of the two products are different.  
ASICs require a custom set of photolithography masks that are used to fabricate the device, which  
increases the time and cost required to design the device. FPGAs, on the other hand, do not perform  
any function when fabricated: a configuration bitstream is loaded into the device that is used  to  
“program” the part in order to perform a specific function. This configuration bitstream is loaded into  
the FPGA whenever power is applied.
Each device technology has advantages and disadvantages in terms of cost and design time, which can  
be broken down into four broad categories:
• Development cost. The time and cost required to develop an ASIC is much higher than the 
equivalent time and cost needed to develop an FPGA. This is mostly due to the fact that ASICs 
require a custom mask set to produce the device. FPGAs do not use custom masks, and thus this 
time and cost are avoided.
• Production cost. ASIC devices are usually much smaller than the equivalent FPGA device that 
would be needed to implement the design. This results in lower production costs for ASICs.
247

--- Page 267 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
• Power. ASIC devices use lower power than FPGAs. An ASIC contains only the transistors that 
are needed to implement the design, whereas FPGAs contain both programmable logic functions 
and the structures required to load the configuration bitstream, which increases the transistor count 
and power requirements.
• Reprogrammability. An ASIC device performs a specific function. Any changes to the logic 
require the ASIC design to be redone and a new mask set to be created to implement the updated 
logic design. FPGAs do not have any inherent functionality—the functionality is defined by the 
bitstream that is loaded at power-up. Any changes to the logic can be made by creating a new 
bitstream that contains the modified functionality, without having to create a new mask set or 
soldering a new integrated circuit to the board. FPGAs can also be updated once the unit is sold by 
providing the end user with an updated bitstream.
Development  and  production  unit  costs  usually  are  the two  most  important  considerations  when  
determining which technology to use. The following graph shows the trade-off between the two:
In a high-volume design such as a cell phone, where it is reasonable to expect millions of units to be  
sold, it is better to implement the logic using an ASIC because the unit cost savings will exceed the  
higher production cost and result in a lower lifetime cost for the device. In low-volume designs such as  
professional audio devices, where the entire lifetime production run may only be in the thousands,  
using an FPGA is a better choice, since the unit cost savings of using an ASIC versus an FPGA, over  
the total number of units sold, will never approach the higher development cost.
CMOS Technology
Modern digital logic devices are almost always implemented using a family called Complementary  
Metal Oxide Semiconductor ( CMOS, pronounced “SEE-moss”) logic. CMOS logic consists of a pair  
of  field  effect  transistors,  a  p-channel  device  (PMOS)  and  an  n-channel  device  (NMOS).  The  
schematic symbols for these two types of transistors are:
248
Development Cost
Unit Cost
ASICs
FPGAs

--- Page 268 ---
CHAPTER 15 ASIC AND FPGA TECHNOLOGY
PMOS NMOS
The PMOS and NMOS transistors are connected together to perform a Boolean logic function, which  
works by having either the p- or n-channel transistor in a saturated “on” state and the corresponding  
transistor in the pair in a high impedance “off” state. This is the reason why CMOS technology has a  
low static power requirement and is such a popular logic family choice: as long as the input signal is in  
a static state, the transistor pair will use minimal current, since with one of the transistors in the “off”  
state there is a high impedance current path between the power supplies. In addition, there is a high  
impedance current path from the output of the transistor pair to the insulated gate of the transistors of  
the next logic gate. Note that CMOS logic gates always invert the output; thus, the native Boolean  
CMOS logic gates are NANDs, NORs, inverters, etc. The following diagram shows the connections that 
perform a logic inversion ( NOT) Boolean function and the state of the inverter for each of the binary  
input values:
If we were to look at a cross-section of the silicon die, we would see the following CMOS transistor 
structure for an inverter:
For the PMOS transistor, shown on the right side of the diagram above, when ground is applied to the  
gate, the electrons are repelled away from the surface of the n-well, and a p-channel is induced. This  
saturates the PMOS transistor and creates a low resistance path between the  p+ source and drain  
regions. At the same time, a ground voltage on the gate of the NMOS transistor turns that transistor off, 
since the free electrons will be repelled from the surface of the p-substrate, maintaining a high resistive 
path between the  n+ source and drain regions. The process is reversed when a positive voltage is  
connected to the gates of the two transistors; the electrons of the p-substrate are attracted to the surface, 
249

and an  n-channel is induced between the n+ source and drain regions, while the PMOS transistor is  
turned off.
While  it  is  not  absolutely  necessary  to  understand  the  device  physics  associated  with  a  CMOS  
transistor pair, having a basic knowledge of how this NMOS/PMOS transistor pair performs logic  
functions is desirable.
ASIC Implementation
There are three basic types of ASICs that are used to implement a logic network:
• Full custom. These devices pack the transistors as tightly together as possible and require a full  
mask set to fabricate. Due to this fact, full custom devices have the highest production cost but the  
lowest production unit cost.
• Standard cell. These devices use predefined “cell” layouts for each of the logic gates that are  
used  to  implement  the  design.  Just  like  with  full  custom  devices,  custom  cells  such  as  
microprocessors and memories can be placed on the die. While this type of ASIC still requires a  
full mask set to fabricate, the production costs are somewhat lower than that of full custom  
devices, while the production unit cost is somewhat higher. This is because the layout of the masks 
uses a computer program to automate the task, but die area is wasted due to the routing of signals  
between the cells. Overall, however, the production costs are still high, and the unit cost is still  
low.
• Gate array. These devices use predefined arrays of CMOS transistors in various die sizes, and  
the custom  logic  function  is  implemented  via  the metal  layers  used  to  connect  the array  of  
transistors together. Gate arrays have the lowest production cost, since only the metal masks are  
required to personalize the device, and have the highest unit cost. Despite this, gate arrays are a  
very popular ASIC technology, because unpersonalized wafers can be stockpiled by the ASIC  
vendor, and personalization is only done with a small subset of fabrication masks; thus, the time  
required to fabricate and package the design is reduced.
Examples of these three types of ASIC technology are shown below:
250
Full Custom Standard Cell Gate Array


--- Page 270 ---
CHAPTER 15 ASIC AND FPGA TECHNOLOGY
Note that hybrid approaches to ASIC implementation can also be used to realize digital logic devices.  
For example, a System-on-Chip (SoC) device could be implemented using full custom cells for a  
processor subsystem, which might contain a microprocessor, memory interfaces for external DDR  
RAM and flash memory, and Ethernet and USB interfaces. This could be combined with a logic array  
implemented as a gate array to implement the remaining custom logic for the device. The advantage of  
such a hybrid device is that changes to the logic could be made via changes only to the metal masks in  
the gate array portion of the die, which would minimize the cost of any such design change.
Gate Arrays
Because of their reduced development cost and time, gate arrays are a very popular ASIC technology  
for  custom  logic  devices,  especially  for  “medium”-sized  production  quantities.  As  mentioned  
previously, a gate array is a sea of CMOS transistors that can be connected together via custom  
personalization metal layers. The basic structure of a  gate array, also known as a “ sea of gates ,” is 
shown below:
Inside each of the horizontal rows are alternating rows of PMOS and NMOS transistors with power  
and ground power supply rails for each pair of rows:

various Boolean logic gates:

Recall that CMOS logic gates naturally invert the output; thus, inverters, NAND gates, and NOR gates 
are the most efficient method  for implementing a logic network using CMOS technology. When a  
noninverting gate is necessary, it is implemented  using the inverting version of the  logic function  
followed by an inverter; thus, an AND gate is created using a NAND gate and an inverter:
When implementing a CMOS logic function in an ASIC, application of DeMorgan’s theorem is used  
extensively to create as many inverting logic functions as possible. Synthesis tools are aware of the  
fact that CMOS logic gates are inverting and will attempt to implement the logic  using as many  
inverting logic gates as possible to minimize the number of logic gates needed to realize the design.

Due to the fine-grained nature of ASICs, any logic function can be targeted to a sea of gates. For more  
complex functions, the designer normally has access to premade libraries that contain macrofunctions,  
such as microcontrollers, RAM and ROM, and multipliers.
FPGAs
The internal structure of an FPGA is vastly different from that of an ASIC. Instead of having pairs of  
CMOS transistors that can be connected together to form logic gates and networks, FPGAs contain an  
array of configurable logic blocks (CLBs)  that implement logic functions along with programmable  
switch matrices (PSMs) that implement the “wires” used to connect the CLBs together. FPGAs, as the  
name implies, are field programmable, meaning that the functionality of the device is programmed by  
the end user using a configuration bitstream. Most FPGAs are reprogrammable, so modifications to the  
functionality of the design can easily be made by simply generating a new configuration bitstream.
A notional diagram for an FPGA looks like:
The CLBs contain look up tables (LUTs) which are programmed by the configuration bitstream and 
can be  used to implement any arbitrary  n-input logic function. The CLBs also contain a flip-flop,  
usually one fl ip-flop per LUT,  that can either be connected to  the LUT output or be driven directly  
from the interconnect. The LUT itself is implemented as a small RAM where the contents are written  
from the configuration bitstream and the inputs to the function are used as the RAM read address. For  
example, given a LUT with three inputs and one output, the LUT would be physically realized as an  
8×1 RAM. The serial configuration data is written into the LUT to create the output for any three-input 
Boolean logic equation, as follows:
254
I/O Blocks
Logic Blocks (CLBs)
Interconnect (PSMs)

--- Page 274 ---
CHAPTER 15 ASIC AND FPGA TECHNOLOGY
In reality, most modern FPGAs contain LUTs  that  have more than  three inputs. The following is a  
description of the CLBs that are contained in a Xilinx Artix 7 series FPGA:
1. 2,000 to 33,650 CLBs in 8 array sizes.
2. Each CLB contains two slices.
3. Each slice contains four six-input LUTs. There are six independent inputs (A inputs—A1 to A6) 
and two independent outputs (O5 and O6) for each of the four function generators in a slice. The 
function generators can implement:
◦ Any arbitrarily defined six-input Boolean function.
◦ Two arbitrarily defined five-input Boolean functions, as 
long as these two functions share common inputs.
◦ Two arbitrarily defined Boolean functions of three and two 
inputs or less.
4. A six-input function uses:
◦ A1-A6 inputs.
◦ O6 output.
5. Two five-input or less functions use:
◦ A1–A5 inputs.
◦ A6 driven High.
◦ O5 and O6 outputs.
While it is not necessary to memorize all of the features of the Artix 7 CLBs, it is worth noting how 
flexible these CLBs are. Over time, the FPGA vendors have analyzed samples of the designs that have

been implemented on their devices and have added features to more efficiently implement these types  
of typical designs. As shown in the above list, each CLB consists of two slices, and each slice contains  
four LUTs. Each slice contains the following programmable logic structure:
Each slice can be configured to implement  up to four  independent flip-flops with up to a six-input  
logic function for the data input to each flip-flop or up to eight independent flip-flops with up to a five-
input logic function for each flip-flop input. Note that the flip-flops in a slice all have a common clock  
and clock enable input; if a flip-flop uses a unique clock or clock enable, the remaining flip-flops in the 
slice cannot be used. The slice can also be configured as four independent 4:1 multiplexers, with an  
optional  registered  output.  Using  the  dedicated  F7AMUX and  F7BMUX,  two  independent  8:1  
multiplexers can be implemented in one slice, again with an optional registered output. Finally, using  
the  F8MUX, a single 16:1 multiplexer can be implemented in one slice with an optional registered  
output.
Each  slice  also  contains  dedicated  fast  look-ahead  carry  logic  that  is  provided  to  perform  fast  
arithmetic addition and subtraction within the slice. Addition (and correspondingly, subtraction) is one 
of the most common logic functions because it is used for adders, subtractors, counters, comparators,  
256


--- Page 276 ---
CHAPTER 15 ASIC AND FPGA TECHNOLOGY
serial multipliers, etc. The inclusion of a dedicated fast look-ahead carry chain efficiently implements  
this common logic function. The carry circuitry is cascaded vertically between slices from the bottom  
to the top of the CLB array:
CLBs can also be configured as distributed RAMs by allowing the user to access the LUT inputs as the 
read and write address lines to the LUT RAM with a one-bit data input/output. Multiple LUT RAMs  
can  be  combined  together  to  create  larger  RAMs.  For  example,  a  32 ×16 bit  RAM  could  be  
implemented using 16 LUT RAMs (one LUT RAM per bit), which could be realized using only four  
slices or, equivalently, two CLBs.  In certain FPGA families, the shift register used to configure the  
LUT can also be accessed by the user. This will result in a shift register that doesn’t use any of the  
dedicated flip-flops in the slice. By offloading the shift register flip-flops to the configuration shift  
register, there is more room on the FPGA for the remaining logic, which may result in the design being 
able to fit into a smaller FPGA and a reduction of unit cost and power. Techniques that can be used to  
code logic that take advantage of these features will be discussed in chapters 16 and 17.
Many FPGAs also contain dedicated block RAMs , which are used to implement large memory arrays. 
For example, the Xilinx Artix 7 FPGA contains block RAMs that store 36 Kbits of information and can 
be configured in different aspect ratios from 32K×1 bit to 512×72 bits. Each block RAM can also be  
257


--- Page 277 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
configured as two independent 18  Kbit RAMs, which can be configured in aspect ratios from 16K×1 
bit to 512× 36  bits. The RAMs can  be configured  as a single read/write port (1 read/write port),  
independent read/write dual-port RAMs (1 read port, 1 write port), or true read/write dual-port RAMs  
(2  read/write  ports).  The  data  contents  of  these  RAMs  can  be  programmed  at  startup  using  the 
configuration bitstream to create ROMs (read-only memories) instead of RAMs.
Many FPGA families feature dedicated fast multiplier blocks . These blocks can be used to multiply  
two binary words together, either signed or unsigned, in a single clock cycle. Certain FPGA vendors  
place  additional  mathematical  circuitry  around  these multipliers  to  form  digital  signal  processing  
(DSP) elements. Xilinx FPGAs, for example, contain DSP blocks that contain three  operations: an  
initial  add/subtract  unit,  a  25×18  fast  multiplier,  and  a  final  add/subtract/accumulate  engine.  
Furthermore, the DSP block contains flip-flops between the processing elements to pipeline the data  
path for high-speed computations. The conceptual diagram for the DSP block is:
This allows the DSP block to perform the following functions:
• P   = B x (A ± D) ± C, or
• P += B x (A ± D)

each FIR tap directly to a DSP block without any added CLB logic, as follows:

dedicated interconnection networks specifically designed to reach all of the clock inputs of the various  
clocked resources within an FPGA. These networks have been designed to have low skew and low

PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Summary
There are two types of devices that are used to implement digital logic networks: ASICs and FPGAs.  
ASICs are more expensive to develop but have a lower production unit cost. FPGAs, on the other  
hand, are the least expensive to develop and have the highest production unit cost. The choice of which 
type of device to use depends on the anticipated number of devices that will be used in the end  
product: higher production quantities favor ASICs, while lower production quantities favor FPGAs.
ASICs are fine-grained devices that can implement  any logic function through the connection of  
individual transistors using metal personalization masks. An ASIC performs a specific function and in  
general cannot be used in any product other than its intended target. ASICs also require much more  
time  and  cost  if  changes  to  the  logic  are  required.  FPGAs  implement  logic  through  the  use  of  
programmable look-up tables and switch matrices and do not have any inherent functionality—the  
function of the device is programmed via a configuration bitstream that is loaded into the device at  
power-up. Most FPGAs are reprogrammable so that modifications to the design functionality can  
easily be made by simply generating a new configuration bitstream.
The best digital designers are those who can “see” the logic gates that are being described by the RTL.  
When a designer understands the target technology of the target device, the RTL can be written to take 
advantage of the underlying features of the technology. Understanding how the actual logic will be  
implemented and the functional features that can be utilized to create the logic will almost always  
result in a more efficient design.
260

--- Page 280 ---
CHAPTER 15 ASIC AND FPGA TECHNOLOGY

4. Which device technology can be reprogrammed without needing a mask change?
5. A high-volume design such as an integrated processor/radio transceiver in a cell phone favors 
which type of device technology?
6. Name the three types of ASICs and compare the development and production costs of each type.
7. Why is CMOS technology popular for implementing digital designs?
8. What are the two main function blocks that FPGAs use to implement logic designs?

CHAPTER 16
SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
We are now at a point where we can look at code examples that will be synthesized into the common  
building blocks that are used to design a digital network.
As we stated in chapter 8, there are only a handful of common macro-level digital functions that are  
used in digital design. The reader can view this as a set of Lego blocks that are connected together to  
provide a specific digital logic function. The actual macro-level functions are rather simple; the real  
magic occurs when these blocks are connected together to perform a larger task. Readers may want to  
go back and refamiliarize themselves with the process examples that were presented starting on page  
131 prior to reading further.
The functions we will look at in this chapter are:
• Data Multiplexers
• Parallel Register Files
• Shift Registers
• Adders/Subtractors
• Counters
• Clock Dividers
• Loop Unrolling
• Tri-State Drivers
This chapter (and the next) is organized as a “cookbook” of code samples for each of the common  
macro-level functions. We will present the code that can be reliably synthesized into logic that matches  
the functionality of the RTL and will explore several alternative coding methods that can be used to  
realize a digital design. We will concentrate on FPGAs because they are somewhat more restrictive on  
how the logic is implemented, but the examples presented here are also applicable to ASICs.
Concurrent Logic
Concurrent  logic  (Boolean  AND/OR/NOT/etc.  gates)  is  best  modeled  using  concurrent  signal  
assignment  statements.  Earlier  in  this  book,  we  presented  an  example  of  a  concurrent  signal  
assignment statement that describes a set of concurrent Boolean logic gates:
SEL <= '1' when (ADDRL = "00" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "10" and ENA = '1') else
       '1' when (ADDRL = "11" and ENA = '1') else
       '0';
We stated that although this signal assignment could also be written as follows:
263

--- Page 283 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
SEL <= ENA and (ADDRL (1) or (not ADDRL (1) and not ADDRL (0)));
The intent of the code is much easier to understand using when-else clauses as opposed to describing it 
using only Boolean operators. Describing concurrent logic using the when-else form of the concurrent  
logic statement will usually result in fewer design errors and thus is the preferred way to write VHDL  
code for concurrent logic. The synthesis algorithm will apply the techniques and rules similar to those  
of Karnaugh maps and DeMorgan’s theorem to produce the most efficient set of logic gates that match  
the functionality of the statement.
Data Multiplexers
Multiplexing data is a common function used in digital logic design. In VHDL, the best method of  
modeling a data mux is using either a conditional signal assignment or a selected signal assignment  
statement. If you model the data mux using the conditional signal assignment statement:
OUTPUT <= IN0 when (SEL = "000") else
          IN1 when (SEL = "001") else
          IN2 when (SEL = "010") else
          IN3 when (SEL = "011") else
          IN4 when (SEL = "100") else
          IN5 when (SEL = "101") else
          IN6 when (SEL = "110") else
          IN7;
the equivalent selected signal assignment statement will be:
with SEL select 
  OUTPUT <= IN0 when "000",
            IN1 when "001",
            IN2 when "010",
            IN3 when "011",
            IN4 when "100",
            IN5 when "101",
            IN6 when "110",
            IN7 when others;
The  result  of  synthesizing  either  of  these  two  statements  will  result  in  the  following  logic  
implementation:
264

--- Page 284 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
There is a third method that can be used to create a data mux, one that uses array indexing. This can be  
particularly useful in situations where the input signals to the multiplexer can be represented as an  
array:
  type    INPUT_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal  INPUT : INPUT_ARRAY  (0 to 7);
  signal  INDEX : natural range 0 to 7;
begin
  INPUT (0) <= IN0;
  INPUT (1) <= IN1;
  INPUT (2) <= IN2;
  INPUT (3) <= IN3;
  INPUT (4) <= IN4;
  INPUT (5) <= IN5;
  INPUT (6) <= IN6;
  INPUT (7) <= IN7;
  INDEX  <= To_Integer (unsigned (SEL));
  OUTPUT <= INPUT (INDEX);
This obviously works only if all of the possible values of SEL are valid as is the case in this example,  
where all eight possible values of SEL select a valid input signal. This code will be synthesized into:
