Register Files
We previously mentioned that digital designs often contain configuration registers that change the  
circuit’s behavior, which are written to via a processor bus. The example that was presented used a  
case statement inside a clocked process, which writes the value of DIN into a set of registers based on 
ADDR when the WRENA signal is asserted:
  signal  REG_0 : std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal  REG_1 : std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal  REG_2 : std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal  REG_3 : std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
begin
  ------------------------------------------
  REG_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      REG_0 <= x"01";
      REG_1 <= x"12";
      REG_2 <= x"34";
      REG_3 <= x"56";
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (WRENA = '1') then
        case ADDR is
          when "0000" =>  REG_0 <= DIN;
          when "0001" =>  REG_1 <= DIN;
          when "0010" =>  REG_2 <= DIN;
          when "0011" =>  REG_3 <= DIN;
          when others =>  null;          --  Note:  Ignores ADDR "0100" to "1111"
        end case;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Synthesizing this code sample results in the following logic:
266


--- Page 286 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
The register file can also be modeled using an array index, similar to  the  method described in the  
previous section. In this case, however, you do not need to have all of the possible values for the select  
input be valid, because the case statement will ignore the invalid values via the others clause:
267


--- Page 287 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  type    REG_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal  REG   : REG_ARRAY (0 to 3);
  signal  INDEX : natural range 0 to 15;
begin
  INDEX <= To_Integer (unsigned (ADDR));
  ------------------------------------------
  REG_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      REG (0) <= x"01";
      REG (1) <= x"12";
      REG (2) <= x"34";
      REG (3) <= x"56";
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (WRENA = '1') then
        case INDEX is
          when 0 to 3 =>  REG (INDEX) <= DIN;
          when others =>  null;            --  Note:  Ignores ADDR "0100" to "1111"
        end case;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
As described on page  240 in the section on synthesis implementation checks, it is a good idea to  
perform a sanity check on the synthesis results by counting the number of flip-flops you would expect  
to see. In this case there are four registers, each containing eight bits. Therefore, the expected number  
of flip-flops is 32.
Shift Registers
Shift registers are primarily used for serial-to-parallel and parallel-to-serial conversion and are also  
used to provide data path alignment by delaying data values for a certain number of clock cycles. A  
simple example of a shift register is the following, in which the data is shifted in from the right to the  
left and uses a clock enable signal to control when the data is shifted through the register. In this case,  
the DIN signal is delayed by eight enabled clock cycles before appearing on DOUT:
The RTL that implements this register is as follows:
268
DINDOUT

--- Page 288 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
  signal  SREG : std_logic_vector (8 downto 1);
  
begin
  
  --------------------------------------------
  SHIFT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SREG <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SREG <= SREG (7 downto 1) & DIN;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  DOUT <= SREG (8);
Synthesizing this code produces the following result:
If we were to reverse the direction of the shift register as in:
the RTL would be changed to the following:
  signal  SREG : std_logic_vector (1 to 8);
  
begin
  
  --------------------------------------------
  SHIFT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SREG <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SREG <= DIN & SREG (1 to 7);
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  DOUT <= SREG (8);
269
DOUTDIN


--- Page 289 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
If we inspect the synthesis results for this example we can see the same set of flip-flops, only the  
indexing has changed from an index that increases from left to right to one that decreases from left to  
right. This may at first be surprising, but upon analysis it makes sense. Regardless of the shift direction  
in the VHDL code, the schematic is drawn with the data flow going from left to right.
Recall from chapter 15 that Xilinx FPGAs can repurpose the LUT configuration data shift register as a  
user-accessible data shift register. However, there is a catch to using this register: the configuration  
shift register cannot be reset. For the synthesis engine to target a shift register to the configuration  
structure, the reset must be removed from the process:
  signal  SREG : std_logic_vector (8 downto 1);
  
begin
  
  -------------------------------------
  SHIFT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -------------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SREG <= SREG (7 downto 1) & DIN;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  DOUT <= SREG (8);
This will result in the following circuit being synthesized:
Although our shift register has eight stages, the synthesis engine was able to realize it using only two D 
flip-flops ( FDRE) and a cell named  SRL16E that accesses the configuration data shift register. The  
reason that there is a flip-flop at the beginning and end of the shift register is to improve the overall  
timing of the shift register by using the normal LUT flip-flops and global clocking resources. This is a  
case where if the designer tries to predict the number of flip-flops in the synthesized design, there will  
be a discrepancy that needs to be addressed. It seems unlikely that an eight-stage shift register can be  
implemented using only two flip-flops. However, after inspection of the synthesized design, it turns out 
that this code and circuit are okay.
270


--- Page 290 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
If we extend our FPGA example to a 16-stage shift register with a width of 4 bits, the following code  
can be used to describe this circuit:
  type    SHIFT_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (3 downto 0);
  signal  SREG : SHIFT_ARRAY (16 downto 1);
  
begin
  -------------------------------------
  SHIFT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -------------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SREG <= SREG (15 downto 1) & DIN;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  DOUT <= SREG (16);
Before revealing the result of synthesis, ask yourself the following question: how many flip-flops  
would it take to implement this shift register? Normally, we would expect to see 4 × 16 = 64 flip-flops. 
However, there is no reset input to the shift register, meaning the configuration shift register could be 
reused for this purpose. Since we have seen in the previous example that a flip-flop is still used for  
each input and output stage of the shift register, this would mean that we would expect to see a circuit  
with  4 × 2 = 8 flip-flops instead.  Indeed, inspecting the resulting synthesized circuit confirms this  
expectation:
271

--- Page 291 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The end result of using the shift register structures in the FPGA is that the 64 bits needed to implement  
the shift register consume only 8 flip-flop resources, opening up the remaining 56 flip-flops (and their  
LUTs) for additional circuitry.
One final note about shift registers. The author likes to define shift register indexes as the number of  
clocks needed for the input data to shift through each stage of the register. The input stage is always  
numbered with an index of 1, and the index increases for each subsequent stage of the shift register.  
This index-numbering scheme makes it extremely easy to pull data off the register at various delays.  
For example, suppose you have a 10-stage shift register defined as:
type    SHIFT_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
signal  SREG : SHIFT_ARRAY (1 to 10);
and need to delay the data by 8 and 10 clock cycles. Using the above index numbering scheme, the  
following would access the data after 8 and 10 clock cycle delays:
DATA1 <= SREG  (8);
DATA2 <= SREG (10);
272


--- Page 292 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
Adders
We stated in chapter 15 that addition operators are among the most common logic functions used in  
digital  design  because  they  are  used  to  realize  adders,  subtractors,  counters,  comparators,  serial  
multipliers, etc. Before the introduction of the numeric_std package, addition could only be performed  
on integer and real data types, but now specifying addition and subtraction operations on vector data  
types is trivial with this package.
Addition
Before we show how addition is performed on vectors in VHDL, there is a bit of housekeeping that we  
need to perform to make sure our addition operation does not overflow or at least is handled gracefully  
and predictably. Consider the following 4-bit unsigned vector addition example: the possible range of  
values that a 4-bit unsigned vector can have is 0 to 15. Adding two 4-bit unsigned vectors together  
results in an output that ranges from 0  + 0 = 0 to 15 + 15 = 30  ("00000" to "11110"). Thus, in order 
to properly add these 4-bit vectors together requires a 5-bit adder. Next, consider what happens if the  
4-bit vectors represent signed data instead of unsigned data. The possible range of input values changes 
to  −8  to  +7,  and  the output  value will  range  from  ( −8) + (−8) = −16  to  7 + 7 = 14  ("10000" to  
"01110"), which again requires 5 bits:
Unsigned Addition Signed Addition
Input Range 0 to 15 −8 to +7
Output Range 0 to 30 −16 to +14
Therefore, the rule of thumb for properly sizing adders is this: given two input vectors named A and B 
(either signed or unsigned) that are added together to generate a vector named S, the number of output 
bits for S is:
SWIDTH = max (AWIDTH, BWIDTH) + 1
Note that when adding two binary numbers together, the signed/unsigned addition operation is exactly  
the same. The only difference is in the interpretation of the binary input and output values.
If you are performing a mixed operation where an unsigned input value is added to a signed input  
value, the number of output bits for the sum changes to:
SWIDTH = max (UnsignedWIDTH + 1, SignedWIDTH) + 1
We next must turn to the definition of the “ +” operator in the numeric_std package. The number of  
output bits returned for the “ +” operator is always max (AWIDTH, BWIDTH), not max (AWIDTH, BWIDTH) + 1. 
Thus, in order to properly handle the overflow bit, the inputs must be extended by one bit by either  
padding an unsigned vector with a '0' or adding an extra sign bit to the left side of a signed vector.  
This is accomplished via the following statements:
SUM <= ('0' & A) + ('0' & B);          --  Unsigned addition
SUM <= (A (MSB) & A) + (B (MSB) & B);  --  Signed addition
273

--- Page 293 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
or, more conveniently, by using the resize function in the numeric_std package, which works for both  
signed and unsigned data:
SUM <= resize (A, A'length+1) + resize (B, B'length+1);  --  Signed or unsigned
There is one more aspect of addition that we need to be aware of, that being what overloaded versions  
of the “+” operator are available for our use. Recall that VHDL is a strongly typed language; therefore,  
the addition function needs to be defined for each input data type are:
function "+" (L, R : unsigned) return unsigned;
function "+" (L, R : signed)   return signed;
function "+" (L : unsigned; R : natural)  return unsigned;
function "+" (L : natural;  R : unsigned) return unsigned;
function "+" (L : integer;  R : signed)   return signed;
function "+" (L : signed;   R : integer)  return signed;
You will note that there is no addition operator that will add an unsigned type to a signed type. In order 
to perform this operation, you must convert the unsigned vector to a signed vector prior to adding the  
two vectors together. For example, given two 4-bit vectors, one unsigned and one signed, the inputs  
will range from 0 to 15 and −8 to +7; that means that our output will range from −8 to +22 ("111000" 
to  "010110"), which requires 6 bits to fully represent this range of values. Adding a  signed vector 
SVEC to an unsigned vector UVEC is accomplished by:
SUM <= resize (SVEC, SVEC'length+2) + signed (resize (UVEC, UVEC'length+2));
In all cases, you must perform the data analysis presented in this section to make sure the width of the  
addition can fully represent the range of possible output values.
Armed with this background information, we can now show how to perform addition in VHDL that  
will be successfully synthesized into logic gates. The following code describes a 16-bit unsigned  
clocked adder with a clock enable, taking into account the overflow bit as a result of adding two 16-bit  
values together:
  signal  A, B : unsigned (15 downto 0);
  signal  SUM  : unsigned (16 downto 0);
begin
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SUM <= resize (A, A'length+1) + resize (B, B'length+1);
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
If we synthesize this code, the circuit, before the mapping of the circuit to the logic cells, looks like:
274

--- Page 294 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
The circuit, after the mapping phase, looks like:
With this overall world view of our synthesized circuit, we can begin to see the power of using VHDL  
to design digital logic. The mapped schematic for our adder is quite complex, involving many logic  
cells and even more connections between these cells. If done by hand, each cell instantiation and wire  
connection is an opportunity for the designer to make an error, whereas the VHDL code for this circuit  
275


--- Page 295 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
is quite easy to write and understand. In addition, the time it takes to write the VHDL code for our  
adder circuit is far less than what it would take to draw the equivalent schematic. As stated early on in  
this book, using VHDL simultaneously  increases both the efficiency of the design effort and the  
probability of first-time design success.
If we zoom in to the top left corner of the schematic shown above, we can see the structure of our  
synthesized and mapped circuit in greater detail:
Each  LUT2 cell performs an  xor operation on the bits of the  A and  B inputs, which then feeds a  
CARRY4 cell that is the fast look-ahead carry logic structure inside the CLB. Note that the MSB of the  
first CARRY4 carry output (C0 (3)) feeds the carry-in of the next CARRY4 stage (CI), which is how the 
carry ripples through the CLBs.
It is worth noting that if we had written our adder using signed instead of unsigned vector types, the  
resultant logic would have been exactly the same. Again, the only difference is in the interpretation of  
the input and output binary data values.
Subtraction
Subtraction, as you may have surmised, can be performed using the “-” operation defined for integer, 
real, and the vector types defined in the  numeric_std package. Just as with addition, we need to  
perform the same housekeeping to make sure that our operation does not overflow or again is handled  
gracefully and predictably. First, we must recognize that there isn’t really such a thing as a pure  
276


--- Page 296 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
unsigned subtraction operation, because the output could possibly be a negative number. If we have  
two 4-bit unsigned vectors, the possible range of the input values is 0 to 15. However, the possible  
output ranges from 0 − 15 = −15 to 15 − 0 = 15 ("10001" to "01111"), which is a 5-bit signed result.  
With 4-bit signed vectors, the possible range of input values changes to −8 to +7 and the output value  
will range from (−8) − 7 = −15 to 7 − (−8) = 15, which again requires 5 bits:
Unsigned Subtraction Signed Subtraction
Input Range 0 to 15 −8 to +7
Output Range −15 to 15 −15 to +15
Therefore, the rule of thumb for properly sizing a subtractor is this: given two input vectors named A 
and B (either signed or unsigned) that generate a vector named S, the number of output bits for S is:
SWIDTH = max (AWIDTH, BWIDTH) + 1
Since the result of a subtraction operation can be a negative result, it makes the most sense to always  
use the signed version of the “-” operation. This means that if you were to perform subtraction with  
two unsigned vectors, you would need to convert both input vectors to signed vectors by either adding 
a '0' sign bit or using the resize function and recasting each as a signed vector:
DIFF <= signed ('0' & A) – signed ('0' & B);
DIFF <= signed (resize (A, A'length+1) – signed (resize (B, B'length+1));
Let’s now see how we can perform subtraction in VHDL. The following code describes a 16-bit signed 
clocked subtractor with a clock enable, taking into account the overflow bit as a result of subtracting  
two 16-bit values:
  signal  A, B : signed (15 downto 0);
  signal  DIFF : signed (16 downto 0);
begin
  -------------------------------------------
  DIFF_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  -------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      DIFF <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        DIFF <= resize (A, A'length+1) - resize (B, B'length+1);
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
If we synthesize this code, the circuit, before the mapping of the circuit to the logic cells, looks like:
277

--- Page 297 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
The mapped schematic will look almost exactly the same as the one generated by our addition circuit,  
which again makes sense since subtraction is realized in logic via:
A – B  =  A + B + 1
If we zoom in to the top left corner of the mapped schematic as we did with our addition example, we  
see the following:
278


--- Page 298 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
Each LUT2 cell now performs an xnor operation on the bits of the A and B inputs instead of an xor 
operation, and the CYINIT input to the first CARRY4 cell is now being forced to a logic '1' (it was a  
logic  '0' for  the  adder).  The  LUT2 cell  performs  an  xnor operation  because  of  the  Boolean  
equivalence of:
A xor B  =  A xnor B
and forcing the CYINIT input to a '1' performs the “+ 1” operation. Thus, our synthesized circuit uses  
the familiar “invert and add 1” rule for implementing subtraction.
Overflow Protection
In  both  the addition  and  subtraction  examples  above,  we  stated  that  we need  to  make sure our  
operation does not overflow, or is handled gracefully and predictably. How is this accomplished? Let’s  
first look at what happens if we do nothing. Consider a 12 -bit signed adder that is used in a signal  
processing system that adds two sine waves together:
Sine Wave 1 Sine Wave 2
The output of our adder would be:
279


--- Page 299 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Notice that the output waveform exceeds the 12-bit range of our data path, as represented by the  
dashed lines in the bottom plot. If we ignore this overflow condition and just produced a 12 -bit result, 
we would end up with the following output waveform:
The output values that exceed the 12-bit maximum end up appearing as negative values, and the output 
values that exceed the 12 -bit minimum appear as positive values. Given this, it is difficult to see what  
the expected output signal should look like. In technical terms, this result is a “mess” and definitely not 
what we want.
How do we correct this issue? The easiest way is to extend our adder to 13 bits, as we have shown in  
our discussion on addition and subtraction, and live with the 1 bit of data path growth.  In this case, we  
don’t need to concern ourselves with overflow detection. However, in digital signal processing there  
are usually multiple mathematical operations that are applied to the signal, each of which may result in  
data path growth. The end result is that there can be a large number of bits that are added to the data  
path, something we may need to keep a handle on.
One method that is often used in signal processing is to saturate, or clip, the output such that any value  
that exceeds the maximum or minimum is forced to those values, as shown in the following plot:
While this adds a certain amount of distortion to the output, it is much better than the previous plot  
where the sign of the output was flipped when an overflow occurred. While saturation should be a rare 
280


--- Page 300 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
occurrence in order to minimize the amount of distortion added to the signal, it is definitely the better  
choice in many cases—certainly better than doing nothing.
Saturation is also a good choice because in an analog amplifier; as the signal input grows, the amplifier  
output reaches a point where it starts compressing, in other words, saturating. VHDL saturation mimics 
real-world analog compression, which is considered "graceful" degradation.
How, then, do we detect for overflow? In an unsigned adder, the detection of an overflow condition is  
easy: the MSB of the adder, extended by one bit, will be a logic '1' only when there is an overflow.  
Thus, the following code can be used to create a saturated unsigned adder:
  constant  SATURATE : unsigned := To_Unsigned (2 ** 12 - 1, 12);
  signal    PREADD   : unsigned (12 downto 0);
  alias     OVERFLOW : std_logic is PREADD (12);
begin
  PREADD <= resize (A, 13) + resize (B, 13);
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (OVERFLOW = '1')
          then SUM <= SATURATE;
          else SUM <= PREADD (11 downto 0);
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
We begin by defining a constant that contains the output  saturation value. This is easily done by  
computing the maximum value that a 12-bit vector can equal ( 212 - 1) and converting it to a 12-bit  
unsigned vector via the To_Unsigned function. Next, we move the actual addition function out of the  
process, because we will need both the result of the addition and the result of the overflow bit in order  
to properly clock the potentially saturated value into the register. This requires us to define a signal that 
we will use to compute the sum, which we call  PREADD in the example. Finally, we use an  alias 
declaration as a convenience to designate that the MSB of the bit-extended adder is our overflow  
detection bit. Inside the process, on each enabled clock we examine the status of the OVERFLOW bit to 
determine whether we should clock in the addition result or saturate the output.
Overflow detection with signed addition is not quite as clear-cut. You may recall when you learned  
digital logic design that a signed adder overflows when the carry into the MSB is not equal to the carry  
out of the MSB. Unfortunately, when using the “+” operator in VHDL, we don’t have access to the  
carry bits of the adder. Instead, we must use the alternate rule, which states that:
When adding 2 signed numbers together, if they both have the same sign (both positive or  
both negative), then an overflow occurs if and only if the result has the opposite sign.
This can be restated as:
281

--- Page 301 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
1. Adding two positive numbers must give a positive result; otherwise, an overflow has occurred.
2. Adding two negative numbers must give a negative result; otherwise, an overflow has occurred.
3. Adding a positive and negative number together can never result in an overflow.
This rule for signed overflow detection is shown in the following code:
  constant  SATURATE : signed := To_Signed (2 ** 11 - 1, 12);
  signal    PREADD   : signed (11 downto 0);
  signal    OVERFLOW : std_logic;
begin
--  Add the inputs together and examine the sign bits to determine whether an
--    overflow has occurred (an overflow happens if the sign of the two inputs
--    are the same and the sign of the output is different)
  PREADD   <= A + B;
  OVERFLOW <= '0' when (A (11) /= B (11))      else
              '0' when (A (11)  = PREADD (11)) else '1';
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (OVERFLOW = '0')
          then SUM <= PREADD;
        elsif (PREADD (11) = '1')
          then SUM <=     SATURATE;  --  Positive overflow
          else SUM <= not SATURATE;  --  Negative overflow
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Several changes can be seen when comparing the signed adder to the unsigned one we presented  
earlier. First, the  SATURATE constant has been changed to the maximum value that a 12-bit  signed 
vector can equal (211 - 1) and converting it to a 12-bit signed vector via the To_Signed function. Next, 
our  PREADD signal  now only  requires  12  bits  in  order to  compute the sum, since we are only  
interested in the sign bits of the input and output vectors to detect an overflow. Finally, OVERFLOW 
now needs to be declared as a signal instead of an alias, because we need to define a Boolean logic  
function to drive the signal. The logic for OVERFLOW is defined using a conditional signal assignment 
statement, which the reader should verify matches the rule for signed overflow detection stated above.  
Inside the process, on each enabled clock we examine the status of the OVERFLOW bit to determine  
whether we should clock in the addition result or saturate the output. If OVERFLOW is asserted, we use 
the MSB of PREADD to determine whether we should saturate with the most positive 12-bit value or  
the most negative value. Note that the negative saturation value ( "1000…") is the Boolean inverse of  
the positive saturation value ( "0111…"), which is why we can clock in either  SATURATE or  not 
SATURATE.
There is another equally valid method that can be used for signed addition overflow detection. This  
method  extends  the  addition  operation  by  one  bit  and  examines  the  two MSBs  of  the  result  to  
282

--- Page 302 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
determine whether an overflow has occurred. If both bits match, there is no overflow. If they don’t  
match, we use the incorrect sign bit to saturate the output. The code that implements this method is:
  constant  SATURATE : signed := To_Signed (2 ** 11 - 1, 12);
  signal    PREADD   : signed (12 downto 0);
  signal    OVERFLOW : std_logic;
begin
--  Add the inputs together and examine the extended sign bits of the output to
--    determine whether an overflow has occurred (an overflow occurs when the
--    sign bits disagree)
  PREADD   <= resize (A, 13) + resize (B, 13);
  OVERFLOW <= '1' when (PREADD (12) /= PREADD (11)) else '0';
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (OVERFLOW = '0')
          then SUM <= PREADD (11 downto 0);
        elsif (PREADD (11) = '1')
          then SUM <=     SATURATE;  --  Positive overflow
          else SUM <= not SATURATE;  --  Negative overflow
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
In either case, the amount of logic resources required to implement the saturated signed adder is the  
same. Therefore, the method you choose to use is entirely up to you.
Addition/Subtraction
There will be times when a design requires a circuit that performs both addition and subtraction. The  
preferred way to perform this operation is to help the synthesis engine out by “steering” the result  
through the method in which we compose the code for the circuit, taking advantage of our knowledge  
that subtraction is equivalent to addition using the “invert and add 1” rule:
283

--- Page 303 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  signal  A, B : signed (15 downto 0);
  signal  BIN  : signed (15 downto 0);
  signal  SUM  : signed (16 downto 0);
  
begin
--  Adder/subtractor ('0' = add, '1' = subtract)
  BIN <= B when (CTRL = '0') else not (B);
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (CTRL = '0')
          then SUM <= resize (A, 17) + resize (BIN, 17);
          else SUM <= resize (A, 17) + resize (BIN, 17) + 1;  --  "Invert and add 1"
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
The synthesis results for this code is:
If we zoom in to the top left corner of the mapped schematic, as we have done with both the addition  
and subtraction examples, we see the following:
284


--- Page 304 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
Each LUT2 cell has been replaced with a LUT3 cell that performs the following Boolean function:
A xor B xor CTRL
which is equivalent to:
A xor B when (CTRL = '0') else A xnor B
The reader should confirm this by composing a truth table if this equivalency is not clear.
Note how the synthesis engine has created a design that may not have been obvious to the designer.  
This is another advantage of using  VHDL:  whether we’re willing to  admit  it  or not,  sometimes  
synthesis is “smarter” than we are. This is not always the case, but in general synthesis produces results 
that are as good as if not better than what the designer could come up. However, although the circuit  
above has been implemented rather cleverly, it does have one cell that is unnecessary: there is a LUT2 
cell (shown in the bottom of the schematic above) that is being used to drive the CYINIT input of the  
first CARRY4 block. This LUT2 performs an xor operation of the CTRL and B(0) inputs. At the same  
time, the first LUT for the A(0) and B(0) inputs is a fixed xor operation. If we were to change our code 
to fully steer the circuit as follows:
285


--- Page 305 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  signal  A, B  : unsigned (15 downto 0);
  signal  BIN   : signed   (15 downto 0);
  signal  SUM   : unsigned (16 downto 0);
  signal  CARRY : natural range 0 to 1;
begin
--  Use CTRL for "invert and add 1"
--  CTRL = '0' for add, '1' for sub
  BIN   <= B when (CTRL = '0') else not (B);
  CARRY <= 0 when (CTRL = '0') else 1;
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SUM <= resize (A, 17) + resize (BIN, 17) + CARRY;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
The synthesis results would change to:
If we zoom in to view the lower 4 bits of the mapped schematic, we see the following:

--- Page 306 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
Note that the two LUT2 cells have been replaced by a single LUT3 (SUM[3]_i_5) and that the CTRL 
input is now being used as a direct input into the CYINIT port of the first CARRY4 cell. Is saving a  
single LUT significant? Probably not. But … there is a certain satisfaction in knowing that you are still  
smarter than the synthesis engine.
Counters
Counters are used extensively in digital design to provide control for the data path portions of the  
digital logic. Counters are really nothing more than an adder that adds a constant 1 to the current value  
if it counts up or subtracts a constant 1 if the counter counts down. In chapter 8, we presented code  
examples that implemented both an up counter and a down counter, repeated here for convenience:
287


--- Page 307 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  constant  CYCLE : natural := 160;
  signal    COUNT : natural range 0 to CYCLE-1;
begin
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (COUNT = CYCLE-1)
          then COUNT <= 0;
          else COUNT <= COUNT + 1;  --  Up counter
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
The equivalent down counter process would be implemented as:
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (COUNT = 0)
          then COUNT <= CYCLE - 1;
          else COUNT <= COUNT - 1;  --  Down counter
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
The synthesis results for the up-counter code are:
A down counter would produce similar results.
The reader may wonder why the code uses an if statement to detect the counter roll-over instead of  
using the mod operator. The reason for this is that the mod operator will generate the correct result even 
if the count is greater than the counter modulus, which implies that:
288


--- Page 308 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        COUNT <= (COUNT + 1) mod CYCLE;  --  Up counter
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
would actually be implemented in the logic as:
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (COUNT >= CYCLE-1)
          then COUNT <= COUNT + 1 - CYCLE;  --  Counter roll-over
          else COUNT <= COUNT + 1;          --  Up counter
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
For example, using the mod operator, if the count were to equal 170 in our modulo 160 counter, the  
next  count  value  would  be  11 (170 + 1 − 160),  which  is  what  (COUNT + 1) mod 160 equals.  The  
counter that uses the equality test ( COUNT = CYCLE − 1) uses 8 flip-flops and 5 LUTs, while the  
counter that uses the mod operator uses the same 8 flip-flops but needs 8 LUTs instead. Thus, the circuit 
using the mod operator is larger and is therefore not the preferred method. Why would the count equal  
170? In normal operation the counter would never exceed a value of 159, but if there were to be a  
single event upset due to a power or ground glitch, an 8 -bit counter could equal any value from 0 to  
255. Thus, the mod operator will produce the mathematically correct result for any count value that is  
out of range, whereas by using the equality test, the counter will eventually automatically recycle back  
to 0. If you would prefer that the counter recycle back to 0 immediately, the condition of the if test 
should be changed to the following, although this is generally not necessary:
if (COUNT >= CYCLE-1)
We next turn our attention to multi-modulo counter circuits. Oftentimes, you will find that your logic  
requires a variety of count delays in order to properly control your circuit, such as using a counter to  
control a state machine. Instead of using multiple counters to implement these delays, it is more  
efficient to use a single counter and control the counter modulus via the state value each time the  
counter rolls over. In this case, it’s also more efficient to implement the counter as a down counter  
because the count comparison is always the same value of 0. Thus, this code:
289

--- Page 309 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  signal  COUNT : natural range 0 to 139;
begin
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= 15;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (COUNT > 0) then
        COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
      else
        case STATE is
          when IDLE  =>  COUNT <=  11;  --   12 counts
          when START =>  COUNT <= 139;  --  140 counts
          when RUN   =>  COUNT <=   3;  --    4 counts
          when DONE  =>  COUNT <=  15;  --   16 counts
        end case;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
is preferred over:
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      case STATE is
        when IDLE  =>  if (COUNT =  15)  --   16 counts
                         then COUNT <= 0;
                         else COUNT <= COUNT + 1;
                       end if;
        when START =>  if (COUNT =  11)  --   12 counts
                         then COUNT <= 0;
                         else COUNT <= COUNT + 1;
                       end if;
        when RUN   =>  if (COUNT = 139)  --  140 counts
                         then COUNT <= 0;
                         else COUNT <= COUNT + 1;
                       end if;
        when DONE  =>  if (COUNT =   3)  --    4 counts
                         then COUNT <= 0;
                         else COUNT <= COUNT + 1;
                       end if;
      end case;
    end if;
  end process;
Besides being easier to code, the down counter will result in less logic. Note that in order for both of  
these code examples to provide the same delay in each state, the reload value for the down counter  
represents the number of clock delays in the next state, not the current state.
The synthesis results for our multi-modulo down counter is:
290

--- Page 310 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
Clock Dividers
A clock divider is a circuit that produces a single clock-wide output pulse on a periodic basis. It is used  
to generate a clock enable pulse at a rate that is lower than the master clock rate. For example, given a  
master clock rate of 25 MHz, if we create a clock divider that generates a single clock enable pulse for  
every 25 master clock cycles, we would produce a clock enable at the rate of 1  MHz. The following  
simulation waveform shows how a clock enable works:
Why would we choose to do this versus creating a second internal clock at 1 MHz? Having signals that 
can be reliably transferred between different clock domains is a very difficult task because we can  
never be sure what the underlying relationship is between the two clocks. Using a single master clock  
and a clock enable at the desired rate is a much easier and more reliable method for creating a slower  
effective clock rate.
The VHDL code that creates this 1 MHz clock enable is:
291


--- Page 311 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  constant  RATE_1MHZ : natural := 25;
  signal    DIV_1MHZ  : natural range 0 to RATE_1MHZ-1;
  signal    ENA_1MHZ  : std_logic;
begin
  ----------------------------------------------
  DIV1MHZ_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ----------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      DIV_1MHZ <=  0;
      ENA_1MHZ <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (DIV_1MHZ = 0) then
        DIV_1MHZ <= RATE_1MHZ - 1;
        ENA_1MHZ <= '1';
      else
        DIV_1MHZ <= DIV_1MHZ - 1;
        ENA_1MHZ <= '0';
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Again, we define a constant named  RATE_1MHZ to make our divider circuit reusable. We also have  
created an ENA_1MHZ output that is the output of a flip-flop, which helps optimize the maximum clock  
rate that the circuit can operate at.
For each of the processes that need to run at our 1  MHz rate, we would use the ENA_1MHZ flip-flop 
output as the clock enable, as shown for the following counter running at 1 MHz:
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (ENA_1MHZ = '1') then
        if (COUNT = 0)
          then COUNT <= CYCLE - 1;
          else COUNT <= COUNT - 1;  --  Down counter
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Given the above code for a clock divider, the reader might be tempted to use the following process if a  
second clock divider is needed to generate a 100 kHz clock enable:
292

--- Page 312 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
  constant  RATE_100KHZ : natural := 10;
  signal    DIV_100KHZ  : natural range 0 to RATE_100KHZ-1;
  signal    ENA_100KHZ  : std_logic;
begin
  ------------------------------------------------
  DIV100KHZ_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      DIV_100KHZ <=  0;
      ENA_100KHZ <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (ENA_1MHZ = '1') then
        if (DIV_100KHZ = 0) then
          DIV_100KHZ <= RATE_100KHZ - 1;
          ENA_100KHZ <= '1';
        else
          DIV_100KHZ <= DIV_100KHZ - 1;
          ENA_100KHZ <= '0';
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Unfortunately, simulating both of these clock dividers reveals a problem:
Zoomed into the region of interest, we see the following:
Notice what happens to the ENA_100KHZ signal from time 25 3 uS to 278  uS. Instead of generating a  
pulse that is one clock cycle wide, it is high for 25 clock cycles. This is because, as written, the  
ENA_100KHZ output is only allowed to change whenever  ENA_1MHZ is asserted. This is clearly not  
what we wanted. To correct this problem, we need to ensure that the ENA_100KHZ signal is held low  
whenever the ENA_1MHZ signal is low, as follows:
293


--- Page 313 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  constant  RATE_100KHZ : natural := 10;
  signal    DIV_100KHZ  : natural range 0 to RATE_100KHZ-1;
  signal    ENA_100KHZ  : std_logic;
begin
  ------------------------------------------------
  DIV100KHZ_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      DIV_100KHZ <=  0;
      ENA_100KHZ <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (ENA_1MHZ = '0') then
        ENA_100KHZ <= '0';
      elsif (DIV_100KHZ = 0) then
        DIV_100KHZ <= RATE_100KHZ – 1;
        ENA_100KHZ <= '1';
      else
        DIV_100KHZ <= DIV_100KHZ – 1;
        ENA_100KHZ <= '0';
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Simulating the updated process shows that we have corrected the problem (the ENA_100KHZ signal is 
now only one clock cycle wide):
While we are on the subject of clocking logic, there is a subtle trap that a designer may fall into that 
will cause unexpected results. In order for  a circuit to operate properly,  each clock signal in your  
design should be driven from a common source (i.e., a single signal driver), and you should never  
create additional copies of any clock signal via a signal assignment statement. Consider the following  
code, where FF1 is clocked by the CLOCK signal and FF2 is clocked by a second clock named CLOCK2, 
which is driven by CLOCK:
294


--- Page 314 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
    if (RESET = '1') then
      FF1 <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      FF1 <= DIN;
    end if;
  end process;
  CLOCK2 <= CLOCK;
  process (RESET, CLOCK2) begin
    if (RESET = '1') then
      FF2 <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK2'event and CLOCK2 = '1') then
      FF2 <= FF1;
    end if;
  end process;
Simulating this code results in the following:
Notice that the waveforms for FF1 and FF2 are identical instead of the data on FF2 being delayed by  
one clock cycle as we would have expected. Why did this occur? The reason is that we have introduced 
a delta delay cycle between CLOCK and CLOCK2. Thus, the behavior of this code is that FF1 clocks in 
DIN on the active edge of CLOCK, which will be updated one delta cycle later.  Simultaneously, CLOCK2 
is updated one delta cycle later as well, so when the active edge of CLOCK2 is detected in the second  
process,  FF1 has  already  been  updated,  and  that  new data value will be clocked  into  FF2. This  
behavior is shown in the following diagram:
Obviously  this  phenomenon  should  be  avoided,  because  in  actual  hardware  the  clock  signal  
reassignment is nothing more than a wire. However, since the signal assignment statement creates a  
driver for  CLOCK2 and since drivers in VHDL have inherent delays of at least one  delta cycle , the  
simulation will not match the hardware. Bugs of this nature are extremely difficult to track down and  
295
Δ
CLOCK2
CLOCK
DIN
FF1
FF2
Δ ΔΔ

--- Page 315 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
correct. The easy way to avoid this problem is to use a common clock source for all processes that use  
that clock.4
Loop Unrolling
When a synthesis engine encounters a loop within a process, the resulting logic is synthesized using a  
procedure called loop unrolling. You may recall from our discussion about generate statements that  
the contents were unrolled into discrete concurrent statements when the code was elaborated. For  
example, the following generate statement:
G1:  for I in 0 to 3 generate
  DOUT (I) <= DIN (3-I) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (I);
end generate G1;
would be expanded and replaced with the following four signal assignments upon elaboration:
DOUT (0) <= DIN (3) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (0);
DOUT (1) <= DIN (2) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (1);
DOUT (2) <= DIN (1) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (2);
DOUT (3) <= DIN (0) when (FLIP = '1') else DIN (3);
This is due in part to the fact that generate statements are used to create a repetitive set of concurrent 
statements. Loops, on the other hand, are contained within the  sequential confines of the process  
statement and need not be expanded during simulation; the loop can be executed by the simulator just  
as any loop would be treated in a sequential language. This is not the case when the process is  
synthesized, however. The synthesis engine needs to replace the contents of the loop with a repetitive  
set of sequential statements, similar to how generate statements are expanded. Hence, the loops are  
unrolled. The following example shows how loops are unrolled:
-------------------------------------------
LOOP_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
-------------------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    A <= (others => (others => '0'));
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    for I in 0 to 3 loop
      A (I) <= B (I) + C (I);
    end loop;
  end if;
end process;
becomes:
4 It is worth noting  that along with the question of how many delta cycles there are per uS, this is also a typical question  
asked during job interviews. Therefore, it’s important that you fully understand this concept so you can fully explain the  
unwanted behavior and answer the question correctly.
296

--- Page 316 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
-------------------------------------------
LOOP_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
-------------------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    A <= (others => (others => '0'));
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    A (0) <= B (0) + C (0);
    A (1) <= B (1) + C (1);
    A (2) <= B (2) + C (2);
    A (3) <= B (3) + C (3);
  end if;
end process;
We can use this feature to efficiently compose code using a loop that expands into a regular array of  
sequential statements. For example, consider an eight-stage clocked serial adder with a clock enable  
that generates an 11-bit output:
  type    Vector_Array is array (natural range <>) of unsigned (10 downto 0);
  signal  A   : Vector_Array (0 to 7);
  signal  SUM : Vector_Array (1 to 7);
begin
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => (others => '0'));
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SUM (1) <= A (0) + A (1);
        for I in 2 to 7 loop
          SUM (I) <= SUM (I-1) + A (I);
        end loop;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  DOUT <= SUM (7);
The statements inside the loop will be unrolled into the following set of signal assignments:
for I in 2 to 7 loop
  SUM (I) <= SUM (I-1) + A (I);
end loop;
SUM (2) <= SUM (1) + A (2);
SUM (3) <= SUM (2) + A (3);
SUM (4) <= SUM (3) + A (4);
SUM (5) <= SUM (4) + A (5);
SUM (6) <= SUM (5) + A (6);
SUM (7) <= SUM (6) + A (7)
Synthesizing this code results in the following circuit:
Zooming in reveals additional detail about the synthesized circuit:
297


--- Page 317 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
As predicted, we have a set of flip-flops with an adder between each stage. The resource estimate for  
this circuit postsynthesis is:
It is worth noting that it takes seven clock cycles for the circuit to produce a valid result. There is  
another technique that can be used to implement this adder: using a parallel adder instead of a serial  
one, which produces valid results in three clock cycles instead of seven:
  type    Vector_Array is array (natural range <>) of unsigned (10 downto 0);
  signal  A   : Vector_Array (0 to 7);
  signal  SUM : Vector_Array (0 to 6);
begin
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM <= (others => (others => '0'));
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        for I in 0 to 3 loop
          SUM (I) <= A (2*I) + A (2*I+1);
        end loop;
        for I in 0 to 2 loop
          SUM (I+4) <= SUM (2*I) + SUM (2*I+1);
        end loop;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  DOUT <= SUM (6);
The statements inside the two loops will be unrolled into the following set of signal assignments:
298


--- Page 318 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
for I in 0 to 3 loop
  SUM (I) <= A (2*I) + A (2*I+1);
end loop;
SUM (0) <= A (0) + A (1);
SUM (1) <= A (2) + A (3);
SUM (2) <= A (4) + A (5);
SUM (3) <= A (6) + A (7);
for I in 0 to 2 loop
  SUM (I+4) <= SUM (2*I) + SUM (2*I+1);
end loop;
SUM (4) <= SUM (0) + SUM (1);
SUM (5) <= SUM (2) + SUM (3);
SUM (6) <= SUM (4) + SUM (5);
and the new synthesized circuit will look like:
Readers who are familiar with how college basketball tournament brackets are put together will see the 
similarity between this circuit and  a tournament bracket:  there are four adders in the first round of  
addition, two adders in the second round, and a single adder in the final round. Interestingly, if we look  
at the resource estimate for this parallel adder, we will find the following:
We now have a slightly smaller implementation (67 flip-flops versus 74), and we get the results faster  
(3 clock cycles vs. 7). Clearly this is the superior method between the two, since it results in a logic  
implementation that is both smaller and faster.
Tri-State I/O Drivers
Digital devices are often connected to a microprocessor via a parallel data bus that is shared with other  
devices such as RAM and ROM. In this case, the data bus on our device must be able to be read and  
written, which means we need a tri-state I/O driver. The term tri-state means that the I/O pin can drive  
299


--- Page 319 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
low or high or be placed in a high impedance condition (essentially turned off). This is shown in the  
following diagram:
This I/O structure will drive the output port DBUS with the value on DOUT when RDENA is asserted; 
otherwise, the tri-state driver is disabled, and the external value on DBUS can be read on DIN.
When using the std_logic_vector data type, the following code can easily be used to model a tri-state  
bus I/O driver:
DBUS <= DOUT when (RDENA = '1') else (others => 'Z');  --  Tri-state (1/0/Z)
DIN  <= DBUS;
It should be obvious that these two signal assignments model the functionality of a tri-state I/O driver.  
The value on DOUT is assigned to DBUS when RDENA = '1', and the driver is turned off (by setting  
all of the bits to the logic state 'Z') when RDENA = '0'. At the same time, DIN always follows the  
value that is on DOUT.
It was stated in our discussion on entity port modes that we should always use either in or out and 
reserve inout only for cases where it truly is a bidirectional port. This is a case where we would want  
to use the inout port mode for DBUS at the top level of our design:
-------------
entity TOP is
-------------
  port ( ...
         DBUS : inout std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
         ... );
Inside our top-level architecture, we use the two concurrent signal assignments shown above to de-
multiplex the bidirectional DBUS port into an input signal and an output signal. We then use the DIN 
signal for all of the references to the input value and use DOUT to set the value of the port when read.  
If DIN is connected to an instantiated component, the port on that component should be of mode in. 
Similarly, if DOUT is driven by a port of an instantiated component, the port on that component should  
be of mode out.
A More Complex Example
All of the code examples we have presented in this chapter have been for a single function, described  
early on as resembling Lego blocks that can be connected together to implement the desired function.  
The  reader  should  not  assume  that  each  of  these  examples  should  be  used  to  create  an  
entity/architecture  pair  that  performs  each  function;  rather,  these  processes  should  be  connected  
together within an architecture to implement the desired behavior. Consider how traditional software is  
composed.  The  programmer  wouldn’t  create  an  addition  function  and  then  call  it  whenever  two 
numbers needed to be added together:
300
DBUS
RDENA
DOUT
DIN

--- Page 320 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
int add (int a, int b) {
  return a + b;
}
...
r = add (s, t);
Rather, the programmer would just add the numbers together using the “+” operator:
r = s + t;
The same is true in digital design; the designer wouldn’t create a generic adder function and then  
instantiate this component wherever an addition was needed. Instead, the addition would be performed  
by including a process within the architecture that performed addition. We have already seen this  
concept in action during the development of the engine management system on page 152. Instead of  
creating  three  entity/architecture  pairs  for  the  circuit  (the  run  control,  accumulator,  and  ready  
generator), we instead created three processes—one for each function—and connected them together  
via signals inside a single architecture.
To further illustrate this concept, consider the 4×16 shift register example shown earlier in this chapter. 
If we wished to take advantage of the configuration data shift register to reduce the amount of FPGA 
logic resources consumed to implement this shift register, it was noted that the shift register could not  
contain a reset. This may present a problem, as the data within the shift register is unknown until the  
first 16 data values have been shifted in, as shown in the following simulation:
A way to overcome this problem is to force the DOUT signal to a value of "0000" until valid data  
appears at the output of the shift register, then allow the data in the shift register to drive DOUT. This is 
best accomplished by creating a counter that counts the number of shift operations and controls what  
drives DOUT:
301


--- Page 321 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  type    SHIFT_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (3 downto 0);
  signal  SREG  : SHIFT_ARRAY (15 downto 1);
  signal  DELAY : natural range 0 to 15;
begin
  -------------------------------------
  SHIFT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -------------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SREG <= SREG (14 downto 1) & DIN;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  --------------------------------------------
  DELAY_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      DOUT  <= (others => '0');
      DELAY <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (DELAY < 15)
          then DELAY <= DELAY + 1;
          else DOUT  <= SREG (15);
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
Instead of creating a shift register component and a counter component and instantiating both of them 
within the architecture , we instead use two process statements to implement our shift register and  
counter. Why do we need to implement this using two processes instead of one? Note that the two  
processes have different direct action signals: the shift register depends only on  CLOCK, while the  
counter depends on both CLOCK and RESET. This means that we need to implement these two logic  
circuits using different processes, since the sensitivity lists are different.
Note that the shifted data output is still a registered output, in other words, the output of a set of flip-
flops. This means that  DOUT is now our last shift register stage and that  the 16-stage shift register  
needs to be shortened to a 15 -stage shift register. The circuit works by having the delay process force  
DOUT to "0000" upon reset and initialize the DELAY counter to 0. As each value is shifted into the  
shift register (when CLKENA = '1'), the counter is incremented as long as it is less than 15. When the  
counter equals 15, DOUT is driven by SREG (15) , and the counter will no longer increment. This will  
continue until RESET is asserted, at which point the process will repeat.
Simulating the code containing these two processes reveals that the circuit performs as desired:
302


--- Page 322 ---
CHAPTER 16 SYNTHESIS CODE EXAMPLES
DOUT now equals "0000" until 15 data samples have been clocked in, and then on the 16th sample  
and all subsequent samples  DOUT equals the delayed value of the shift register output. This can be  
confirmed by noting that the first sample shifted into the register is "1010" and the first valid value  
shifted out is also "1010".
Before we synthesize this code, we first want to predict how many flip-flops this circuit will take in  
order to do a sanity check that our circuit is implemented in the configuration shift register as intended.  
Recall that the synthesis engine will use CLB flip-flops for the input and output stage of the shift  
register for improved timing, with the remaining shift stages in SRL blocks. Thus, our shift register  
will use 4  + 4 = 8 flip-flops for our 4 -bit wide shift register and use 4 SRL blocks. In addition, our  
counter  ranges  from  0  to  15,  which  will  require  4  flip-flops.  Thus,  we  predict  that  our  shift  
register/counter circuit will require a total of 12 CLB flip-flops.
Running synthesis on our code produces the following result:
Inspecting the utilization report generated by the synthesis step contains the following:
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+
|          Site Type         | Used | Fixed | Available | Util% |
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+

|   LUT as Logic             |    4 |     0 |     63400 | <0.01 |

As this table shows, we indeed have a total of 12 flip-flops in our synthesized circuit, and it contains a  
total of 8 LUTs, 4 of them used as shift registers. We therefore have confirmation that our circuit  
synthesized as intended.
Summary
There are only a handful of common macro-level digital functions that are used in digital design. The  
reader can view these common functions as a set of Lego blocks that are connected together to provide  
a specific digital logic function. The actual macro-level functions are rather simple; the real magic  
occurs when these blocks are connected together to perform a larger task.
This chapter presented code for these common digital functions that can be reliably synthesized into  
the desired functionality. When the designer needs one of these common functions, the sample code  
can be used as a starting point for each of these functions.  The reader should not assume that each of  
these code samples should be used to create an entity/architecture pair that performs each function;  
rather, these processes should be connected together within an architecture to implement the overall  
desired behavior.
304

SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
In this chapter, we will look at code samples that can be synthesized into special logic structures that  
are common to most FPGAs. The functions we will look at in this chapter are:
• Fast Multipliers
• DSP Blocks (multiply/accumulate)
• RAM Blocks
• ROM Blocks
As in chapter 16, we will present the code samples in a cookbook format. The code that is presented in  
this chapter can be reliably synthesized into logic that matches the functionality of the RTL. We will  
concentrate on code that can be used with Xilinx FPGAs, but the general concepts introduced in this  
chapter are applicable to other FPGA families and ASICs. Each FPGA and ASIC vendor will have a set 
of application notes that describe the best way to code your VHDL to properly infer these special logic 
structures.

Before  we  present  the  VHDL coding  techniques  that  can  be  used  to  access  these  special  logic  
structures, let’s take a look at how many of these structures we have at our disposal. The following

For example, if we were targeting our design for an Artix XC7A50T, we would be able to use 52,160
