following diagram:
In this example the FPGA is divided into eight regions, each of which contains the logic elements and  
routing resources necessary to implement the design. In the expanded view the small white boxes  
represent the CLBs, the small shaded boxes represent the switch matrices, the large light gray boxes  
represents the block RAM, and the large dark gray boxes represent the DSP blocks. One thing to note  
from this diagram is how much of the FPGA die area is used for routing (the wires and switch  
matrices). In fact, the most common reason why designs will not fit in an FPGA does not have to do  
with limited logic resources; rather, the place and route tool  runs out of routing resources. Because of  
this, most FPGAs have more routing resources than logic resources to try to minimize the effects of  
routing congestion.
Multipliers
Multiplication is performed using the “*” operation, which is defined for integer, real, and the vector  
types defined in the numeric_std package. Just as with addition and subtraction, we need to perform the 
same “housekeeping” to make sure that our operation does not overflow or is handled gracefully and  
predictably. If we have two 4-bit unsigned vectors, the possible range of the input values is 0 to 15.  
The  possible  output  ranges  from  0  × 0 = 0  to  15 × 15 = 225,  which  is  an  8-bit  unsigned  result  
("00000000" to "11100001"). With 4-bit signed vectors, the possible range of input values is −8 to 
+7, and the output value will range from ( −8) × 7 = −56 to ( −8) × (−8) = 64, which again requires 8  
bits ("11001000" to "01000000"):
308


--- Page 328 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
Unsigned Multiplication Signed Multiplication
Input Range 0 to 15 −8 to +7
Output Range 0 to 225 −56 to +64
With this knowledge, we can properly size a multiplier using the following rule of thumb: given two  
input vectors named  A and  B (either signed or unsigned) that, when multiplied together, generate a  
vector named S, the number of output bits for S is:
SWIDTH = AWIDTH + BWIDTH
While the sizing of unsigned multiplication is pretty straightforward, there is a special consideration  
that we can make when multiplying signed vectors together. Note that the only output value that  
requires 8 bits in the evaluation we presented above was when we multiplied ( −8) × (−8) = 64. All 
other output values can be represented using only 7 bits, which has a possible range of −64 to +63. If  
you need to perform an exact signed multiplication, you have no choice but to create an 8-bit output.  
However,  if  the  multiplication  is  being  performed  in  a  digital  signal  processing  chain,  it  is  
advantageous to avoid the bit growth that comes from having an extra bit for the one input case when  
you multiply the two most negative inputs together.
Thus, if you can be assured that you will never multiply the two most negative inputs (one of the inputs 
may be a constant, for example), you can safely do the following:
DOUT <= PRODUCT (6 downto 0);  --  Assuming 4×4 signed vectors
If there is a potential that this one case could exist, you can saturate the output to be one less than the  
maximum, forcing the 64 result to 63, by doing the following:
DOUT <=     PRODUCT (6 downto 0) when (PRODUCT (7) = PRODUCT (6)) else
        not PRODUCT (6 downto 0);  --  Assuming 4×4 signed vectors
This will force the 8 -bit multiplication output result of 64 ( "01000000") to a 7 -bit value of 63  
("0111111"). As was the case with our previous discussion on saturation, this adds some distortion to 
our result, but in most DSP applications the trade-off between accuracy and reducing the bit width of  
our data path is one we are willing to make. Also note that our saturated output is only off by one  
count, so the amount of distortion is minimal.
Looking at the definition of the “ *” operator in the  numeric_std package, we would find that the  
number of output bits returned is always AWIDTH + BWIDTH. We would also find the following overloaded 
versions of the “*” operator:
function "*" (L, R : unsigned) return unsigned;
function "*" (L, R : signed)   return signed;
function "*" (L : unsigned; R : natural)  return unsigned;
function "*" (L : natural;  R : unsigned) return unsigned;
function "*" (L : integer;  R : signed)   return signed;
function "*" (L : signed;   R : integer)  return signed;
Again, there is no operator that will multiply an unsigned type with a signed type. In order to perform  
this operation, you must convert the unsigned vector to a signed vector prior to multiplying the two  
vectors together. Given two 4-bit vectors, one unsigned and one signed, the inputs will range from 0 to 
309

--- Page 329 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
15  and  −8  to  +7;  this  means  that  our  output  will  range  from  −120  to  +105  ("10001000" to  
"01101001"), which requires 8 bits to be fully represented. The following code multiplies a signed 
vector SVEC with an unsigned vector UVEC:
PROD <= resize (SVEC * signed (resize (UVEC, UVEC'length + 1)),
                SVEC'length + UVEC'length);
The following code models a 16 -bit unsigned clocked multiplier with a clock enable, adjusting the  
output to have the same 16-bit precision as the inputs:
  signal A, B    : unsigned (15 downto 0);
  signal DOUT    : unsigned (15 downto 0);
  signal PRODUCT : unsigned (31 downto 0);
  
begin
  ----------------------------------------------
  PRODUCT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ----------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      PRODUCT <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        PRODUCT <= A * B;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  
  DOUT <= PRODUCT (31 downto 16);
If we were performing signed multiplication, the code would be modified as follows:
  signal A, B    : signed (15 downto 0);
  signal DOUT    : signed (15 downto 0);
  signal PRODUCT : signed (31 downto 0);
  
begin
  ----------------------------------------------
  PRODUCT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ----------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      PRODUCT <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        PRODUCT <= A * B;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  
  DOUT <= PRODUCT (31 downto 16);  --  Signed vectors, no saturation
--DOUT <= PRODUCT (30 downto 15);  --  Signed vectors without saturation
--DOUT <=     PRODUCT (30 downto 15) when (PRODUCT (31) = PRODUCT (30)) else
--        not PRODUCT (30 downto 15);  --  Signed vectors with saturation
The choice for which statement you would use to drive DOUT would depend on whether you want full  
accuracy for the result or can ignore the one output that requires DOUT (31) with or without saturation.
310

--- Page 330 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
Multiply/Accumulate
Multiply/accumulate  functions  (MACs)  are  often  used  to  implement  digital  signal  processing  
algorithms. The Xilinx DSP48 block was designed to efficiently implement a MAC operation:
The same caution applies to using the DSP48 block as was the case with using the configuration shift  
register, that being that the flip-flops in the DSP48 block do not contain resets. In order to successfully 
implement a MAC operation in an DSP48 block, the following code should be used:
  signal  A, B    : unsigned (15 downto 0);
  signal  DOUT    : unsigned (15 downto 0);
  signal  PRODUCT : unsigned (31 downto 0);
  signal  SUM     : unsigned (39 downto 0);
  
begin
  -----------------------------------
  MAC_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLEAR = '1') then
        SUM     <= (others => '0');
      elsif (CLKENA = '1') then
        PRODUCT <= A * B;
        SUM     <= SUM + PRODUCT;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  
  DOUT <= SUM (39 downto 24);  --  Using unsigned vectors
There are several things to note about this example. First, we have sized  SUM such that it will not  
overflow as long as we do not accumulate more that 256 products, which results in SUM being 8 bits  
wider than PRODUCT. Obviously, this will need to be adjusted depending on the actual use case of  
your circuit. Second, we are “ripping” the top 16 bits off of SUM to drive DOUT in order to maintain a 
16-bit data path. The indexes used to perform this operation will also need to be adjusted depending on  
your use case. By sizing SUM appropriately, we maintain full bit precision for our accumulator, only  
performing the truncation on the output, which increases the precision of the MAC operation. Finally,  
we have included a CLEAR control to zero out the accumulator before each series of MAC operations  
311
Multiply Accumulate

--- Page 331 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
are performed. If we did not include a provision to clear the accumulator, it would continue to add  
product values forever, which would present a problem in that we cannot properly size SUM to avoid 
overflow, and is generally not a case that would be found in real life.
Synthesizing the code above results in the following logic utilization:
which shows that the entire MAC function is contained within one DSP block, just as we had hoped.
To illustrate how an asynchronous reset affects the implementation of the MAC function, changing the  
process in our example to:
  ------------------------------------------
  MAC_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      PRODUCT <= (others => '0');
      SUM     <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLEAR = '1') then
        PRODUCT <= (others => '0');
        SUM     <= (others => '0');
      elsif (CLKENA = '1') then
        PRODUCT <= A * B;
        SUM     <= SUM + PRODUCT;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
results in the following logic utilization:
Both the register for the multiplier output and the accumulation function are now implemented using  
CLB logic instead of the logic inside the DSP48 block. The only logic implemented inside the DSP48  
block is the multiplication operation.
There is one more consideration we will discuss with respect to digital signal processing and the  
containment  of  bit  growth.  In  the  example  presented  above  we  truncated  the  result,  which  will  
introduce a certain amount of inaccuracy in our result. Indeed, if we were to look at the error statistics  
between the full bit-width output and the truncated output, we would find that the mean error is  
312


--- Page 332 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
approximately ½ with an error variance of 1⁄12. This indicates that we have a positive bias in our answer 
and, worse, have introduced a DC component that is equal to ½ LSB.
A better approach is to round the result instead of performing truncation. This is best accomplished by  
forcing a value of ½ into the accumulator when it is cleared instead of setting it to 0. This results in us  
adding a constant of ½ LSB to DOUT, which produces the equivalent of rounding when the truncation  
operation is performed (for values equal to or greater than ½, truncation “rounds” to the next higher  
integer value, while values less than ½ “round” to the next lower integer value).
How do we add ½ LSB to DOUT? The truncation operation places an implied decimal point to the right 
of the LSB of the truncated result:
Thus, in order to add ½ LSB to DOUT, we would force SUM (23) to '1' and the remaining bits to '0' 
when CLEAR is asserted. Note that we don’t want to add ½ LSB to SUM on each enabled clock cycle  
because that would accumulate ½ LSB during each addition instead of forcing it one time:
  -----------------------------------
  MAC_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLEAR = '1') then
        PRODUCT <= (others => '0');
        SUM     <= (23 => '1', others => '0');
      elsif (CLKENA = '1') then
        PRODUCT <= A * B;
        SUM     <= SUM + PRODUCT;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
The error statistics of our modified MAC output change to a mean error that is approximately equal to  
0 while maintaining the error variance of  1⁄12. This is what is predicted by statistical  analysis of  
rounding operations.
RAM Blocks
Most FPGAs contain dedicated block RAMs, which are used to implement large RAM arrays. In  
Xilinx FPGAs, there are two types of RAM: Distributed RAM, which uses the CLB LUT as a RAM  
and is useful for implementing small RAM blocks, and  Block RAM , which is dedicated memory  
structures that can be used to implement larger RAM blocks. In general, the mapper will automatically  
determine whether a distributed or block RAM should be used to implement the memory.
313
SUM
DOUT
39 24• • • 23 0• • •

--- Page 333 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Distributed RAM
During the discussion of the basic features of CLBs, we mentioned that they can be configured as  
distributed RAMs by allowing the user to access the LUT inputs as the read and write address lines to  
the LUT RAM with a one-bit data input/output. Furthermore, multiple LUT RAMs can be combined  
together to create larger RAMs. For example, a 32×16-bit RAM could be implemented using 16 LUT  
RAMs (one LUT RAM per bit). LUT RAMs can be used in two configurations: asynchronous and  
synchronous reads. The difference is that with an asynchronous read, the output data is available  
immediately after the application of the read address. With a synchronous read, the output data is  
available after a clock edge, which typically means that the read data is delayed by one clock cycle  
after the read address.
A single-port asynchronous read 32×16-bit distributed RAM can be coded as follows:
  type   MEMORY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
  signal RAM   : MEMORY (0 to 31);
  signal INDEX : natural range 0 to 31;
begin
  INDEX <= To_Integer (unsigned (ADDR));
  -----------------------------------
  RAM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (WRENA = '1') then
        RAM (INDEX) <= DIN;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  DOUT <= RAM (INDEX);  --  Asynchronous read
Note that the RAM read is performed using a concurrent signal assignment statement; thus, whenever  
ADDR changes (which forces a change on INDEX), the RAM data is immediately available on DOUT. 
Synthesizing this code generates the following results:
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+
|          Site Type         | Used | Fixed | Available | Util% |
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+
| Slice LUTs                 |   16 |     0 |     63400 |  0.03 |
|   LUT as Logic             |    0 |     0 |     63400 |  0.00 |
|   LUT as Memory            |   16 |     0 |     19000 |  0.08 |
|     LUT as Distributed RAM |   16 |     0 |           |       |
|     LUT as Shift Register  |    0 |     0 |           |       |
| Slice Registers            |    0 |     0 |    126800 |  0.00 |
|   Register as Flip Flop    |    0 |     0 |    126800 |  0.00 |
|   Register as Latch        |    0 |     0 |    126800 |  0.00 |
| F7 Muxes                   |    0 |     0 |     31700 |  0.00 |
| F8 Muxes                   |    0 |     0 |     15850 |  0.00 |
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+
As this table shows, the 32×16-bit RAM was implemented using 16 LUTs, configured as distributed  
LUT RAM.
314

--- Page 334 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
Changing the RAM to a single-port synchronous read distributed RAM can be done as follows:
  type   MEMORY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
  signal RAM   : MEMORY (0 to 31);
  signal INDEX : natural range 0 to 31;
begin
  INDEX <= To_Integer (unsigned (ADDR));
  -----------------------------------
  RAM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (WRENA = '1') then
        RAM (INDEX) <= DIN;
      end if;
      DOUT <= RAM (INDEX);  --  Synchronous read
    end if;
  end process;
Note that the only difference between the two is that the RAM read operation has been moved inside  
the process  and  is now dependent on  a clock  edge to update the output. Synthesizing  this code  
generates the following results:
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+
|          Site Type         | Used | Fixed | Available | Util% |
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+
| Slice LUTs                 |   16 |     0 |     63400 |  0.03 |
|   LUT as Logic             |    0 |     0 |     63400 |  0.00 |
|   LUT as Memory            |   16 |     0 |     19000 |  0.08 |
|     LUT as Distributed RAM |   16 |     0 |           |       |
|     LUT as Shift Register  |    0 |     0 |           |       |
| Slice Registers            |   16 |     0 |    126800 |  0.01 |
|   Register as Flip Flop    |   16 |     0 |    126800 |  0.01 |
|   Register as Latch        |    0 |     0 |    126800 |  0.00 |
| F7 Muxes                   |    0 |     0 |     31700 |  0.00 |
| F8 Muxes                   |    0 |     0 |     15850 |  0.00 |
+----------------------------+------+-------+-----------+-------+
Note that we are now using 16 flip-flops to implement the design, whereas in the first example there  
were no flip-flops used.
Block RAM
Dedicated block RAMs  are used when the RAM array becomes sufficiently large that it is more  
efficient to map the memory to the block RAM structures instead of using distributed RAM. The one  
caveat to this is that block RAM  always performs a synchronous read. If you try to implement an  
asynchronous read RAM it will consume a potentially large amount of LUT RAMs, and so it is in your  
best interest to change your design to support synchronous reads instead.
Code that implements a single-port synchronous read block RAM is exactly the same as for distributed  
RAM. The only difference is the size. Thus, a 1K×8-bit block RAM would be coded as follows:
315

--- Page 335 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  type   MEMORY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal RAM   : MEMORY (0 to 1023);
  signal INDEX : natural range 0 to 1023;
begin
  INDEX <= To_Integer (unsigned (ADDR));
  -----------------------------------
  RAM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (WRENA = '1') then
        RAM (INDEX) <= DIN;
      end if;
      DOUT <= RAM (INDEX);
    end if;
  end process;
This would be implemented using a single RAMB18 cell, which is one half of a full 36-kbit block  
RAM and consumes no CLB logic. Trying to synthesize this using an asynchronous read would result  
in an implementation requiring 140 LUTs, 128 used as distributed RAM cells and 12 used as read and  
write addressing logic. Clearly, it is best to use block RAMs with synchronous reads for large memory  
arrays.
A dual-port RAM is one that uses separate read and write addresses. This is typically found in RAM-
based shift registers and first-in, first-out (FIFO) data buffers. A dual-port 1K×8-bit block RAM is  
implemented using the following code:
  type    MEMORY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal  RAM   : MEMORY (0 to 1023);
  signal  WRPTR : natural range 0 to 1023;
  signal  RDPTR : natural range 0 to 1023;
begin
  WRPTR <= To_Integer (unsigned (WRADDR));
  RDPTR <= To_Integer (unsigned (RDADDR));
  -----------------------------------
  RAM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (WRENA = '1') then
        RAM (WRPTR) <= DIN;
      end if;
      DOUT <= RAM (RDPTR);
    end if;
  end process;
This again would be implemented using a single RAMB18 cell and would consume no CLB logic.
In the examples shown above, the depth and width of the RAMs were powers of two. While the depth  
of a RAM can be any value, by their nature RAM depths must be a power of two, which will be  
extended to a power of two during synthesis if necessary. The width can be any number of bits, subject  
to the practical limit that it must fit into the available RAM resources available on the FPGA. For  
316

--- Page 336 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
example, a 12K×24-bit block RAM would be implemented using 12 RAM36B cells configured as  
16K×2-bit block RAMs.
ROM Blocks
From a conceptual standpoint, ROMs are nothing more than RAMs that have had data preprogrammed  
into them. From a practical standpoint, this is exactly how ROMs are realized in FPGAs: they are  
block RAMs that have been preprogrammed using the configuration bitstream. It should be noted that  
not all FPGA families or FPGA vendors support preprogrammed RAM. In this case the ROM will be  
implemented using LUTs, with the address LSBs connected to the LUT inputs and the address MSBs  
used to select which LUT bank output to pass through. Obviously, the timing of a LUT ROM will be  
slower than the equivalent block ROM; thus, it is imperative that the user check the results of timing  
analysis to guarantee that the circuit will work at the designated clock rate.
To implement a 512×8-bit block ROM, the following code can be used:
  type      ROM_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) std_logic_vector (7 downto 0);
  signal    INDEX : natural range 0 to 511;
  constant  ROM   : ROM_ARRAY := (
           x"FF", x"FF", x"FF", x"FF", x"FF", x"FF", x"00", x"00",
           x"02", x"1C", x"00", x"00", x"00", x"00", x"07", x"11",
           ...
           x"45", x"00", x"00", x"1E", x"00", x"00", x"00", x"00",
           x"FF", x"FF", x"FF", x"FF", x"00", x"00", x"FF", x"FF" );
begin
  INDEX <= To_Integer (unsigned (ADDR));
  -----------------------------------
  ROM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      DOUT <= ROM (INDEX);
    end if;
  end process;
Recall that block RAM reads must be synchronous; therefore, the data out of the ROM must be inside  
a clocked process.
At this point, a reasonable question is how does one create the ROM table. One method would be to  
hand-edit the ROM data, but this is time-consuming and extremely prone to error. A better method is to 
use Excel or Matlab to generate the data and write a small utility program that converts the data into a  
valid VHDL array declaration. This eliminates the chance of error and provides a proven path forward  
in case the data has to change. In addition, this utility program can be used on subsequent designs  
where ROMs are required.
The third method involves computing the table automatically within VHDL, which is most applicable  
to ROM data that is purely mathematical in nature. This method computes the data as real values and 
then scales the data to the proper bit width. This is a case where the built-in IEEE.math_real library can 
be used to create the data values, especially if they involve functions other than the standard arithmetic  
operations.
317

--- Page 337 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
For example, if we wished to create an entity that generated a 16 -bit sine wave that could be generated 
at different frequencies, we could use the sin function to create the data table. We begin by including  
the following library and use statements in the top of our file:
library IEEE;
use     IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
use     IEEE.numeric_std.all;
use     IEEE.math_real.all;
Then, in the architecture we use the following code to create our sine wave generator:
  type ROM_ARRAY is array (0 to 1023) of std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
  function SINE_TABLE return ROM_ARRAY is
    variable SINE  : real;
    variable TABLE : ROM_ARRAY;
  begin
    for I in 0 to 1023 loop
      SINE      := sin (2.0 * MATH_PI * real (I) / 1024.0);
      TABLE (I) := std_logic_vector (To_Signed (integer (32767.0 * SINE), 16));
    end loop;
    return TABLE;
  end function SINE_TABLE;
  signal  TABLE : ROM_ARRAY := SINE_TABLE;
  signal  ADDR  : unsigned (9 downto 0);
begin
  -------------------------------------
  TABLE_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -------------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        DOUT <= TABLE (To_Integer (ADDR));
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  -------------------------------------------
  ADDR_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  -------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      ADDR <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (INIT = '1') then
        ADDR <= (others => '0');
      elsif (CLKENA = '1') then
        ADDR <= ADDR + unsigned (INCR);
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
We begin by defining a 1024 ×16-bit std_logic_vector type named  ROM_ARRAY. Next, we define a  
function that returns a 16-bit sin (x) function table with 1,024 entries for angles of 0 to 2  π - 1 / 1024, 
which we have called SINE_TABLE. The output is scaled to 16 bits by multiplying the sin (x) value by 
32,767; multiplying by 32,768 would result in an overflow when  sin (x) = 1. We then declare our  
sin (x) ROM-based look-up table, named TABLE, and initialize it to the return value of SINE_TABLE. 
Finally, we declare a signal named ADDR, which we use to access the individual values within TABLE.
318

--- Page 338 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
The first process, TABLE_PROCESS, performs the actual ROM look up based on ADDR. The second 
process, ADDR_PROCESS, can be initialized to 0 via INIT or will add the external value INCR in order 
to generate sine waves at different frequencies.
Simulating this circuit generates the following waveform:
As this waveform shows, when  INCR equals 1, the circuit traces out one complete sine wave cycle.  
When INCR equals 2, it traces out two complete sine wave cycles in the same amount of time. Setting 
the value of INCR to 3 and 4 produces similar results.
RAM Design Examples
We will now look at several examples of logic functions that can benefit from being utilized in large  
block RAMs. The advantage of using a block RAM is that the FPGA can implement a large amount of  
flip-flops in an extremely small footprint, freeing up the CLB flip-flops for other logic.
RAM-Based Shift Register
The first example involves using a block RAM  as a large shift register. Consider a design that requires 
a 256-stage by 16-bit shift register. Implemented in flip-flops, that would result in 256  × 16 = 4,096 
flip-flops. Using a block RAM to store the data, we can reduce this down to 26 flip-flops and 11 LUTs, 
which are used to implement a control counter, and one RAMB18 block that stores the actual data  
samples.
We begin by declaring the signals we will use for this design:
  constant  DELAY : natural := 256;
  type      RAM_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
  signal    RAM   : RAM_ARRAY    (0 to DELAY-3);
  signal    COUNT : natural range 0 to DELAY-3;
  signal    RDOUT : std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
  signal    FULL  : std_logic;
  signal    VALID : std_logic;
Note that we have declared a constant called  DELAY that we are using to set the number of clock  
cycles required to shift the data from the input to the output. This is because this value is used  
extensively in the code, and this makes adjusting the delay easy if the need arises. Note also that the  
RAM and the counter modulus are two locations/clock cycles less than the shift register delay: instead  
of defining them from 0 to DELAY−1, we have declared them as 0 to DELAY−3. The reason behind this 
will become evident as we discuss how the RAM-based shift register operates.
319


--- Page 339 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Next, we write the process statements that will be used to model the behavior of our circuit:
  -----------------------------------
  RAM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        RAM (COUNT) <= DIN;
        RDOUT <= RAM (COUNT);
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  --------------------------------------------
  COUNT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <=  0;
      FULL  <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        if (COUNT < DELAY-3) then
          COUNT <= COUNT + 1;
        else
          COUNT <=  0;   --  Reset COUNT to first RAM location on roll-over
          FULL  <= '1';  --  FULL asserts when the last RAM location is written
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
  -------------------------------------------
  DOUT_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  -------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      DOUT  <= (others => '0');
      VALID <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        VALID <= FULL;   --  VALID asserts one CLKENA cycle after FULL
        if (VALID = '1') then
          DOUT <= RDOUT;
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
RAM_PROCESS should look familiar; it simply is a single-port synchronous read RAM that uses  
COUNT as the address. The only difference is that we are taking advantage of the read enable feature  
of the block RAM in this case and only allowing a read if CLKENA is asserted.
COUNT_PROCESS is used to generate the RAM address and uses the count to determine when the  
RAM buffer is full. The first time the counter rolls over after a reset, it asserts FULL, indicating that the 
RAM buffer is full.
DOUT_PROCESS clears  DOUT on reset  and  then asserts  VALID, which is  FULL delayed by one  
enabled clock cycle, to indicate that the read data from the RAM is valid. The RAM output is then  
320

--- Page 340 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
clocked into  DOUT each time there is valid RAM read data by only allowing  DOUT to be clocked  
when VALID = '1'.
In our discussion of using SRL components and a counter to create a shift register with valid outputs  
during the shift register run-up (beginning on page 300), we noted that the shift register needed to be  
shortened by one stage because DOUT was being used as the final stage of the shift register. This is the  
case with this example as well; however, in this example there are two final shift register stages  
consumed by flip-flops. The first stage is the register used for the synchronous RAM read (contained in 
the block RAM), and the second one is DOUT. This is why the RAM is two locations shorter than the  
full number of shift register stages.
Simulating our circuit, we would get the following results:
The simulation begins by clocking in data values of 1, 2, 3, 4, etc. As this simulation shows, the output  
is 0 for the first 255 clock cycles and then starts shifting out 1, 2, 3, 4, etc., indicating that our circuit is  
working as expected.
The utilization report generated after synthesis shows the following:
If we do an estimate of the number of flip-flops required to implement this circuit, we would expect  
that DOUT would consume 16 flip-flops, COUNT would consume 8 flip-flops to represent the count  
range of 0 to 253, and two flip-flops would be needed for FULL and VALID. The expected total number 
of flip-flops is 16 + 8 + 2 = 26, which is what synthesis reports.
RAM-Based FIFO Buffer
The second example we will look at involves using a block RAM  to implement a FIFO buffer . FIFOs 
are usually used for data buffering and flow control between software and hardware. Software can  
store data in the FPGA by writing it into a FIFO, then signal the hardware that the data is available for  
processing.  The hardware then  reads  the data out  of the FIFO.  Two common flags  are used  for  
indicating the status of the FIFO: FULL and EMPTY. FULL is used to indicate to the write side of the  
321


--- Page 341 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
FIFO that no more space is available and that any subsequent writes will be lost. EMPTY is used to  
indicate to the read side that no more data is available and that any subsequent reads will result in  
invalid data.
This circuit would be a good candidate for creating a generic component that we could reuse in future  
designs, so we begin by creating a generic entity for our FIFO:
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  FIFO
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  This entity implements a First-In First-Out data buffer with FULL and EMPTY
--  status outputs. DIN is written into the buffer when WRENA = '1' as long as
--  the buffer is not full. DOUT is read from the buffer when RDENA = '1' as
--  long as the buffer is not empty. The size of the buffer is specified by
--  WIDTH and DEPTH.
library IEEE;
use     IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
--------------
entity FIFO is
-------------- 
  generic ( WIDTH : natural :=  16;                           --  FIFO width (bits)
            DEPTH : natural := 256 );                         --  FIFO depth (size)
  port    ( RESET : in  std_logic;                            --  Active high reset
            CLOCK : in  std_logic;                            --  Master clock
            DIN   : in  std_logic_vector (WIDTH-1 downto 0);  --  Input data
            WRENA : in  std_logic;                            --  Write data request
            DOUT  : out std_logic_vector (WIDTH-1 downto 0);  --  Output data
            RDENA : in  std_logic;                            --  Read data request
            FULL  : out std_logic;                            --  FIFO full
            EMPTY : out std_logic );                          --  FIFO empty
end entity FIFO;
We will write the RTL code for our FIFO using the generics WIDTH and DEPTH to set the size of the  
RAM and the data path. The functional FIFO code within the architecture follows:
---------------------------
architecture RTL of FIFO is
---------------------------
  type    RAM_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of std_logic_vector (WIDTH–1 downto 0);
  signal  RAM : RAM_ARRAY (0 to DEPTH–1);
  signal  WRADDR, RDADDR : natural range 0 to DEPTH–1;  --  RAM read/write address
  signal  RDNEXT, RDHOLD : natural range 0 to DEPTH–1;  --  RAM read addresses
  signal  UNREAD         : natural range 0 to DEPTH;    --  Unread contents of the RAM
  signal  IWRENA, IRDENA : std_logic;                   --  Internal read/write enables
  signal  IEMPTY, IFULL  : std_logic;                   --  Internal empty/full flags
begin
--  Qualify the read and write enables with the empty and full flags and use these
--    signals to generate the internal read and write addresses
  IWRENA <= WRENA and not IFULL;   --  Valid when write requested and not full
  IRDENA <= RDENA and not IEMPTY;  --  Valid when read requested and not empty
322

--- Page 342 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
--  Read/write address generator counters
  -------------------------------------------
  ADDR_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  -------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      RDNEXT <= 1;
      RDHOLD <= 0;
      WRADDR <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (IRDENA = '1') then     --  Increment RDNEXT each time IRDENA is asserted
        if (RDADDR = DEPTH–1)    --  Roll over when RDADDR is at the max address
          then RDNEXT <= 0;
          else RDNEXT <= RDNEXT + 1;
        end if;
        RDHOLD <= RDNEXT;        --  Save current RDNEXT in RDHOLD
      end if;
      if (IWRENA = '1') then     --  Increment WRADDR each time IWRENA is asserted
        if (WRADDR = DEPTH–1)    --  Roll over when WRADDR is at the max address
          then WRADDR <= 0;
          else WRADDR <= WRADDR + 1;
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Read the next RAM address (RDNEXT) value when IRDENA = '1'
--  Otherwise read the current RAM address (RDHOLD)
  RDADDR <= RDNEXT when (IRDENA = '1') else RDHOLD;
--  Count the number of unread locations in the memory
  ---------------------------------------------
  UNREAD_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  ---------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      UNREAD <= 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (IRDENA = '1' and IWRENA = '0') then     --  Read without write:
        UNREAD <= UNREAD – 1;                     --    Decrement UNREAD
      elsif (IRDENA = '0' and IWRENA = '1') then  --  Write without read:
        UNREAD <= UNREAD + 1;                     --    Increment UNREAD
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
323

--- Page 343 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
--  FIFO FULL and EMPTY status flags
  -------------------------------------------
  FLAG_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK, RESET) begin
  -------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      IEMPTY <= '1';
      IFULL  <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (UNREAD = 1) then
        IEMPTY <= IRDENA;  --  Set IEMPTY when a read occurs with UNREAD = 1
      end if;
      if (UNREAD = DEPTH–1) then
        IFULL  <= IWRENA;  --  Set IFULL when a write occurs with UNREAD = DEPTH–1
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Drive the flag output ports with the internal flag signals
  FULL  <= IFULL;
  EMPTY <= IEMPTY;
--  Dual port RAM
  -----------------------------------
  RAM_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (IWRENA = '1') then
        RAM (WRADDR) <= DIN;
      end if;
      DOUT <= RAM (RDADDR);
    end if;
  end process;
end architecture RTL;
The model for the FIFO is fairly complex, which is the reason why there is extensive  use of comments 
in the  code to aid in explaining the intention of each section. For clarity, the following paragraphs  
provide additional details on how the FIFO code works.
IWRENA and IRDENA are the internal versions of the ports WRENA and RDENA and are qualified so  
that a write request to a full FIFO is ignored, as is a read request to an empty FIFO. Either of these  
cases is bad, but we have created a FIFO that has a defined reaction to each of them, which is to ignore  
the requests. Any circuit that instantiates this FIFO component must be aware of this behavior.
ADDR_PROCESS is used to generate the read and write addresses for our RAM array. The code for  
WRADDR is straightforward; the counter is initialized to 0 and counts to a maximum of  DEPTH−1 
before rolling over back to 0. The read address code, on the other hand, is more complicated. Because  
the memory is implemented as a synchronous read RAM, we need to account for the fact that when a  
RDENA occurs, we need to read the next location from the RAM in order for the correct output value  
to be ready in case another RDENA occurs on the next clock cycle. At the same time, if RDENA does 
not occur, we need to continue to read from that location, which will now be the current one. This is 
why there are two  registers used for generating  RDADDR,  a counter that computes  the next read  
address and  a register  to hold the current address. The  registers are defined such that  RDHOLD is  
324

--- Page 344 ---
CHAPTER 17 SPECIALIZED CODE EXAMPLES
always one count less than RDNEXT: RDHOLD is set to RDNEXT as RDNEXT is incremented to the  
next value.  When  RDENA = '1',  RDADDR is  driven  by RDNEXT (the next read  address);  when  
RDENA = '0', RDADDR is driven by RDHOLD (the current read address, which was the next address). 
If you are unsure how the read address is generated in this circuit, it would be good to reread this  
paragraph to gain an understanding on how this works.
UNREAD_PROCESS implements an up/down counter that is used to count the number of unread data 
values in the RAM. We use this counter to detect when to assert the FULL and EMPTY flags. UNREAD 
will be decremented whenever a RDENA occurs without a WRENA and will be incremented whenever  
there is a WRENA without a RDENA. When neither RDENA nor WRENA is asserted or when both are  
asserted, the number of unread  data values  in the RAM does not change, and  UNREAD retains its  
current count value.
FLAG_PROCESS generates the FULL and EMPTY status flags by monitoring when a read occurs with  
a single unread location in the RAM (which sets EMPTY) or when a write occurs with only one “free”  
location left in the RAM (which sets FULL). The process uses two internal signals named IFULL and 
IEMPTY because these status flags are used internally and are also output ports for the entity (recall  
that ports of mode out cannot be read).
Finally,  RAM_PROCESS implements what should now be evident as a dual-port synchronous read  
RAM that is used to store the FIFO data.
Summary
Most FPGA families have specialized logic that can be used to more efficiently implement complex  
circuitry than by using the standard CLB LUTs and flip-flops. Writing your RTL to target these logic  
cells results in a design that fits onto a smaller die area and can potentially save both cost and power if  
the design can be targeted for a smaller FPGA. The designer should always be on the lookout for ways  
to use these specialized logic cells.
325

--- Page 345 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL

CHAPTER 18
STATE MACHINES
In chapters 16 and 17, we presented RTL code that will synthesize into a set of common digital design  
building blocks. One common block that was not  covered was the  state machine ,  which is  a very  
powerful design abstraction that can be used to easily create complex control structures within a digital  
design. However, state machines can also be fraught with danger: an ill-conceived state machine can  
operate erratically or, worse, lock up, requiring the user to perform a power cycle to unlock.
State machines are such an important concept that we will spend an entire chapter discussing the  
design methods that should be used to create a sound design, one that reacts in a predictable fashion  
when the inputs occur unpredictably. Although the concepts discussed in this chapter apply primarily to 
the subject of digital design and not necessarily VHDL, we will show how the design of a state  
machine in VHDL is an extremely easy process, allowing the designer to spend more time creating a  
robust design and less time on the gate-level mechanics of implementing the actual state machine.
State Machine Basics
Before we discuss how to perform a proper state machine design, we first need to review what these  
designs are. State machines implement digital logic systems that track external events that are expected 
to occur in a specific order over time. The state machine itself is a design abstraction that is described  
using  a  finite  number  of  user-defined  states;  thus,  state  machines  are  also  known  as  finite state  
machines (often abbreviated as FSMs), although it is understood that all state machines by necessity  
must contain a finite set of states. A state machine can only be in one state at any given time, which is  
called the current state, and can change from one state to another by an external event or condition.  
This state change is known as a  state transition . The state that the logic transitions to due to this  
external event is known as the next state, at which point the next state becomes the current state. A  
state machine can be completely and uniquely defined by the list of the possible states that the state  
machine can have and the conditions that must be met for each state transition.
The following simple conceptual diagram shows how an actual state machine is implemented in logic:
327

--- Page 347 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
A state machine consists of a set of combinatorial logic gates that compute the next state based on the  
current state and the value of the input signals. Combinatorial logic gates are also used to compute the  
output control signals from the state machine. The current state is held in a set of clocked flip-flops,  
which is updated whenever a state transition occurs.
State machines come in two classic forms: Mealy and Moore. The outputs from a Mealy state machine  
are based on the current state and the value of the input signals:
In contrast, the outputs from a Moore state machine are based solely on the current state:
As we shall see, although there are two classic forms used to describe state machines, most designs  
will in fact use a combination of both by including some outputs that are driven by inputs and state  
values, while other outputs are driven only by the present state value.
The actual encoding of the states within the state machine can take on several different forms, the most  
popular  being  binary,  Gray  code,  and  one-hot.  These  state  encoding methods  are  shown  in  the  
following table:
328
COMBINATORIAL
LOGIC
OUTPUTSINPUTS
REGISTERS

LOGIC
NEXT STATE
LOGIC
INPUTS
OUTPUTS
STATES
DD Q
INPUTS
OUTPUTSNEXT STATE
LOGIC
OUTPUT
LOGIC

DD Q

--- Page 348 ---
CHAPTER 18 STATE MACHINES
BINARY GRAY CODE ONE-HOT
000 000 100000
001 001 010000
010 011 001000
011 010 000100
100 110 000010
101 100 000001
In most designs the best approach for state encoding is one-hot, where the number of flip-flops used to  
encode the states is equal to the number of states in the state machine. This is especially true for  
FPGAs, which have a large number of flip-flops available on the device. The term  one-hot derives 
from the fact that at any given point in time, one of the flip-flops is at a logic '1' while the remaining 
flip-flops are at a logic '0'. One-hot state machines are also usually the best approach because they  
use the least amount of logic required to drive the gates used to drive the next state logic. The logic  
required to decode the binary or Gray code state into a set of unique present state signals, along with  
the logic needed to reencode the next state values, is not necessary if the state machine uses one-hot  
encoding.
In general, designers need not concern themselves with the underlying state encoding method because  
the synthesis engine will automatically pick the best method (in terms of both size and clock speed)  
based on the logic required to implement the state machine along with the surrounding logic that is  
being controlled by the state machine.
State Machine Design
State machines are usually designed and documented using a state transition diagram. Each individual 
state is shown as a bubble on the diagram, with arrows that show the transitions between each of the  
states. Attached to each arrow is the condition that must be met for the state machine to make the  
transition to another state. An example of a state transition diagram is:
329

--- Page 349 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
This diagram is read as follows:
• When RESET is applied, the state machine is set to the IDLE state.
• In the IDLE state, when READY = '1', the state machine transitions to the START state.
• In the START state, the state machine unconditionally transitions to the BUSY state.
• In the BUSY state, when FULL = '1', the state machine transitions to the STOP state.
• In the STOP state, when EMPTY = '1', the state machine transitions to the IDLE state, and the 
entire process is repeated.
Note that transitions that would force a state machine to remain in its current state, such as the one 
shown below based on the value of FULL:
are typically not shown. It is implied that anytime conditions for state transitions are not met, the state  
machine remains in its current state.  This helps de-clutter the state transition diagram and makes it  
easier to compose, read, and maintain.
To give you an understanding of how relatively easy it is to translate a state transition diagram into  
VHDL, the following code would implement a state machine for the state transition diagram shown  
above:
330
FULL = '0'
FULL = '1'

IDLE
START
BUSY
STOP
EMPTY = '1'
READY = '1'
FULL = '1'

  type    STATE_T is ( IDLE, START, BUSY, STOP );
  signal  STATE : STATE_T;
begin
  --------------------------------------------
  STATE_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      STATE <= IDLE;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      case STATE is
        when IDLE  =>  if (READY = '1') then
                         STATE <= START;
                       end if;
        when START =>  STATE <= BUSY;
        when BUSY  =>  if (FULL = '1') then
                         STATE <= STOP;
                       end if;
        when STOP  =>  if (EMPTY = '1') then
                         STATE <= IDLE;
                       end if;
      end case;
    end if;
  end process;
The recommended approach used to design a state machine in VHDL is to use an enumerated data type 
for the state and a  case statement within the process to implement the state transitions. In the code  
above, an enumerated type called STATE_T is defined with the four states of our state machine. This is  
followed by a signal declaration named  STATE that uses our enumerated type. Inside the process, a  
case statement is used to determine when to perform a state transition based on the current state and  
the conditions that must be met to perform that state transition. At this point, it would be good for the  
reader to look at the VHDL code to confirm that it does indeed implement the state machine defined by 
our state transition diagram.
Compare this design method to the traditional process of creating a present state/next state table based  
on the chosen state encoding style. After the table is completed, the designer must determine the logic  
equations used to compute the next state values  via the use of Karnaugh  maps and DeMorgan’s  
Theorem. Using VHDL, none of this is necessary. The state machine can be written quite easily using a 
case statement with nested if statements, and the job of selecting the state encoding values and next  
state logic is left to the synthesis engine to figure out. This saves a huge amount of design time,  
allowing the designer to focus on the “what” instead of the “how,” avoiding the errors that often occur  
using the traditional approach.
Note that just like with the state transition diagram, the transitions in VHDL are not explicitly defined  
when the state machine remains in the same state. This means that there is no need to set the STATE 
signal to the same value as the current state if the condition is not met. Thus, the else clause in the  
following code is unnecessary:
        when BUSY  =>  if (FULL = '1')
                         then STATE <= STOP;
                         else STATE <= BUSY;
                       end if;
331

--- Page 351 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
Oftentimes with state machines, it is a good idea to provide a control that synchronously resets the  
state machine. This is typically done for safety reasons but can also be used to signal that processing is  
complete and the state machine should be reset back to its  IDLE condition. In this case, the state  
machine code is modified as follows:
  type    STATE_T is ( IDLE, START, BUSY, STOP );
  signal  STATE : STATE_T;
begin
  --------------------------------------------
  STATE_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      STATE <= IDLE;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (ENABLE = '0') then
        STATE <= IDLE;
      else
        case STATE is
          when IDLE  =>  if (READY = '1') then
                           STATE <= START;
                         end if;
          when START =>  STATE <= BUSY;
          when BUSY  =>  if (FULL = '1') then
                           STATE <= STOP;
                         end if;
          when STOP  =>  if (EMPTY = '1') then
                           STATE <= IDLE;
                         end if;
        end case;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
This code adds an ENABLE signal that synchronously forces the state machine into the IDLE state when 
it equals '0'; otherwise, the state machine is allowed to operate as designed. This is indicated by the  
ENABLE signal on the RESET arrow at the top of the diagram.
Given the example above, it is very easy to design a state machine using the following steps:
1. Determine the states that are needed to solve the problem at hand.
2. Draw the state transition diagram.
3. Translate the state transition diagram into VHDL using an enumerated data type and case 
statement.
The following guidelines are provided to help ensure that the state machine will be designed and  
implemented successfully in logic.
First, use “action” words for the state names. In the example above, the states were named  IDLE, 
START,  BUSY, and  STOP. From these state names, the designer has a pretty good idea  of  what is 
supposed to happen in the surrounding logic when the state machine  is  in  each of  these states.  The 
worst-case scenario in naming states would be to call them STATE1, STATE2, STATE3, and STATE4. 
First, from a design standpoint, it is difficult to keep track of what action is supposed to take place in  
each state, and the possibility of choosing the wrong state to perform an action is greatly increased. In  
332

--- Page 352 ---
CHAPTER 18 STATE MACHINES
addition, from a maintainability standpoint, if it is determined that a state needs to be inserted into the  
state machine, say a circuit initialization step between  IDLE and  START, either the serial manner of  
numbering is broken—STATE1 would feed STATE5 (the initialization state), which then transitions to  
STATE2—or the designer will need to globally change the state names within the design— STATE4 is 
changed to STATE5, STATE3 is changed to STATE4, etc.—before adding the new STATE2. In either  
case, this is far from the ideal method of maintaining a state machine.
Second, try to limit the number of states in a state machine to less than 10 to 15. As the number of  
states increases, the state transition diagram becomes harder to draw. Consequently, the state machine  
itself becomes harder to debug and maintain. If you find that your state machine has outgrown this  
recommendation, it would be wise to  refactor your original  state machine using  2 or more state  
machines to perform the task.
Third, counters are often used to control state machine transitions. They are typically used to count  
data samples, clock cycles, etc., which will hold the state machine in its present state until the required  
number of events have occurred. In this case it is best to place both the state machine and the counter  
inside the same process, which will aid in readability and maintainability. This is a further example of  
refactoring: without the counter, the state machine would work just fine, but there would need to be  
many more states to keep track of the  delays. For example, consider the following state machine,  
which uses a multi-modulo down counter to delay the transition between the START and CHECK states 
as well as between the RUN and IDLE states:
This state machine can be written in VHDL as follows:
333
IDLE
START
CHECK
RUN COUNT = 0
READY = '1'
DETECT = '1'
RESET
ENABLE
COUNT = 0
DETECT = '0'
Initialize COUNT 
to 3 on entry
Initialize COUNT to 
SIZE-1 on entry

--- Page 353 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  type    STATE_T is ( IDLE, START, CHECK, RUN );
  signal  STATE : STATE_T;
  signal  COUNT : natural range 0 to 255;
begin
  --------------------------------------------
  STATE_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      STATE <= IDLE;
      COUNT <=    0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (ENABLE = '0') then
        STATE <= IDLE;
        COUNT <=    0;
      else
        case STATE is
          when IDLE  =>  if (READY = '1') then
                           STATE <= START;
                           COUNT <=     3;
                         end if;
          when START =>  if (COUNT > 0)
                           then COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
                           else STATE <= CHECK;
                         end if;
          when CHECK =>  if (DETECT = '0')
                           then STATE <= IDLE;
                           else STATE <= RUN;
                                COUNT <= To_Integer (unsigned (SIZE) – 1);
                         end if;
          when RUN   =>  if (READY = '1') then
                           if (COUNT > 0)
                             then COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
                             else STATE <= IDLE;
                           end if;
                         end if;
        end case;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
This state machine performs the following function:
• When RESET is applied, or ENABLE = '0', the state machine is forced into the IDLE state.
• In the IDLE state, when READY = '1', the state machine transitions to the START state, and the 
counter is set to 3.
• In the START state, the state machine will transition to the CHECK state after four clock cycles 
(the counter will decrement from 3 to 0 before the transition is allowed to occur).
• In the CHECK state, if DETECT = '0', the state machine transitions to the IDLE state; otherwise, 
the state machine transitions to the RUN state, and the counter is set to SIZE−1.
• In the RUN state, each time READY occurs, the counter is allowed to decrement. When it equals 0, 
the state machine will transition to the IDLE state. This has the effect of processing a number of 
requests equal to SIZE (the counter will decrement from SIZE−1 to 0 before the transition is 
allowed to occur).
334

--- Page 354 ---
CHAPTER 18 STATE MACHINES
The following waveforms show this state machine in action. First, READY is asserted, but DETECT is 
held low. We expect the state machine to transition from IDLE to START to CHECK and then back to  
IDLE:
Next, we reassert READY with DETECT held high. We expect the state machine to now transition from 
IDLE to START to RUN, and we load the value of 239 into the counter ( "11110000" equals 240; we  
load one less into the counter):
We can observe that the counter decrements each time REQUEST is asserted, and at the end, we would 
expect the state machine to transition back to IDLE when the 240th REQUEST occurs, which is indeed 
what happens:
Inputs and Outputs
Previously we mentioned Mealy versus Moore state machines and that the outputs of a Mealy state  
machine were based on the current state and the value of the input signals, as opposed to the outputs of  
a Moore state machine, which were based solely on the current state. Furthermore, it was stated that  
most designs will use a combination of both types of state machine when it comes to how the outputs  
are driven. An example that shows this follows.
Consider a modification  to  the design in our previous example, in  which  we want  to  provide a  
REQUEST signal in order to signal that the circuit can accept data. This provides two-directional  
335


--- Page 355 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
handshake control  (a  REQUEST output and a  READY input), which indicates when data should be  
transferred into the circuit. The data source still provides the  READY signal, indicating that data is  
ready to be transferred, while the circuit itself provides the REQUEST signal back to the data source,  
indicating that it can accept data. Data is only transferred between the two blocks when both the  
READY and REQUEST lines are asserted. This concept is shown in the following diagram:
The intended functionality of our example circuit is as follows:
• When the circuit is in the IDLE state, it can accept data and asserts REQUEST. The state machine 
will stay in this state waiting for the first data sample, indicated by the assertion of READY.
• The circuit then transitions to the START state and performs a four-clock cycle initialization task, 
during which time the REQUEST line is held low.
• The circuit then transitions to the CHECK state, during which time the REQUEST line continues to 
be held low.
• If the circuit transitions to the RUN state, it reasserts REQUEST to accept the data samples, again 
indicated by the assertion of READY.
The REQUEST signal can simply be defined using a conditional signal assignment, which is a Moore-
type output since it depends only on the current state:
  REQUEST <= '1' when (STATE = IDLE or STATE = RUN) else '0';
Meanwhile, we would like to create an internal clock enable signal that we can use to clock the data  
into the processing chain of our circuit. This clock enable signal is asserted whenever both REQUEST 
and READY are asserted, as follows:
  CLKENA  <= READY when (STATE = IDLE or STATE = RUN) else '0';
This signal is a Mealy-type output since it depends on not only the current state but also the value of  
the READY input. Note that we cannot define CLKENA using the statement:
  CLKENA  <= READY and REQUEST;
because REQUEST is an output of our circuit, and ports of mode out cannot be read.
If we employ both of these statements in our design, we would then have a mixed Mealy-Moore state  
machine. Simulating our circuit with the two outputs:
336
DATA
SOURCE CIRCUIT
DATA
READY
REQUEST


--- Page 356 ---
CHAPTER 18 STATE MACHINES
We see that the  REQUEST and  CLKENA signals work as expected. This example shows that the  
designer should define the state machine output signal assignments based on the desired behavior.
One  caution  to  be  aware  of  is  that  any  state  machine  output  driven  by  concurrent  logic  could  
potentially be glitchy. This is not a problem if the output signal is used as an indirect action signal  
clocked by the same clock as the state machine. It is a problem, however, if the state machine output is  
used as a direct action signal (set, reset, or clock). One should never use a state machine output as a  
direct action signal. Any direct action function can always be rewritten using an indirect action signal,  
such as using the output as a synchronous reset versus an asynchronous reset.
Another caution involves the inputs to the state machine. In order for the state machine to be “bullet-
proof” and work over a variety of input conditions, the input signals should be in the same clock  
domain as the state machine . In other words, all of the state machine input signals should be traced  
back to flip-flops that are clocked by the same clock as the state machine. The reason for this is that the 
logic equations used to compute the next state will probably have different delays because of the  
variations in the complexity of the equations and the delays due to routing:
If the input signal were to change near the active edge of the state machine clock, it is possible that the  
top flip-flop would not change while the bottom one would. This difference in delays would result in  
the state machine entering an illegal state, which would cause it to act erratically. Even worse, if the  
state machine uses one-hot encoding, we could end up with all of the state flip-flops being cleared,  
which is in reality no state. In this case, the state machine would lock up and require a reset to clear out 
this nonstate condition. Therefore, the rule of thumb is that all of the state machine inputs must be in 
the same clock domain as the state machine itself.
It must be mentioned that this type of error condition cannot be detected during the normal process of  
performing a functional simulation of an RTL design. The reason for this is that the simulation uses  
337
LOGIC
EQUATIONS
(long delay)
LOGIC
EQUATIONS
(short delay)
STATE F/F
STATE F/F
INPUT


--- Page 357 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
delta delays to compute the logic output, which as we have seen consumes no physical time. In the  
example above, if the lower logic needs only a single signal assignment (one delta delay) while the top  
one is computed using a maze of concurrent signal assignments and conditional sequential statements  
(tens of delta delays), both paths still consume the same amount of physical time (remember, there is  
an infinite number of delta delays for each unit of physical time). This means that the outputs from the  
logic  equations  occur simultaneously,  and  thus  the error  condition  will  not  occur.  Therefore,  the  
designer must adhere to best design practices, making sure that the inputs are in the same clock domain 
as the state machine.
Design Example
We will now present an example of a real-world state machine design involving a Serial Peripheral  
Interface (SPI) block . This interface is used to read and write register values from a processor to a  
digital circuit using a simple four-wire connection and is a common interface on microprocessors,  
microcontrollers, and digital signal processors. The signal definition for a SPI interface is:

Serial CLocK

The connection between the two devices using this interface is:
The typical transfer of data between the two devices looks like:
The interface specification for the data transfers are as follows:
• SS, MOSI, and MISO are stable on the rising edge of SCLK.
• MOSI and MISO are valid only when SS = '0'.
• SS is high for at least one SCLK cycle between data transfers.

• The number of bits transferred on MOSI and MISO during each transaction and the format of the 
data transferred are implementation-dependent.
In this example, the interface definition is that there are 24 bits transferred on each SPI transaction. The 
format of the MOSI data is the first bit is a write enable bit: data is written when the bit equals '1', and 
data is read back when the bit equals '0'. This is followed by a 7 -bit field that specifies the register  
address that is being accessed. Finally, on a write transaction, the last 16 bits contain the data being  
written  to  the FPGA,  and  the  MISO line echos  the entire  24 -bit  command  to  provide loop-back  
debugging capability. When data is being read the last 16 bits on MOSI are ignored, and the MISO line 
contains the read data value. Thus, to write 0x1234 to address 6, the command on  MOSI would be  
0x861234, which would be echoed back on MISO. To read the contents of address 4, the command  
would be 0x040000 and the data on  MISO would be 0x04DCBA if the data value of address 4 was  
0xDCBA. Pictorially, the interface looks like:
Given the definition for our SPI interface, we can create a state machine with the following states:
• IDLE. Waits until the start of a serial transaction, detected when SS = '0', and saves the 
read/write bit.
• ADDR. Collects the 7 address bits.
• DATA. Collects the 16 write data bits or responds with the 16 read data bits.
• DONE. Executes the write command (if the first bit transferred is a '1') and then waits until 
SS = '1' before going back to IDLE.
The state transition diagram for our state machine is:

While this appears to be a reasonable approach to our problem, note what would happen if the FPGA  
powered up during a SPI transaction. The state machine would assume that a transaction was starting  
because SS = '0' and begin collecting address and data bits, when in reality the bits the FPGA is  
receiving are being interpreted incorrectly; that is, we have missed one or more bits at the beginning of  
the transaction because the FPGA powered up in the middle of the command.
The solution for this is to detect a falling edge on SS instead of simply detecting a level. This is best  
accomplished by having RESET force the state machine to a new state, named INIT. Then, if SS = '1', 
the state machine will transition to the  IDLE state, and the remainder of the state machine would  
operate as shown above. This guarantees that the state machine will not launch into a command  
reception unless there is a '1' on SS before we detect a '0', and thus we properly detect a falling  
edge on SS.
Furthermore, if  SS were to rise back to a  '1' unexpectedly, the state machine should ignore the  
transaction because it does not match our expectations. This also applies if  SS stays at  '0' after a  
transaction: we again should ignore the transaction. Adding both of these conditions on our state  
machine helps to make sure that the circuit doesn’t write incorrect data when the interface does not  
follow the expected behavior. The state machine is modified as follows to add these additional state  
transitions:
Before reading any further, mentally walk through the state transition diagram and confirm that the  
state machine properly handles any unexpected values on SS and also only transitions from the IDLE 
state on the falling edge of SS, including when transitioning from the DONE state.
Our state machine VHDL code is easy to write given this state transition diagram:

  -------------------------------------------
  STATE_PROCESS:  process (RESET, SCLK) begin
  -------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      STATE <= INIT;
      COUNT <=    0;
    elsif (SCLK'event and SCLK = '1') then
      case STATE is
        when INIT =>  if (SSN = '1') then
                        STATE <= IDLE;
                      end if;
        when IDLE =>  if (SSN = '0') then
                        STATE <= ADDR;
                        COUNT <=    6;
                      end if;

                        then STATE <= IDLE;

                        then COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
                        else STATE <= DATA;
                             COUNT <=   15;
                      end if;
