STALL
FULL = '1'
RESET
(INPUT) TVALID = '0' and
(OUTPUT) TREADY = '1'
(INPUT) TVALID = '1' and
(OUTPUT) TREADY = '0'(OUTPUT) TREADY = '1'

--- Page 411 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
CLKENA <= DIN_TVALID  when (STATE = FILL)  else  --  DIN_TVALID  and DIN_TREADY
          DOUT_TREADY when (STATE = STALL) else  --  DOUT_TVALID and DOUT_TREADY
          DIN_TVALID and DOUT_TREADY;
One issue that arises out of the philosophy of maintaining a full internal pipeline at all times is that  
when the state machine transitions to the STALL state, an input transfer will take place, but the pipeline  
does not shift ( CLKENA will equal  '0' when this condition occurs). We therefore need to save the  
input sample in a holding register until the output transfer occurs and the pipeline is allowed to shift.  
This is accomplished by the following code:
-------------------------------------------
HOLD_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
-------------------------------------------
  if (RESET = '1') then
    HOLD <= (others => '0');
  elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
    if (STATE = RUN) then
      if (DIN_TVALID = '1' and DOUT_TREADY = '0') then  --  STALL condition
        HOLD <= DIN;                                    --  Save DIN in HOLD
      end if;
    end if;
  end if;
end process;
DATA <= HOLD when (STATE = STALL) else DIN;
Instead of using the port signal  DIN to feed the front end of the pipeline, we use the internal signal  
DATA. The holding register value will be used to drive the pipeline when the state machine is in the  
STALL state and will allow the DIN signal to drive the pipeline in all other cases.
Using an interface such as this to provide two-directional handshaking makes the component much  
more adaptable to applications with different input and output data rates, which raises the likelihood  
that it will be reused in a variety of applications.
Design Example
We will now present an example of creating a fully reusable function by converting the fixed 12 -bit 
10-tap boxcar filter design of chapter  20 into a generic component  using the techniques we have  
outlined above. Instead of having fixed values for the filter length and data path width, a general-
purpose reusable function would allow the designer to change these parameters quickly through the use 
of generics. We can modify the entity declaration as follows, using BITS and TAPS to set the data path  
width and filter length:
----------------
entity BOXCAR is
---------------- 
  generic ( BITS   : natural := 12;                            --  Data path width
            TAPS   : natural := 10 );                          --  Filter length
  port    ( RESET  : in  std_logic;                            --  Active high reset
            CLOCK  : in  std_logic;                            --  Master clock
            CLKENA : in  std_logic;                            --  Data clock enable
            DIN    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);   --  Data sample input
            VALID  : out std_logic;                            --  Data output valid
            DOUT   : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0));  --  Data output
end entity BOXCAR;
392

--- Page 412 ---
CHAPTER 21 DESIGN REUSE
We will first address the simpler task of converting the filter length into one that depends on the TAPS 
generic. Looking at the code on page  383, we can modify each reference to the filter length by  
replacing  the  fixed  value  10 with  the  TAPS generic.  We  first  look  at  the  signal  and  constant  
declarations in our architecture and modify them as follows:
  signal    SREG  : SHIFT_ARRAY (1 to 10);    --  Data sample shift register
  signal    SUM   : signed (15 downto 0);     --  Data sample accumulator
  signal    DIFF  : signed (15 downto 0);     --  Data sample difference
  signal    AVG   : signed (31 downto 0);     --  Average data sample value
  signal    COUNT : natural range 0 to 10;    --  Control counter
  constant  SCALE : signed := To_Signed (32768 / 10, 16);  --  Output scalar
  signal    SREG  : SHIFT_ARRAY (1 to TAPS);  --  Data sample shift register
  signal    SUM   : signed (15 downto 0);     --  Data sample accumulator
  signal    DIFF  : signed (15 downto 0);     --  Data sample difference
  signal    AVG   : signed (31 downto 0);     --  Average data sample value
  signal    COUNT : natural range 0 to TAPS;  --  Control counter
  constant  SCALE : signed := To_Signed (32768 / TAPS, 16);  --  Output scalar
Looking  through  the  code,  we  see  that  we  will need  to  modify  the  shift  register  assignment  in  
SREG_PROCESS:
SREG <= signed (DATA) & SREG (1 to 9);
SREG <= signed (DATA) & SREG (1 to TAPS-1);
the final shift register index for the DIFF assignment in SUM_PROCESS:
DIFF <= resize (signed (DATA), 16) - resize (SREG (10), 16);
DIFF <= resize (signed (DATA), 16) - resize (SREG (TAPS), 16);
and the initialization value for COUNT and the test for when to generate VALID in VALID_PROCESS:
if (RESET = '1') then
  COUNT <= 10;
  VALID <= '0';
elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
  if (CLKENA = '1' and COUNT > 0) then
    COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
  end if;
  if (COUNT < 9) then
    VALID <= CLKENA;
  end if;
end if;
if (RESET = '1') then
  COUNT <= TAPS;
  VALID <= '0';
elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
  if (CLKENA = '1' and COUNT > 0) then
    COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
  end if;
  if (COUNT < TAPS-1) then
    VALID <= CLKENA;
  end if;
end if;
393

--- Page 413 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
These very minor changes to our boxcar filter changes it into one with a generic number of taps.
We next turn to modifying the data path width using the  BITS generic. Recall from our  numerical 
analysis that given 12 -bit input samples that range from −2,048 to 2,047, performing an addition over  
10 samples will result in a sum that could range from −20,480 to 20,470, which requires 16 bits. The  
bit width of the accumulator is therefore dependent upon both the port width and the number of filter  
taps. Performing a numerical analysis on various filter lengths, we find that the internal data width for  
the accumulator needs to be:
BITS + ceil (log 2 (TAPS))
These  two  functions  are  not  part  of  the  normal  VHDL  distribution,  but  they  do  exist  in  the  
IEEE.math_real package, so we can assign the width of the accumulator to a constant named WIDTH 
and adjust the internal data path with this constant:
  constant  WIDTH : natural := BITS + natural (ceil (log (real (TAPS), 2.0)));
  type      SHIFT_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of signed (BITS-1 downto 0);
  signal    SREG   : SHIFT_ARRAY (1 to TAPS);     --  Data sample shift register
  signal    SUM    : signed (WIDTH-1  downto 0);  --  Data sample accumulator
  signal    DIFF   : signed (WIDTH-1  downto 0);  --  Data sample difference
  signal    AVG    : signed (WIDTH+15 downto 0);  --  Average data sample value
  signal    COUNT  : natural range 0 to TAPS;     --  Control counter
Alternately, we could define a function within our architecture that computes WIDTH, as follows:
--  
--  Integer logarithm function:  Returns int (ceil (log (N)))
--                                                     2
  function ILOG (N : positive) return natural is    --  +-------+----------+
    variable I, R : natural;                        --  |   N   | ILOG (N) |
  begin                                             --  +-------+----------+
    I := 1;  R := 0;                                --  |   2   |    1     |
    while (N > I) loop                              --  |  3-4  |    2     |
      I := I * 2;                                   --  |  5-8  |    3     |
      R := R + 1;                                   --  |  9-16 |    4     |
    end loop;                                       --  | 17-32 |    5     |
    return R;                                       --  | 33-64 |   etc    |
  end function ILOG;                                --  +-------+----------+
and use ILOG to define the WIDTH constant:
  constant  WIDTH : natural := BITS + ILOG (TAPS);
Either method works, and which one to use is ultimately up to the designer. For the remainder of this  
discussion, we will use the functions from the IEEE.math_real package.
The reader may be curious as to why we can use the while … loop form of the loop statement in this  
function. Recall from our discussion on page  111 that loops in synthesizable code must be static at  
compile time. Furthermore, the only form of the loop statement that is static is the for … loop form 
with an iteration range specification using constants or generics. This is where the concept of static  
values becomes somewhat complex. In this case the argument to the ILOG function is a generic, which  
by definition is a static value. Passing this static value into the ILOG function means that the number of 
iterations of the while loop can be determined at compile time. Since the ILOG function is implicitly  
declared as a pure function, there is a 1:1 correspondence between the input value and the value that is  
394

--- Page 414 ---
CHAPTER 21 DESIGN REUSE
returned. Therefore, calling ILOG with a static input value produces a unique static return value. The  
end result is that the code can be optimized during compilation by replacing the function call with the  
return value of the function. Note that if we had attempted to pass a nonstatic value into the  ILOG 
function, the number of iterations of the while loop would not be deterministic at compile time and  
thus would not be synthesizable.
The two modifications we need to make to the RTL are to change the size that the resize function uses 
to compute DIFF, as follows:
  if (COUNT = 0)
    then DIFF <= resize (signed (DATA), 16) - resize (SREG (TAPS), 16);
    else DIFF <= resize (signed (DATA), 16);
  end if;
  if (COUNT = 0)
    then DIFF <= resize (signed (DATA), WIDTH) - resize (SREG (TAPS), WIDTH);
    else DIFF <= resize (signed (DATA), WIDTH);
  end if;
and the bits we select from AVG to drive the DOUT output:
  DOUT <= std_logic_vector (AVG (26 downto 15));
  DOUT <= std_logic_vector (AVG (BITS+14 downto 15));
The complete modified architecture is:
-----------------------------
architecture RTL of BOXCAR is
-----------------------------
  constant  WIDTH : natural := BITS + natural (ceil (log (real (TAPS), 2.0)));
  type      SHIFT_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of signed (BITS-1 downto 0);
  signal    SREG  : SHIFT_ARRAY (1 to TAPS);     --  Data sample shift register
  signal    SUM   : signed (WIDTH-1  downto 0);  --  Data sample accumulator
  signal    DIFF  : signed (WIDTH-1  downto 0);  --  Data sample difference
  signal    AVG   : signed (WIDTH+15 downto 0);  --  Average data sample value
  signal    COUNT : natural range 0 to TAPS;     --  Control counter
  constant  SCALE : signed := To_Signed (32768 / TAPS, 16);  --  Output scalar
begin
--  Data sample shift register (shift the data from left to right)
  ------------------------------------
  SREG_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SREG <= signed (DIN) & SREG (1 to TAPS-1);
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
395

--- Page 415 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
--  Take the sum of the values in SREG by accumulating the difference
--    between the data shifting in and the data shifting out
--  Accumulate DIN until the shift register fills with valid data
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM  <= (others => '0');
      DIFF <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SUM <= SUM + DIFF;
        if (COUNT = 0)
          then DIFF <= resize (signed (DIN), WIDTH) - resize (SREG (TAPS), WIDTH);
          else DIFF <= resize (signed (DIN), WIDTH);
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Take the average of the overall sum
  -----------------------------------
  AVG_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        AVG <= SUM * SCALE;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Select the integer result
  DOUT <= std_logic_vector (AVG (BITS+14 downto 15));
--  VALID delay counter
--  Reclock CLKENA for better timing downstream
  --------------------------------------------
  VALID_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= TAPS;
      VALID <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1' and COUNT > 0) then
        COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
      end if;
      if (COUNT < TAPS-1) then
        VALID <= CLKENA;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
end architecture RTL;
We now need to modify the test bench for  our generic boxcar filter to take advantage of the BITS and 
TAPS generics. However, before we modify the test bench, it is a good idea to resimulate the new RTL  
model with BITS set to 12 and TAPS set to 10 using the existing test bench. This way, we can isolate  
396

--- Page 416 ---
CHAPTER 21 DESIGN REUSE
any problems to the changes to the RTL instead of having to debug both the RTL and the test bench.  
Although not necessary, a fairly easy change we can make to the test bench is to update the component  
declaration and instantiation statements with the new generics and define two constants that the test  
bench will use to drive these generics:
  component BOXCAR is
    generic ( BITS   : natural := 12;
              TAPS   : natural := 10 );
    port    ( RESET  : in  std_logic;
              CLOCK  : in  std_logic;
              CLKENA : in  std_logic;
              DIN    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);
              VALID  : out std_logic;
              DOUT   : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0));
  end component BOXCAR;
  constant  BITS   : natural := 12;
  constant  TAPS   : natural := 10;
begin
-------------------
--  Unit Under Test
-------------------
  UUT:  BOXCAR
    generic map ( BITS   => BITS,
                  TAPS   => TAPS )
    port map    ( RESET  => RESET,
                  CLOCK  => CLOCK,
                  CLKENA => CLKENA,
                  DIN    => DIN,
                  VALID  => VALID,
                  DOUT   => DOUT );
We rerun the simulation, which passes, indicating that our conversion of the RTL was successful. We  
can now turn our attention to modifying the test bench to take full advantage of the BITS and TAPS 
generics. The first modification is to the input data driver, where we will change to use the RANDOM 
function we developed during the discussion of constrained random testing on page 195:
---------------------
--  Input Data Driver
---------------------
  process
    variable  LFSR : std_logic_vector (1 to 23) := "011" & x"C0DED";
--  Generate a CCITT O.151 test pattern
    impure function RANDOM (SIZE : natural) return std_logic_vector is
      variable RTN : std_logic_vector (1 to SIZE);
    begin
      for I in 1 to SIZE loop
        LFSR    := (LFSR (18) xor LFSR (23)) & LFSR (1 to 22);
        RTN (I) :=  LFSR (1);
      end loop;
      return RTN;
    end function RANDOM;
397

--- Page 417 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
  begin
    DIN <= (others => '0');
    wait for 2 * TCLK;
    wait until CLOCK = '1';
    while not DONE loop
      DIN <= RANDOM (BITS);
      loop
        wait until CLOCK  = '1';
        exit when  CLKENA = '1';
      end loop;
    end loop;
    wait;
  end process;
We next need to modify the output monitor, which upon inspection is only dependent upon the number 
of filter taps. While the change to the output monitor process is straightforward, an issue that must be  
addressed is that we used integer data types within the process. This will work as long as the internal  
data path of our filter does not exceed 32 bits. Since both  BITS and  TAPS are now generics, the  
potential for an overflow of the integer data type is a real possibility depending on the settings of those 
generics. For example, BITS and TAPS could be set to a data path width of 24 and a filter length of  
1024, which would result in an internal data width of 34 bits. While we won’t comment on the  
practicality of such a filter, it certainly could be configured as such,  and we will therefore need to  
convert the variable types used in the output monitor process.
There  are  two  different  variable  types  that  will  work  in  this  situation:  real and  signed vectors.  
Generally,  using  real is  the  preferred  data  type,  but  the designer  needs  to  be aware of a pitfall  
associated with this type. A single precision real (one that covers the range of −1.0e38 to +1.0e38) only 
has 24 bits of precision. This means that while the range of values for a real far exceeds that of an  
integer (−2.1e9 to +2.1e9), it is at the expense of numerical precision and in fact has less precision than 
that of an integer data type. On the other hand, a double precision real (one that covers the range of  
−1.0e308 to +1.0e308) has 53 bits of precision, which is much greater than that of the  integer data 
type. The VHDL standard is silent as to whether reals are single or double precision, and the only way  
to tell is to look at the source code for the standard library, typically contained in a file named  
standard.vhd, to see what the range definition is for the real data type. The good news is that most  
modern simulators use double precision for real data types, but the designer must make sure that using 
a real data type results in an increase in numerical precision, not a decrease.
The modified output monitor is:
398

--- Page 418 ---
CHAPTER 21 DESIGN REUSE
------------------
--  Output Monitor
------------------
  process (RESET, CLOCK) is
    type      REAL_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of real;
    constant  DEPTH : natural := 16;
    variable  SREG  : REAL_ARRAY   (1 to TAPS);
    variable  FIFO  : REAL_ARRAY   (0 to DEPTH-1);
    variable  RDPTR : natural range 0 to DEPTH-1;
    variable  WRPTR : natural range 0 to DEPTH-1;
    variable  SUM   : real;
    variable  CHECK : real;
  begin
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SREG  := (others => 0.0);
      RDPTR := 0;
      WRPTR := 0;
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then  --  Compute the expected result and store in the FIFO
        SREG := real (To_Integer (signed (DIN))) & SREG (1 to TAPS-1);
        SUM  := SREG (1);
        for I in 2 to TAPS loop
          SUM := SUM + SREG (I);
        end loop;
        FIFO (WRPTR) := SUM / real (TAPS);
        WRPTR := (WRPTR + 1) mod DEPTH;
      end if;
      if (VALID = '1') then   --  Compare DOUT to the expected result in the FIFO
        CHECK := real (To_Integer (signed (DOUT))) - FIFO (RDPTR);
        assert (abs (CHECK) < 2.0) report "Incorrect DOUT value" severity error;
        RDPTR := (RDPTR + 1) mod DEPTH;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
We can now resimulate our circuit using the new test bench—still using BITS set to 12 and TAPS set to 
10—with the expectation that any errors that occur now are the result of changes to the test bench. We  
find that the simulation passes, indicating that the changes to the test bench were successful.
With our new generic circuit and test bench, the last step in proving out the logic is simulating it with a  
variety of BITS and TAPS settings. At this point, we may discover that there may be missing references 
to the generics in either the RTL or test bench, which we need to be mindful of in case the simulation  
fails:
BITS TAPS RESULT
8 10 Pass
8 20 Pass
10 20 Pass
10 40 Pass
16 10 Fail
399

--- Page 419 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
We have found that for the case where BITS equals 16 and TAPS equals 10, the simulation fails. When 
we debug this test case, we see that the difference between DOUT and the expected value computed by 
the test bench is now off by as many as ±4 counts. An analysis of the algorithm we implemented in the 
RTL reveals why this is so. We are dividing the sum of the input samples by the reciprocal of the  
number of taps in the filter, with a fixed 16 -bit width for SCALE. However, the precision of SCALE is 
actually less than the full 16 bits. Consider the original circuit with 10 taps: the value for the SCALE 
constant was set to 0000110011001100, which in reality is a 13-bit value with 3 extra sign bits:
Note that the precision of a mathematical operation result is always equal to the lowest precision of the  
operator’s inputs. Therefore, given the precision of SCALE (13 bits) and the precision of our data path  
width (12 bits), the precision of the multiplier output is set by the data path width, and we got lucky. In  
the case where  BITS equals 16, the precision of the multiplier output is instead set by the  SCALE 
constant (13 bits), which explains why we are off by ±4. We can confirm this behavior by simulating  
our circuit with BITS set to 13, which passes, while changing BITS to 14 causes the simulation to fail.
The solution to this problem is to make sure that the SCALE constant has at least the same precision as  
our data path width. If we look at the computed values for SCALE across various settings for TAPS, we 
see that the number of sign bits in SCALE is equal to internal bit growth of the accumulator, that is,  
ceil (log 2 (TAPS)):

This means that SCALE has to have the same number of bits as WIDTH. This is best accomplished by  
computing SCALE using a signed vector. Because we cannot represent the fractional value +1.0 using  
a  signed  vector  (the  most  positive  value  for  a  16 -bit  vector  is  0x7FFF,  which  is  
32767 / 32768 = 0.999969),  we  will  instead  compute  a  vector  representing  −1.0  (which  we  can 
represent in a signed vector). We then compute SCALE by dividing −1 by − TAPS to obtain 1 / TAPS, 
as follows:
  constant  DIV   : signed (WIDTH-1 downto 0) := '1' & To_Signed (0, WIDTH-1);
  constant  SCALE : signed := DIV / (-TAPS);  --  Output scalar
This is a fairly common trick that is employed in digital signal processing, since +1.0 cannot be exactly 
represented in a vector but −1.0 can. If we examine the new values for SCALE using different  TAP 
selections, we find the following:

We can see that as TAPS increases the width of SCALE increases as well, always preserving the same  
number of bits of precision.  Upon closer inspection  we see that we actually have one extra bit of  
precision in our SCALE value, which we will keep to ensure that the precision of the multiplication  
output is set by BITS and not TAPS.
Note that  we did not  need  to go through this  exercise with the test bench.  The test  bench  uses  
behavioral code to compute the expected output value, and we are simply dividing the sum of the  
values in the shift register by the number of taps. We want to preserve this behavioral computation to  
make sure that our RTL method of multiplying the sum by −1.0 / −TAPS is equivalent to dividing the  
sum by TAPS.
The last modification we need to make to our RTL is to change the size of AVG to be twice the size of  
WIDTH, since we are now multiplying two vectors of size WIDTH together, and to select the correct  
bits from the output of our multiplier that will be used to drive DOUT. In this case, we will use the  
401

--- Page 421 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
resize and shift_right functions from the n umeric_std package to select these bits. We know that there  
are WIDTH−1 fractional bits in SCALE, so we first shift AVG to the right by those fractional bits. Then  
we resize the result to a width of BITS to discard the duplicated sign bits:
  DOUT <= std_logic_vector (AVG (26 downto 15));
  DOUT <= std_logic_vector (resize (shift_right (AVG, WIDTH-1), BITS));
The code for the complete generic boxcar filter is:
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  GENERIC BOXCAR FILTER
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
--  This entity implements a generic boxcar (also known as a moving average) filter.
--  TAPS specifies the number of taps in the filter and BITS specifies the data path
--  width and accuracy of the output, which is +/-1 LSB. Data is shifted through
--  the filter when CLKENA = '1'. VALID indicates when the DOUT value is valid.
library  IEEE;
use      IEEE.std_logic_1164.all;
use      IEEE.numeric_std.all;
use      IEEE.math_real.all;
----------------
entity BOXCAR is
----------------
  generic ( BITS   : natural := 12;                            --  Data path width
            TAPS   : natural := 10 );                          --  Filter length
  port    ( RESET  : in  std_logic;                            --  Active high reset
            CLOCK  : in  std_logic;                            --  Master clock
            CLKENA : in  std_logic;                            --  Data clock enable
            DIN    : in  std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0);   --  Data sample input
            VALID  : out std_logic;                            --  Data output valid
            DOUT   : out std_logic_vector (BITS-1 downto 0));  --  Data output
end entity BOXCAR;
-----------------------------
architecture RTL of BOXCAR is
-----------------------------
  constant  WIDTH : natural := BITS + natural (ceil (log (real (TAPS), 2.0)));
  type      SHIFT_ARRAY is array (natural range <>) of signed (BITS-1 downto 0);
  signal    SREG  : SHIFT_ARRAY (1 to TAPS);      --  Data sample shift register
  signal    SUM   : signed (  WIDTH-1 downto 0);  --  Data sample accumulator
  signal    DIFF  : signed (  WIDTH-1 downto 0);  --  Data sample difference
  signal    AVG   : signed (2*WIDTH-1 downto 0);  --  Average data sample value
  signal    COUNT : natural range 0 to TAPS;      --  Control counter
--  The 1/N term is computed as (-1) / (-N) for maximum accuracy
  constant  DIV   : signed (WIDTH-1 downto 0) := '1' & To_Signed (0, WIDTH-1);
  constant  SCALE : signed := DIV / (-TAPS);  --  Output scalar
begin
402

--- Page 422 ---
CHAPTER 21 DESIGN REUSE
--  Data sample shift register (shift the data from left to right)
  ------------------------------------
  SREG_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SREG <= signed (DIN) & SREG (1 to TAPS-1);
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Take the sum of the values in SREG by accumulating the difference
--    between the data shifting in and the data shifting out
--  Accumulate DIN until the shift register fills with valid data
  ------------------------------------------
  SUM_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  ------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      SUM  <= (others => '0');
      DIFF <= (others => '0');
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        SUM <= SUM + DIFF;
        if (COUNT = 0)
          then DIFF <= resize (signed (DIN), WIDTH) - resize (SREG (TAPS), WIDTH);
          else DIFF <= resize (signed (DIN), WIDTH);
        end if;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Take the average of the overall sum
  -----------------------------------
  AVG_PROCESS:  process (CLOCK) begin
  -----------------------------------
    if (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1') then
        AVG <= SUM * SCALE;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
--  Select the integer result
  DOUT <= std_logic_vector (resize (shift_right (AVG, WIDTH-1), BITS));
403

--- Page 423 ---
PRACTICAL DIGITAL DESIGN: AN INTRODUCTION TO VHDL
--  VALID delay counter
--  Reclock CLKENA for better timing downstream
  --------------------------------------------
  VALID_PROCESS:  process (RESET, CLOCK) begin
  --------------------------------------------
    if (RESET = '1') then
      COUNT <= TAPS;
      VALID <= '0';
    elsif (CLOCK'event and CLOCK = '1') then
      if (CLKENA = '1' and COUNT > 0) then
        COUNT <= COUNT – 1;
      end if;
      if (COUNT < TAPS-1) then
        VALID <= CLKENA;
      end if;
    end if;
  end process;
end architecture RTL;
This component was simulated over a large range of BITS and TAPS settings and always passed with  
an output that was no more than ±1 count from the expected value. This indicates that we have created 
a generic boxcar filter that can be used with any setting.
Summary
The goal of performing digital design with VHDL is to increase the efficiency at which the designs are  
completed.  One  way  in  which  VHDL increases  design  efficiency  is  by  creating  reusable  design  
entities. As stated previously, the philosophy of design reuse is “write once, use many.” The most  
common  form  of  creating  reusable  components  is  through  generics,  but  as  we  have  seen,  
comprehensive test benches and data handshaking are additional methods that are employed.
The example we presented in this chapter shows that in general, converting a logic component to a  
reusable  one using  generics  is  fairly  straightforward.  However,  in  the  course  of  performing  this  
conversion, problems can and often do arise. The designer needs to follow a rigorous process of  
simulating and resimulating the reusable component with a variety of generic settings, whether they  
make sense in the real world or not. Any errors that occur during simulation  must be investigated,  
oftentimes resulting in a modification to the original design to correct for these errors. Only after a  
battery of testing can be completed error-free can the designer feel confident in sharing this reusable  
design with others.
404

--- Page 424 ---
CHAPTER 21 DESIGN REUSE
Review Questions
1. What are the two ways that VHDL increases design efficiency?
2. What is the most common method used to create a reusable design entity?
3. Why is having a test bench for a reusable design entity important?
4. How do data handshake signals help in creating reusable design entities?
5. What are the advantages of using a bidirectional data handshaking scheme such as AXI4-Stream?
6. Modify the generic boxcar filter example presented in this chapter to use the AXI4-Stream 
interface instead of a single CLKENA input. Then modify the test bench to generate randomized 
handshake streams on the TVALID and TREADY inputs. Note that the input data driver and output 
monitor processes will need to be modified to reflect this change, in which the input and output 
data is valid only when the corresponding TVALID and TREADY signals equal '1'.

APPENDIX A
CODING STYLE GUIDELINES
Design Practices
 All storage elements shall be edge-triggered (level-sensitive latches shall not be used).
 Processes shall not mix rising and falling edge-triggered events.
 Processes shall not have both asynchronous reset and asynchronous set inputs.
 All clock domain crossings shall be done with three-bit synchronizers or dual-clock FIFOs.
Code Organization
 Each file shall consist of a single entity/architecture pair.
 Files shall be named [entity].vhd for the RTL model and [entity].tb or [entity]_tb.vhd for the test 
bench, where [entity] is the name of the entity identifier.  Package files associated with an entity 
shall be named [entity]_package.vhd or [entity]_pkg.vhd.
 The architecture identifier for synthesizable code shall be named RTL.
 Effort shall be made to write reusable code through the use of generics and constants.
 Each entity, or tightly grouped set of entities, shall have a self-contained, self-checking test bench.  
Self-checking shall be done with assert statements and/or automatic comparison of the entity 
outputs to a “golden” expected response file.
 Test benches for low-level entities shall comprehensively test the behavior of the entity.  Code 
coverage tools, if available, shall be used to verify the integrity of the test.
 Test benches for high-level entities shall test the proper interconnection and functional interaction 
of the instantiated blocks and shall only comprehensively test the logic defined at that level.
Coding Practices
 Only signals of the following types shall be used within RTL architectures:  std_logic, 
std_logic_vector, signed, unsigned, integer, and natural.
 All integer signal types and subtypes shall be constrained to a range of allowable values.
 Variables shall not be used inside processes.
 Only signals that directly affect process outputs shall be listed in process sensitivity lists.
 Processes shall only contain closely associated logic (no large process statements).
407

--- Page 427 ---
APPENDIX A  CODING STYLE GUIDELINES
 Processes shall not be used to create concurrent logic statements.  Processes shall only be used to 
model flip-flops or memories and the logic that describes their input.
 All process statements shall contain a statement label.
 Statement labels for component instantiation and generate statements shall be kept short.
 Port maps for component instantiation shall always use the named convention (no positional 
mapping).
Commenting Guidelines
 Each file shall have a summary comment, in paragraph form, at the beginning of the file that 
describes the overall behavior of the entity.
 Each signal and port definition shall have a comment describing its behavior.
 Each process shall have a comment directly preceding it that describes the behavior of the process.
 Each group of concurrent signal assignments shall have a comment directly preceding it that 
describes the behavior of the signals.
 “Comment Bars” (i.e., lines of dash characters) shall be used to highlight entity, architecture, 
process, generate, and component instantiation statements.  Bars shall be both above and below 
the statement and have equal length.
Coding Style
 Line indentation should be done with spaces, not tabs.
 Line indentation should be 2 characters per level of indentation.
 The maximum line length should be 100 characters.
 Reserved words in VHDL should use lowercase letters only.
 User identifiers should use upper case letters only.
 Mixed-case identifiers should only be used for type definitions and functions.
 The functionality of the code within an architecture should flow from the top of the architecture to 
the bottom; that is, the flow should be input port processing, followed by intermediate processing 
and ending with output port processing.
 Closely associated signal definitions should be grouped together in the file.
 Closely associated concurrent signal assignments should be grouped together in the file.
 Syntactic elements in closely related sections of code should line up vertically.
 Any modifications to an existing file shall be done in the same style as the current coding style.
408

--- Page 428 ---
APPENDIX B
FUNCTIONAL DESCRIPTION EXAMPLE
This appendix contains an example of a portion of a typical functional description document. While the 
information presented in this example is complete, it normally would be a part of a much larger  
document that fully describes the entire design, including a description of the overall design hierarchy,  
a description of each of the function blocks contained within the design, the device pin assignments,  
descriptions of the interfaces to external components, and the document and design revision history.
As  you  read  through  this  example,  you  should  note  that  it  provides  two  important  pieces  of  
information. First, it provides system-level information about how the function behaves, such as the  
interface signals, the definition of the serial data bits, and which edge of the clock is the active edge.  
This information can be reviewed by system engineers, marketing, digital design peers, and so forth to  
make sure that the design will perform as expected in the system. Second, it documents the internal  
functional structure of the design, in this case using a block diagram and a state transition diagram.  
This portion of the document provides the details of the functional decomposition of the design,  
provides a means for digital design peers to review the design, and documents the structure of the  
design for future design reuse.
One other thing to note is how the information in this example is organized. The author has found that  
functional description documents are best organized using a top-down approach, where the document  
flows from the top level of the design, through the design hierarchy, and ending with a description of  
each  of  the  low-level  functions.  For  example,  the  table  of  contents  for  a  complete  functional  
description document that describes a data radio design might be:
409

Note how the document flows from the top-level components down through the hierarchy of the  
design. Each of the individual function descriptions should flow in a similar fashion, providing a top-
level system overview before delving into the actual nuts and bolts of how the function block is  
realized in logic. Each section within the functional description should include the text description  
necessary to document the target function along with items such as timing diagrams, block diagrams,  
state transition diagrams, truth tables, and so forth.
The following is typical of a functional description, which could be for section 2.2.2 SPI INTERFACE 
in the document map shown above.

APPENDIX B  FUNCTIONAL DESCRIPTION EXAMPLE
SPI Interface
The FPGA shall act as a slave device for an external SPI interface that is controlled by a  
microprocessor. This interface shall accept read and write serial command data streams from  
the  external  processor  and  perform  the  appropriate  actions.  The  external  SPI  interface  
signals shall be as shown in the following diagram:
The signal definition for this interface shall be:
SPI_SCLK
SPI_SSN
SPI_MOSI
SPI_MISO
Serial clock. All signals shall be stable on the rising edge of this clock.
Slave select. Commands shall only occur when this signal is at a logic '0'.
Data from the master (uP) to the slave (FPGA).
Data from the slave (FPGA) to the master (uP).
The relationship between SPI_SCLK and the SPI_SSN, SPI_MOSI, and SPI_MISO interface 
signals shall be as shown in the following timing diagram, where the state on the three  
interface lines are allowed to change on the falling edge of the clock and shall be stable on  
the rising edge of the clock. Note that in order to detect the beginning of a data transfer, the  
SPI_SSN line shall be high for at least one clock cycle before changing to the low, or active,  
state:
Each data transfer shall be a 24-bit word formatted as follows:

--- Page 431 ---
APPENDIX B  FUNCTIONAL DESCRIPTION EXAMPLE
The first bit shall be a write enable bit, which will command the FPGA to write the 16 bits of  
data in bits 15:0 of the data transfer to the address specified by bits 22:16. Write commands  
shall occur when bit 23 is a logic '1'. During a write command, the data on the SPI_MOSI 
input shall be echoed on the  SPI_MISO output to provide data loop-back capability. Read  
commands shall occur when bit 23 is a logic '0'. During a read command, the first eight bits  
of the command shall be echoed on the SPI_MISO output, followed by the 16 -bit data value 
contained in the address specified by bits 22:16.
Data transfers shall be detected by a falling edge on  SPI_SSN. Provisions shall be made  
within the logic to ignore a transfer if the SPI_SSN input is a logic '0' at power-up (no falling 
edge was detected). Provisions shall also be made such that if the SPI_SSN line returns to its 
logic '1', or idle state, before the entire 24 -bit command is received, the command shall be  
ignored.
The internal logic of this function shall be according to the following notional block diagram:

APPENDIX B  FUNCTIONAL DESCRIPTION EXAMPLE
The  state  machine  in  the  block  diagram  shall  implement  the  following  state  transition  
diagram:
The INIT and IDLE states guarantee that the circuit shall only operate when a falling edge on 
SPI_SSN is detected. The ADDR and DATA states shall be used to collect the address and 
data values of the transfer. The DATA state shall also be used to shift the selected register 
data out on SPI_MISO when a read command is received. A data write shall only occur 
during the DONE state when a write command is received and the SPI_SSN input is a logic 
'1' (indicating the proper end of a data transfer).
